PREFACE 

In preparing this book the first aim of the writer was to do away with separate outlines, and to substitute for them the more convenient book form. 

The order of lessons that has been followed is the order that has been found to be best suited to the needs of the Training School of this hospital. 

The more common treatments of eye, ear, nose, and throat are outlined, which may prove helpful to those who have had experience in these lines, and to those who at any time may meet such conditions in general nursing practice. For the use of this material indebtedness is acknowledged to Dr. Harris P. Mosher, chief of service in the Laryngological Department of the 

Massachusetts Eye and Ear Infirmary, and to Miss Helen 

O. Potter, Assistant Superintendent of Nurses of its

Training School, and her assistants; and also for opportunity for observation in the wards and operating room. 

For helpful suggestions, valuable aid and kind coöperation, my appreciation is here expressed to Dr. George A. 

Leland, Jr., who has read the proof of many chapters; to the house officers, head nurses, my assistants, and Mr. 

Joseph Godsoe, the hospital pharmacist. My thanks are due also to the student nurses who have made contributions by camera and pencil. 

A. McC.

PART I Preliminary Procedures

DAILY ROUTINE OF HOSPITAL HOUSEKEEPING SWEEPING AND DUSTING Purpose 

To promote the welfare of the hospital household through sanitation and order. 

Dust 

Outside dust is composed of dry particles of organic waste from human and animal bodies, and of clay, sand, and soot. It is carried into the house on the feet and clothing. It is stirred up by street traffic and blown. through open doors, windows, and crevices. 

Inside dust is composed of particles of textile materials, such as sheets, blankets, and dressings. Added to that are bacteria from dry discharges of wounds, which find their way through the dressings; worn out epitheleal cells; and other effete matter from the human body. 

Sweeping a polished floor

Equipment long-handled floor brush long-handled broom short-handled dust brush dustpan pail with damp sawdust 3

Disposal of dustpan and brush

The dustpan should be placed flat on the floor at one side, where it will not be stepped on. Never place it on tables or chairs.

The short-handled dust brush should be placed on its back on dustpan at all times, never on the bristle side. 

Method 

A handful of sawdust is sprinkled under each bed. 

This is where the greatest collection of dust is found. 

In sweeping, use short strokes without elevating the brush, in order to prevent scattering. In sweeping under a bed, draw the brush toward you. On the open floor, push forward with the grain of the boards. 

Take up sweepings in dustpan at each corner of open ward, and outside of each room door in a closed ward, so that the dust may not be carried through the corridors. 

Scraps of gauze, toilet paper, and hair combings should not be swept about, but should be gathered up at once and put in the proper receptacle.

Adherent pads of dust should be removed from feet of chairs, tables, and screens.

Furniture should be replaced in order as you go along. 

Note 

The above describes the routine proceedings in the morning care of the ward. The floors are gone over again in the middle of the day with a dry twine mop, to pick up particles of fluff. 

After care of utensils

The brush should be cleaned off into dust pail with hand brush and adherent fluff picked off with the fingers. 

Suspend broom in rack with handle down.

HOSPITAL HOUSEKEEPING 

5 The dustpan is swept off into dust pail and hung up by handle. It should be washed in hot suds once a week. 

The hand brush is picked clean of dust and lint and suspended by its handle.

Notes 

Straight pins and safety pins should be picked up, sterilized, left to dry in a towel and used again. 

No attempt should be made to sweep up water, as it ruins the brush and smears the polished floor. Water should be wiped carefully from the floor by using dry picked cotton, before the sweeping is begun. 

Use turpentine or a little floor polish to remove stains. 

Damp dusting

This method of dusting is employed in the care of hospital furnishings which are not upholstered, and for the removal of dust from all surfaces above the floor. 

Equipment 

Agate pail half-full of warm, soapy water.

Piece of rubber sheeting or newspaper to place under dust pail. 

Cheese cloth duster a yard square, with a tape loop sewn on the corner. 

Cake of fine sand soap or a shaker of soap powder.

Method 

A ward should be dusted as soon as dust has settled, following a morning sweeping.

The duster should be wrung from the soapy water as dry as possible, rinsed in it frequently during the process, and the water changed when cloudy.

In the routine of dusting, start with the surfaces at the highest level and work down. 

In dusting of bedsteads the card rack and chart holder should be removed and not pushed back and forth on the rung of bed.

In dusting the bedside table remove all articles if thorough methods are to be carried out. 

The molding and base board should be dusted with care, to avoid leaving a soiled streak on the painted wall. 

A little soap powder or fine sand soap should be used about the edges and knobs of doors to remove finger marks from painted and enameled surfaces. 

Included in the dusting are the window sashes and sills. 

After care of duster

The duster should be washed in suds and hung to dry. 

Note 

The high dusting is done weekly with a long-handled hair brush kept for all surfaces which cannot ordinarily be reached by the hand. 

Care of special furniture

In a hospital ward whenever possible the furnishings are of materials which may be washed and disinfected. 

Furniture polish or turpentine should be used on varnished surfaces weekly, or when necessary, after they have been cleaned with the duster wrung out of tepid water.

Electric fixtures should be dusted with a dry duster.

HOSPITAL HOUSEKEEPING 

7 

DAILY CARE OF ICE TANK FOR DRINKING WATER 

This is attended to early every morning, by emptying out the water and washing the tank with slightly soapy water. 

Use a little soap powder once a week to remove any deposit which may have formed on the lining. Follow with a thorough rinsing of scalding hot water and dry thoroughly. Allow to cool before putting in ice.

Wash the outside of the ice tank and cover. 

Wash and scald the drip pan daily. 

The ice tank should be filled with ice and then with fresh water and replenished as often as necessary during the day.

DAILY CARE OF THE REFRIGERATOR 

In order to be ready for the reception of ice and fresh food, this also is attended to early in the morning. 

The drip pan is emptied and scrubbed with a brush and hot suds to prevent the formation of a slimy deposit. 

In one's own home a less frequent cleaning might prove sufficient, but in a hospital, where refrigerators are in close contact with the ward dust, it is essential that this should be attended to daily. 

It is a duty which should not be left in the hands of an ignorant maid, as the proper care of food and refrigerator plays an important part in household sanitation. 

Cleaning the refrigerator 

A special cloth is provided for this purpose. It is best made of coarse huckabuck, 12" by 16", with a tape loop to suspend it at the side of the refrigerator.

All utensils and food should be removed and the shelves taken out and washed in a pan of hot suds, rinsed, dried thoroughly and aired until ready to be returned to their places.

The food chamber is washed, top, sides, and bottom, and dried. A little fine sand soap may be used occasionally on the bottom, if there are any stains that ordinary cleansing will not remove.

The left-over ice is removed, washed, and used for drinking water in the ice tank.

The corrugated zinc shelf, on which the ice rests, is removed and washed in suds, rinsed, dried, and placed at one side, preferably at an open window, to air. 

The top, sides, and bottom of the ice chamber are now washed.

Leading from the ice chamber is the drip pipe, which carries off the water from the melting ice. This is scalded with a pitcher of hot water and cleaned with a long wirehandled bristle brush, which reaches down to the trap. 

The floor of the ice chamber may be dashed with cold water, in order to cool it before the ice is replenished. 

The ice chamber is dried, being especially careful of the corners where slime is apt to collect. A wooden skewer is used for this purpose. 

The inside of the refrigerator doors and surrounding wooden framework are washed. The wooden skewer is used in cleaning the crevices.

The shelves are put back in the food chambers. 

The varnished outside of the refrigerator is cleaned with tepid water and dried. Soap and water may be used about the nickel door handles,

HOSPITAL HOUSEKEEPING 

9 

Finally, the cup-like appendage hanging below the drip pipe under the refrigerator is washed and dried, as it is likely to become slimy if neglected. 

Care of the food

Left-over food, such as broths, milk, and custards of the previous day, if in good condition, should be used before touching the day's fresh supply. 

The left-over butter should be removed from its jar and put in a bowl, the jar washed and made ready for the fresh daily supply.

The outside of each utensil should be wiped before 

Notes 

Fruit should not be kept in the refrigerator, as it flavors the milk or butter.

Warm food should never be placed in a refrigerator, as the cold air comes in contact with the food and is warmed, thus raising the temperature of the refrigerator. 

Refrigerator doors should be kept closed at all times to prevent waste of ice and cold air, upon which the preservation of food depends. 

The butter jar should be kept covered. 

Do not allow food of any kind to accumulate. 

After care of equipment The refrigerator towel is washed in hot suds, rinsed in cool water, and hung up to dry. The dishpan is washed, dried, and put away, and the floor about the refrigerator mopped up, if necessary.

II A HOSPITAL BED AND BEDMAKING Construction 

Bedstead 

Usually enameled iron.

Springs 

Linked or woven wire, fine or coarse.

Surgical springs, i.e., flat steel slats interlaced. 

The bedstead may be mounted on heavy casters in order that it may be moved out of doors, or on flanges of glass, iron or wood to keep the bed steady. 

Dimensions Ileight 

26 inches to 30 inches to top of mattress.

Width 

30 inches to 36 inches.

Length 

Usually 6 feet, but on a male ward 6 feet is more desirable.

Furnishings and equipment 

2 mattresses, one husk and one hair 

2 pillows, with cases 3 cotton sheets, upper, lower and draw 

I rubber draw sheet

ΙΟ

A HOSPITAL BED II 

2 or more woolen blankets, according to need of the patient I spread 

I patient's towel 

I wash cloth Mattress

If a woven or linked wire spring is used, one hair mattress of ordinary weight, usually thirty pounds, is sufficient.

If a spring of interlaced steel slats, two mattresses are necessary (one husk and one hair) to make up for the lack of buoyancy.

Pillows 

May be large or small, of feathers, hair or down.

Usual size for large pillow is, length, 31 inches; width, 17 inches.

Before starting, have everything needed at bedside; one towel, one sheet, and one pillow case used daily for each bed, laundry bag, and whisk broom or folded towel for brushing the bed.

Preliminary details before starting bed making Use top sheet of previous day for fresh draw sheet, unless it is soiled. A fresh under sheet is put on once a week unless required oftener; a fresh spread once in four days if needed.

Use careful judgment about changing linen and err on the economical side.

MAKING AN EMPTY BED 

Stripping bed 

Making first side

Making second side

Pillows 

General adjustment of bedside or room 

Stripping bed

Pillows 

Turn bedside chair to face head of bed. Note cases and remove the one most soiled, by firmly grasping end of pillow with left hand and stripping case from pillow with right. Place pillows on arms of chair, the soiled case in laundry bag.

Bedclothing 

Loosen bedclothing by slightly lifting edge of mattress with one hand and slip out clothing with other. 

Fold spread and place on head of bed. 

Remove each blanket separately and drape around chair in shawl-like fashion, clear from floor; likewise the sheets, if not soiled.

If soiled, the draw sheet is gathered up, keeping the dust inside, and placed in bag. 

Remove rubber draw sheet and under sheet and place on chair. Permit bed to air. Mattresses

Brush off mattresses around buttons and bindings, sweeping from center toward the edge.

Turn hair mattress from head to foot to shift the wear and position, the greatest pressure being nearer the head of the bed. If mattress becomes flattened on one side more than the other it is permissible to turn it from side to side occasionally.

A HOSPITAL BED 

13 See that the mattresses are even before starting to make the bed.

Note: The first day the patient sits up out of bed after an illness, it is customary to turn both mattresses. 

Making first side of bed 

Application of under sheet

Place sheet on bed with folded crease in middle. 

Carry over at top and extend 6 inches under both mattresses.

Square corner and tuck sheet along side under both mattresses.

At foot pull tightly and tuck under one mattress. 

Square corner and complete tucking in at side. 

Application of rubber draw sheet 

See that rubber sheet is whole.

Place it on the bed so that it extends well under the pillow. Allow 2 inches for extra tension on the opposite side when that shall be drawn tight. 

Tuck snugly under both mattresses. 

Folding and application of cotton draw sheet Fold ordinary-sized sheet end to end, sliding one hem 8 inches below the other in order to cover rubber sheet fully when in position.

Holding the long side of draw sheet towards you, fold evenly in fourths in fan-like fashion, with selvage edges toward the center, ready to be placed in position on bed Place on side of bed with selvage edges towards middle of bed and 2 inches above top of rubber sheet. 

Turn down the upper fold and tuck securely under mattresses.

HOSPITAL BED SHOWING FOLDED DRAW SHEET Application of upper sheet 

Place evenly on bed, hem wrong side up, the edge 6 inches from head of mattress after turning a 12-inch fold under itself, temporarily out of the way. 

Tuck bottom of sheet under both mattresses half way across the foot of bed. 

Square corner and tuck in at side less than half way up towards head.

Application of blankets 

Apply even with fold of upper sheet, i.e., 6 inches from head of mattress, which gives the patient sufficient covering for his shoulders, when lying on his side.

PRELIMINARY COURSE 

15 If blankets are short, the last blanket should be put on the bed about 8 inches or 10 inches lower than the others, in order to cover patient's feet and to hold bedclothing in position. The blankets are tucked under both mattresses at the same time, half way across foot of bed and finished with a flat square corner at side. 

Making second side of bed

Preparation 

Step to opposite side of bed. 

Pick up hanging rubber sheet at side of bed and bed clothes with it. Fold back neatly, exposing half of bed and under sheet.

Arrangement of under sheet Lift up both mattresses at head and tuck in remainder of sheet.

At foot of bed, grasp sheet with both hands, pull firmly and tuck under top mattress. 

Step to side of bed, grasp sheet firmly in the center, with palms of hand downward, right knee braced against bed to steady it, and pull with an upward swing of the hands. This serves as a check and prevents the sheet from being pulled out on the opposite side. 

Tuck under both mattresses while the sheet is at extreme tension. 

Square corners, head and foot.

Adjustment of rubber sheet 

Grasp edge of rubber sheet in middle. Pull tightly and carry to full extent under both mattresses. The same

procedure is carried out the whole length of the sheet, which helps to bind it firmly around the mattresses. 

Adjustment of cotton draw sheet The same measures are employed in this case as with the rubber sheet, being careful that the under side is perfectly smooth before tucking in.

Adjustment of upper sheet Adjust and tuck under both mattresses at foot. 

Square corners and tuck a little less than half way up the side.

Blankets 

Tuck in at foot of bed.

Square corners and carry under both mattresses at side, as far as sheet. 

Application of spread

Place on bed with the prominence of the ironed crease in the middle. 

Tuck under both mattresses at foot. 

Turn corners in an angle, leaving sides of spread hanging in a slant.

Pull edge of spread tight at head of bed and fold over edge of blankets. Bring the hem of upper sheet out over spread, i.e., the 12-inch fold.

Pillows 

Adjustment of first pillow. (Case not changed.)

Grasp the long-seam side and settle the pillow towards the opposite side of case.

Place pillow on table and smooth firmly from end to end.

A HOSPITAL BED 

17 

Reverse pillow and repeat process.

Grasp seam side of case with both hands and place carefully on bed with open end parallel with card rack on left side of bed, and finish by smoothing from center to the sides.

Adjustment of second pillow. (Case changed.) 

A fresh case is put on by holding pillow between left elbow and side while both hands are employed in opening the clean case, in which the end of pillow is inserted and the pillow shaken down into the corners.

As the case is usually larger than the pillow, grasp the long-seam side of case and settle pillow toward the seamless side, when it is treated in the same manner as the first pillow and put in position over first pillow, with open end toward the chart or right side of bed. 

Note: It is not usually necessary to change more than one pillow case daily and that only if soiled or damp with perspiration. 

Attention to general details 

The patient's towel

The towel hangs with folded edge against side of bedstead, with wash cloth, on rung even with upper pillow. 

See that card rack and chart are in position.

Bedside table should be parallel with head of bed, drawer forward.

Inspect drawer for concealed food, drugs, etc., as it is solely for small and necessary effects of patient; i.e., tooth brush, comb, and other toilet articles.

Replace table cover evenly.

If there are flowers, change water and rearrange.

Remove old newspapers. 

Place chair beside foot of bed. 

Scrutinize your work and do not leave until everything is in order.

Notes Time necessary 

If uninterrupted, one should not need more than five to eight minutes to make an empty bed.

Repairs 

Hollowed hair mattresses and perforated rubber sheets should be exchanged. Mattresses should have tufts replaced at once, otherwise they soon become lumpy. Rips in pillow tickings call for immediate repair.

Stains 

Blood-stained pillows and mattresses are inexcusable in a well-equipped hospital ward where rubber pillow cases are provided and rubber sheets kept in good repair. 

The same may be said of unsightly stains on bed linen due to the use of silver solutions, gummy tinctures, ointments, and disinfectants. 

Rules regarding patients

Patients should not be permitted to keep any of their belongings under the pillows or mattresses. 

If a convalescent patient wishes to rest on his bed during the day the spread may be protected by a gray blanket. As soon as the patient arises, the blanket should be removed and the bed smoothed and pillows rearranged. 

In most cases it is best to have the patient undress and get into bed for the rest period.

PRELIMINARY COURSE 

19 

Method of handling bed linen 

In transportation of clean linen from one bedside to another it should not come in contact with any bedding other than that of the bed for which it is intended. 

The soiled linen, on removal from bed, should be put into laundry bag at once. 

Enough cannot be said against throwing soiled bed linen on the floor during bed making. 

It means danger of scattering bed dust and germs. 

It means handling the linen twice. 

It denotes slovenly and disorderly methods. 

Red and white bags are used for soiled laundry from all special precaution cases. A precaution tag is attached, on which are written the ward number and the nature of the disease. These bags are also used for linen which may be stained from discharging wounds, or blood, and from beds of incontinent patients.

III MAKING AN OCCUPIED BED Equipment 

2 sheets 

I pillow case

I towel 

I spread (if needed) 

I nightgown

Procedure laundry bag bottle bathing solution talcum powder whisk broom the last three carried in an agate basin Remove table cover. Arrange linen on table in order wanted, basin on shelf under table. Place laundry bag under bed. 

Change only such sheets as necessary.

As a general rule the nurse begins on the patient's right. 

Making upper part 

Loosen upper clothing. 

Remove spread and place on bed-shelf.

Remove all pillows but one and place on chair. At this time remove soiled case if necessary.

Remove all blankets but one and drape over back of chair.

Turn remaining blanket and sheet back over the feet even with foot of bed and fold back also at the patient's waist to facilitate removal. 20

MAKING AN OCCUPIED BED 

27 Place clean sheet over blanket; turn hem under in usual way.

Take blanket from chair and place over clean sheet, both turned even with foot of bed. 

Removal of soiled upper bedclothing If the patient's strength permits, he may hold upper edges of clean blanket and sheet, otherwise tuck over shoulders before removing soiled linen. 

Stand at foot of bed with left side toward foot. Raise clean sheet and blanket with left hand. With right hand remove soiled sheet and blanket with a quick motion, catching them over right arm to prevent dragging on the floor. Spread blanket over the chair, and if sheet is unsoiled, fold into draw sheet; otherwise put in laundry bag. Turn patient on left side by putting both hands under the sacrum and, drawing patient toward you, slowly turn on side. Position of patient should be hips drawn back and upper shoulder and knee forward for balance. Fold back upper bedclothing over patient, lengthwise, preparatory to 

Care of the back

Patient's own towel is used in each case. Unbutton nightgown. Draw towel through left hand by a corner, folding it back and forth. Holding bottle of bathing solution in right hand, remove cork with little finger of left. Wet folded thicknesses of towel with the solution. 

Replace cork, and put bottle back in basin. Envelop right hand with towel, bringing wet part over fingers, make a couple of turns around back of hand and wrist. Bathe

back with circular motion over prominent parts as shoulders, sacrum, and coccyx and also under edges of swathe, if one is worn. Elbows and heels may be included. Apply powder with both hands. If nightgown is to be changed it should remain unbuttoned. 

Making under part

Loosen each sheet separately, draw sheet first. Gather in small folds, beginning at center of the bed, and press down as close to patient as possible; wedge partially under patient, with fingers held rigidly. Sweep off rubber sheet. Unfasten, turn down a corner at head and fold over patient. Unfasten under sheet and pass it under patient from head to foot. Sweep off mattress.

Place clean sheet on bed, doubled full length, right side up, selvage edge toward patient. Gather upper fold in both hands, starting with selvage edge (at the same time turning under fold out over edge of bed), and press under patient as far as possible; what is left is gathered in small folds close to the patient until the middle of sheet is reached, to be pulled through later when patient is turned over to opposite side of bed. 

The remaining half of sheet is tucked in securely under both mattresses at the head, and square corner made. At the foot, the sheet is grasped firmly in both hands, pulled tight and tucked under the upper mattress only. Square corner is made and remainder of sheet tucked under both mattresses at side.

Rubber sheet is now adjusted and its overhanging edge grasped in the middle with both hands, pulled tight and tucked in, then the top and bottom edges.

MAKING AN OCCUPIED BED 

23 The folded draw sheet is placed in position over the rubber sheet, the upper selvage fold is brought over the edge of mattress and tucked in, the under selvage edge is pressed under patient as far as possible and what is left is gathered in small folds until the middle of draw. sheet is reached, to be pulled through when patient is turned.

When the under sheets are not changed they are brushed free of crumbs and bed dust.

Support patient's head with one hand and with other draw the pillow to completed side of bed. Assist patient to move to completed side and adjust pillow comfortably under head.

Stepping to opposite side of bed, fold back upper bedclothes lengthwise over patient, avoiding exposure. Remove soiled draw sheet, fold in so that no bed dust escapes, and put in bag. Pull through fresh cotton draw sheet and fold over patient in same way as rubber sheet previously. 

Sweep off rubber sheet. Loosen and fold over patient.

Gather up soiled under sheet and put in bag. Sweep mattress. Pull through and adjust clean under sheet. 

Tuck in head and foot; pull and tighten middle. Tuck in and make corners. Tighten rubber sheet by grasping firmly and lifting upward while pulling, and tuck in. 

Adjust draw sheet and tuck in. 

Support head and pull pillow to middle bed. Turn patient on back. At this time, if patient has slipped down in bed, lift higher on pillow. Patient may help by flexing knees and pressing soles of feet on bed and grasping rung of bed with both hands,

Changing nightgown 

Turn down bedclothes to waist. Pull nightgown out from each side of body carefully. The change from soiled to clean gown is made one arm at a time. Place hand in armhole of sleeve and, grasping patient's elbow, with other hand remove sleeve. Fut down arm gently. 

Clean nightgown is placed over soiled one. Introducing hand into sleeve, grasp patient's hand and draw his arm into sleeve. See that gown fits well over shoulders.

Button at neck; with very sick patient it is not essential to button all the way down. Slip out soiled gown and pull down clean gown.

If necessary, wide wrists of gown may be secured with safety pins thrust through horizontally above band, making a frill. Adjust shoulder seams to prevent binding arms.

Arrangement of pillows 

The pillow under patient's head is now removed. Collapse pillows from each end to bring feathers to center. 

Shake feathers down into case, holding pillow by side seam. Grasp in center of side and place under patient for support of shoulders; put clean case on second pillow, shake as first pillow, and place under head.

Final arrangement of upper bedclothing Make sure that bedclothing is well up under chin and over shoulders. Tuck upper sheet under both mattresses at the foot. In making corners a plait is taken at both sides to prevent pressure over the toes. Put on second blanket; tuck in both, making same plait while turning

MAKING AN OCCUPIED BED 

25 corners.

Sheet and blankets are tucked on both sides as far as the patient's knees, not above. 

Put on spread evenly. Arrange evenly with blankets at top, or if long enough, turn over blankets. Tuck under both mattresses at foot and carry round corner to form a diagonal fold. Top of sheet is turned out over spread. 

Center of sheet should meet center of spread. 

If the patient wishes arms outside of bed covers make an additional fold of the upper bedclothing upon itself. as far as arm spaces. Suit the patient's comfort as far as possible.

Bedside adjustment 

Put on table cover. Replace table, inspect drawer and replace chair. Take away laundry bag, basin and whisk broom. Leave bedside in order.

IV CHANGING OR TURNING MATTRESS WITH Purpose PATIENT IN BED WHEN TWO MATTRESSES ARE USED 

To replace an old mattress with a new one.

To change the position of mattress for the comfort of the patient.

Procedure 

In order to move the patient comfortably, two nurses should take part in the operation. Loosen upper and lower bedclothing, remove and fold spread, remove all blankets but one and remove all pillows but one. Have the patient in the middle of the bed. Turn the corners of the bedclothing at the head of the bed over the patient's shoulders; turn up the bedclothing at the foot of the bed over the patient's feet, and fold the lower corners diagonally along the side of the patient's body. The bedclothing which remains hanging at each side of the bed is now carried over the patient snugly from either side. 

Moving the patient to the edge of the mattress The nurse and her assistant stand on the same side of the bed. One encircles the patient's shoulders and back with her arms; the other encircles the patient's pelvis 26

CHANGING MATTRESS 

27 and thighs, and together they draw the patient towards the edge of the mattress. They now step to the opposite side of the bed and grasp the sides of the mattress where the weight of the patient's body is greatest, and draw the mattress towards them until there is sufficient room on the mattress opposite for the patient's body. 

A bedside stand or small table is placed under the overhanging edge of the upper mattress. The nurse and her assistant step back to the first side and together slide the patient on the under mattress, when the top mattress may be exchanged for a new one or be turned from top to bottom for change of position. 

Moving the patient back to the upper mattress The edge of the upper mattress is drawn as close to the patient's body as possible, with its overhanging edge on the opposite side supported on the bedside stand. 

Again one nurse encircles the patient's shoulders and back with both arms and the other nurse the patient's pelvis and thighs: together they move the patient onto the upper mattress.

The upper mattress is now drawn into position, even with the lower one. The patient, still enveloped in the bedclothing, is moved to the middle of the bed and towards the head, so that the bedclothing will be in position for proper readjustment.

WHEN ONE MATTRESS IS USED 

When a single mattress is used over a spring bed, the same procedure may be carried out. A folded bed puff, blankets, or several feather pillows placed lengthwise over the springs will serve in the place of the mattress.

V 

PREPARATION OF PATIENT FOR Purpose THE NIGHT 

To refresh and give comfort to the sick patient. 

To prepare for uninterrupted rest and sleep.

Equipment patient's towel face cloth toilet basin mouth wash solution mouth wash cup orange wood stick absorbent cotton kidney-shaped basin talcum powder bathing solution whisk broom carried in agate basin pitcher of cold water drinking tube extra blanket hot water bag if necessary Carry equipment to bedside. 

PROCEDURE FOR TOILET OF HELPLESS PATIENT 

Care of face and hands

If patient has not his own soap, prepare a slightly soapy solution of warm water in the basin. 

Place towel under patient's chin. 

Place wash cloth in basin and wring partially dry. 

Cleanse the eyes, changing part of cloth for each eye to avoid possible infection. 

Dry immediately with towel. 28

PREPARATION FOR NIGHT 

29 Rinse cloth and slip over right hand with ends tucked in, steady head with left hand and wash face, including neck and ears, with relaxed wrist and firm touch. 

Rinse cloth and wring out tightly; go over face the second time. Dry gently.

Place the basin on the bed and wash hands and arms. 

Dry off with cloth, using towel as before. If arm spaces are included, follow with powder. 

Care of the mouth 

Preparation of mouth wash

Use one part solution to three parts warm water. 

With tooth brush 

If possible, the patient's tooth brush and dental floss. should be used. Moisten the tooth brush by immersing in the warm solution before using. 

Place towel under patient's chin.

By standing slightly back of the patient's head the nurse can manipulate the tooth brush with her right hand and brush with an up and down motion.

With absorbent cotton swabs 

If patient is too ill to have tooth brush used, the mouth should be cleansed with absorbent cotton swabs. Those used for the tongue and gums are larger than those for the teeth.

To prevent the swab from slipping off into the patient's mouth the sharpened end of the orange wood stick is moistened in the solution. 

Twist a small piece of absorbent cotton securely around the point of the orange wood stick or toothpick, seeing that the point is well covered, and dip into the solution.

Before introducing into the patient's mouth the superfluous solution is pressed out of the swab against the inner side of the cup. After using never dip a swab the second time into the solution.

The upper and lower teeth, back and front, are cleansed as with a tooth brush, using at least four swabs for the operation.

From the orange stick the swabs are removed with several folds of tissue paper, or forceps, and placed in a receptacle to be burned; when toothpicks are used a fresh toothpick is used with each swab and both are burned. 

The patient should now rinse his mouth with the remainder of the solution. The nurse supports the patient's head with the pillow and places the cup to his lips. His head is then lowered and the kidney basin is placed in position to receive the solution after the mouth is rinsed. 

Dry the patient's lips and remove the towel. 

Care of false teeth 

Remove after each meal and cleanse with tooth brush and tepid water, then rinse with two per cent boric solution or bicarbonate of soda solution. When not worn by the patient keep in two per cent boric solution. 

After care of materials The materials just used are removed to the lavatory and disposed of. 

All fluids are emptied into the hopper.

In ordinary cases wash all utensils with hot suds, scald, dry and put away; otherwise, in precaution cases, sterilize. Keep toilet utensils for that special case.

PREPARATION FOR NIGHT 

31 

The face cloth is washed and the tooth brush or orange wood stick is scalded and returned to patient's bedside. 

Care of the hair

For a woman 

Place a towel under the side of the head to be combed first. Comb or brush, beginning at the end and working upwards as near as possible to the roots of the hair, then comb the scalp, gradually combing towards the end of the hair.

Braid loosely near the head and more tightly towards the end. 

Secure with ribbon, bandage or elastic band.

Wrap combings in toilet paper and place in receptacle to be burned. 

For a man

The hair should be brushed, combed and parted neatly. 

Care of the body

If the patient is sensitive to cold, the bathing solution may be warmed by loosening the cork and placing the bottle in hot water.

If the patient from necessity maintains the dorsal position, he should be bathed from each side, the body being raised as much as possible; otherwise roll him over on his side and saturate the corner of the towel with bathing solution. 

Rub the back by starting at the neck and rubbing with a firm, circular motion as far as the end of the spine. 

Follow with powder.

Heels, elbows and knees should be inspected for redness and be treated. 

While the patient is still on his side brush off the draw sheet, placing the hand against the patient as a guard against the whisk broom. 

Note: The hands should be used in place of a towel or broom for the removal of crumbs which may be felt under the patient's body. 

Care of the bed

Brush cotton draw sheet, using the hand to remove crumbs from under the patient. Then loosen both draw sheets together. Inspect cotton sheet for moisture and if necessary, change; otherwise re-tighten each draw sheet separately.

Roll patient on completed side of bed and step to opposite side. 

Brush, loosen and re-tighten draw sheets.

Move patient to middle of bed. Support his head while removing both pillows together. Shake and replace them. 

Readjust upper bedclothing.

Apply doubled extra blanket over foot of bed within patient's reach.

Note: If a hot water bag is in use, it should be refilled at the official temperature. 

Arrangement of bedside Have nothing on the table but the articles needed during the night, i.e., small pitcher of fresh water and drinking tube, hand bell if required, and table drawer within the patient's reach.

PREPARATION FOR NIGHT 

33 

Final details to be remembered

In connection with the patient If permissible, a cool sponge bath is refreshing at bedtime in warm weather in place of the local bathing. 

See that all wants are attended to before leaving the patient, so that he may feel settled for the night. It is only in extreme cases that patients are disturbed for medicine, temperature or treatment of any sort. 

Fasten a handkerchief to the patient's nightgown with a small safety pin, as otherwise it may be mislaid. 

In relation to room and ventilation Ventilate by pulling down the windows from the top and raise from the bottom as high as the window board. 

The temperature for comfort at night should not be over 68° in winter. For the protection of the patient in cold weather when full ventilation is desired (as in cases of pneumonia) and when the muslin bed curtain is not sufficient, a sheet or blanket may be used to drape the head and sides of the bed.

Unnecessary noises: Report windows with loose pulleys, as a rattling window may be a source of annoyance. Wedge, in the meantime. Squeaking door hinges may easily be oiled. Door stops should be used where there is danger of a door slamming, as sudden noises are startling.

PROCEDURE FOR TOILET OF CONVALESCENT PATIENTS 

Place at each bedside an empty basin; follow with an agate pail of tepid water slightly soapy. 

Pour sufficient in each basin to wash the face and hands.

Prepare mouth wash solution for a number of patients, a cup for each. Carry to the bedsides on a large tray and distribute. 

Place tooth brush in the solution cup and see that the patient is provided with a towel.

Bathe the backs of those who have been confined to bed all day. 

Brush out bed, tighten draw sheet, shake up pillows, supply extra blanket. 

AMBULATORY PATIENTS 

Those who are up all day wash face and hands and cleanse teeth in the lavatory. 

Turning down bed for the night

In the case of a convalescent or new patient, reach under both pillows and turn upper bedclothing down to within two feet of foot of bed. Fold down the second time and back on itself, forming a double box plait. 

Place an extra blanket over the foot.

VI 

CONTRIVANCES FOR THE SUPPORT AND COMFORT OF THE PATIENT IN BED 

In using all these appliances and substitutes the nurse must not lose sight of the importance of the care for the patient's skin in bathing, rubbing and keeping dry, for which there are no substitutes.

GENERAL CONDITIONS 

In cases of restlessness and nervous fatigue Tuck a pillow, large or small, on either side of the patient's body, on which he may rest his arms. Filled and covered hot water bags at body temperature make a good substitute. 

To prevent slipping down in bed

In ordinary cases of weakness to keep the patient from slipping down in bed, the foot of the bed may be raised four or five inches on blocks. Or a sheet may be folded on the bias, stretched over the mattress close to the patient's feet, and tied firmly to each side of the bedstead. A small pillow or tightly rolled blanket may be introduced between the folds of the sheet to serve as a brace for the patient's feet.

To keep a helpless patient on his side The patient's hips should be well drawn back and the knees carried slightly forward and somewhat flexed. 35

Small pillows or folded sheets may be placed between the knees and ankles to prevent contact of opposing surfaces. For support of the back in place of a bolster, an ordinary feather pillow may be folded lengthwise, tied together at each end with tape or bandage, and firmly wedged against the patient's neck, shoulders, and back. 

An improvised surgical cradle 

An empty soap box may be used with one side knocked out. It should be sufficiently deep and wide for the comfort of the patient. The bottom of the box may be padded to serve as a support to the feet, the other three sides forming the arch to support the bedclothing. 

SPECIAL CONDITIONS 

For support of head and neck

When the patient must keep the flat, recumbent position, use small down, air, feather or hair pillows for slight elevation.

In cervical fractures when the head must be supported on both sides, use small, padded sand bags, salt bags or a rubber ice collar filled with either salt or sand. A dumbbell-shaped hair pillow is excellent, as the narrow part fits snugly under the neck and gives support, while the two bulbous ends hold the head in position. 

For support of a painful shoulder A small down pillow may be used or the corner of an ordinary feather pillow may be smoothed and flattened to serve as a wedge between the patient's shoulder and the

CONTRIVANCES FOR SUPPORT 

37 bed. As substitutes, a smoothly folded blanket or sheet, an air ring or cushion, a hot water bag at body temperature filled sufficiently to serve as a support. 

For support of a septic hand 

A feather pillow on an inclined wooden plane.

For support of the back 

Dorsal region 

In paraplegic or emaciated cases a firm, flat, smooth, hair or feather pillow, extending under the patient from shoulder to hips on either side of the spine, may be used to prevent contact with the bed. 

Lumbar region 

Small pillows, a smoothly folded sheet or blanket slipped under the arch of the back; or an ice collar or hot water bag filled with cool or warm water, depending on which affords the greater comfort, have been the means of great relief for backache following operation. 

Sacral and coccygeal region An inflated air ring, a large or small feather pillow (protected with a rubber case under the pillow cover) placed lengthwise under the patient. 

For support of a pendulous abdomen When the patient is lying on his side a pillow, large or small, may be smoothed flat, and pressed under the edge of the abdomen, with the upper leg flexed upon the end of the pillow.

For support of the knees 

To relax abdominal muscles following operation, or for restful change of position, use a firm rubber-cased pillow or small hair bolster under the knees and against the back of the thighs. Two blankets rolled together, tied at each end and confined in a pillow case, will serve as substitute for a bolster.

For support of lower extremities Place a rubber-cased feather pillow lengthwise from the back of the knees and extending beyond the patient's heels. This simple measure will relieve the body of the dragging weight of paralyzed limbs and prevent pressure of the bed against the heels. A surgical cradle may be used to support the weight of the upper clothing over the feet. 

To prevent toe drop or foot drop

Place under both feet a feather pillow, lengthwise, and over the feet a surgical cradle which should be tied to each side of the bed. Fold a large sheet obliquely and introduce each end through the open space of the cradle on either side, the broader surface of the sheet against the soles of the patient's feet. The sheet should be held taut and the ends tied to each side of the bed. A piece of heavy cardboard or splint wood is introduced between the upright folds of the sheet to serve as a brace. It should be broad enough for both feet and extend beyond the toes, as in the foot piece of a posterior wire splint. It may be padded.

CONTRIVANCES FOR SUPPORT 

39 

TO PREVENT TOE DROP 

For support of a septic foot 

Use a rubber-cased feather pillow on an inclined wooden plane. Over this is placed a surgical cradle, and owing to the height of the inclined plane, it should be placed on end and held in position by a bandage tied to each side of the bed. As a substitute, an ordinary feather pillow may be used, its extreme end elevated by a folded sheet or blanket, or by two or three magazines, to give the desired elevation.

To keep a delirious or restless child in bed 

A sleeveless waist made of Canton flannel or unbleached cotton is buttoned down the back with flat buttons. Attached to the center of the waist line should be a belt of cotton webbing six inches deep and long enough to fasten to both sides of the bed or cot. The belt should be stitched on at the waist line across the width of the webbing, covering five inches of the waist across the front, for security allowing not more than half-inch spaces between

each row of stitching. This waist is worn over the patient's nightgown or shirt.

This form of restraint is perfectly safe, and does not interfere with the comfort of the patient, as it allows freedom of the arms and legs, as well as limited motion from side to side, and brings no direct pressure upon the body.

For protection in the restlessness of chorea two boards, as long as the bed and one and one-half feet wide, should be notched at the extreme ends and secured at these points to the bed. The inner side of the boards should be padded with folded blankets or pillows, which may be held in place by bandages. The head of the bed may be covered with a thick blanket and sheet securely held in place with safety pins, or a pillow may be tied to the rungs of the bed with a bandage at each end.

VII BED WITH CRADLE Purpose 

A bed is made with a cradle for fractures and sprains of the lower leg, and for such cases as arthritis and phlebitis, in which the weight of the bedclothing would be a source of discomfort.

In the case of a fracture of the lower leg, when not feasible to suspend the injured limb, the bed is made up with the following equipment added to the usual requisites for bedmaking.

Equipment 

2 or more fracture boards 

2 sand bags rubber-cased feather pillow surgical cradle blocks when needed for traction

Fracture boards are used with a spring bed and are about 12-15 inches wide. They are made to reach from one side of the bed to the other, under the springs, for firmness and even support. A wooden cleat an inch or more deep nailed on each end of the board rests on the framework of the bed when in place, and serves to raise the board under the springs and prevent sagging. The board is perforated to permit ventilation. Two fracture. 4I

boards are used at a time, one above and the other below the point of fracture.

Old-fashioned bed slats are sometimes used in place of regular fracture boards.

Sand bags and pillow are to hold the injured limb in position. The surgical cradle is to elevate and support the upper bedclothing.

Preparation and procedure 

This bed is made in the same order of procedure as any other bed except in the arrangement of the upper bedclothing, which is made to conform to the surgical cradle. 

The chair, table, and linen are arranged as usual. The upper sheet, draw sheet, and pillow case are to be changed. 

Making upper part

The upper bed coverings are loosened and the spread removed as usual. The top pillow is removed and placed on chair. Top blanket is removed and draped on back of chair. The remaining blanket and sheet are folded back from foot over the cradle cornerwise on the side of the injured leg. The upper bedclothing is supported with the left hand. To avoid striking the foot, the cradle is tipped forward at its lower edge, removed and placed under the bed, and the bedclothes replaced. The sand bags come next.

The nurse now places her extended hand and forearm along the side of the injured leg, grasps it firmly, elevates it in a straight line from the thigh, and with the free hand slips out the pillow. She then replaces the leg carefully and steadily on the bed. The fresh sheet and blanket are

BED WITH CRADLE 

43 put on as usual. The soiled sheet and blanket are pushed down to the waist line. If the fracture is on the right side, the fresh sheet and blanket are elevated by the left hand and the soiled sheet removed diagonally from the opposite side of the bed to prevent contact with the injured leg. The top sheet, if not soiled, is folded for a draw sheet and the blanket is draped over the arm of the chair. 

Care of the back

If the fracture is below the knee, the patient may be able to turn his body slightly to have his back bathed and powdered, which is done from the uninjured side. 

Making the under part of bed The under part is made from the sound side first. The patient's head is supported and the pillow is moved slightly toward opposite side of bed. If the injury is below the knee the patient is instructed to grasp the lower rungs at the head of the bed with both hands while the nurse with both hands supports the injured leg above and below the point of break, keeping the leg in a straight line, with slight traction, and following the patient as he moves slowly to opposite side of bed.

The procedure is as usual; the sheets to be changed are pushed as far as possible under the uninjured side, which will facilitate the final removal on opposite side, as the patient may not turn. On the completion of the first half, the patient is again moved as already described, and the second half is completed.

The final move is assisting the patient to the middle of the bed, when he may be moved up on the pillows. As he supports himself on the rungs of the bed, he may draw

his body up while the nurse supports the injured leg. The nightgown is changed and clean case put on the pillow, which is shaken and arranged to suit the patient's comfort. 

Arrangement of the fractured leg The pillow with the rubber case is shaken up, smoothed and grooved in the center lengthwise by making pressure with both arms. The sides are then held together in order to maintain the groove. Standing on the side of the injured leg, facing the foot of the bed, the free forearm is placed alongside the leg to brace it, while the leg is grasped firmly, bridging the break, extended and raised. 

The pillow, with its closed end first, is inserted lengthwise and drawn up beyond the popliteal space. The leg is now steadily lowered so that it rests in the groove of the pillow.

To prevent overpressure on the heels the pillow should be slightly bolstered upward at the projecting end, or a heel ring be used if more comfortable. The sand bags are replaced under the pillow, the longer one on the outer side of the leg, the shorter one on the inside, so arranged that thorough support is secured. 

The surgical cradle is now placed in position, reaching to the foot of the mattress if the patient is an adult, otherwise it may be moved higher in bed to suit the length of the patient.

Arrangement of upper bedclothing The top sheet and blanket are re-adjusted and tucked in at the foot. The extra fullness of the sheet outlines the edge of the cradle in a fold, and a square corner is made as usual. If on account of the height of the cradle the

BED WITH CRADLE 

45 first blanket cannot be tucked in at the side, the second or third blanket, as it may be, is put on crosswise to supplement, and arranged as the sheet in outlined folds over the edge of the cradle, with fullness carried to the sides of bed and tucked in securely. The spread is put on as usual with the exception of arranging it at the foot of the bed in an outline fold along the edge of the cradle, from which it hangs at the side in free straight folds. 

Final details 

The patient's towel is placed in position on the lower rung behind the pillow. The bedside table and furniture are left in order, the soiled linen is removed. 

Early observations in fracture cases Report any abrasions of the skin so that they may be attended to at once to prevent infection and bedsores. 

Keep the patient clean and dry by bathing and powdering the back twice a day and more frequently if there is indication of redness of the skin.

See that the patient holds his position in relation to the fracture as the surgeon has ordered.

In caring for the patient at bedtime, the cradle and pillow should be removed for re-arrangement, to insure comfort and rest during the night.

BED WITH A BALKAN FRAME AND OVERHEAD SUSPENSION 

A Balkan Frame is a rectangular wooden, or metal, overhead bed frame, reaching nearly to the floor, and fastened to each corner of the bed with iron clamps or

bandages. It was first introduced during the Balkan war in 1911.

Purpose 

It serves as a support for the attachment of pulleys, ropes, weights, and counterweights, by which a fractured limb, or injured joint of the upper or lower extremities, may be suspended above the surface of the bed in a heavy wire splint, usually a Thomas splint, and canvas hammock or sling. Hanging from the overhead frame, within easy reach of the patient, are stirrup-shaped hand grasps which enable the patient to change his position from side to side, or by which he may be raised in bed for any purpose or may raise himself horizontally or to a sitting position. This changing of position is to be encouraged by the nurse, as it promotes circulation and is a definite factor in preventing bedsores and hypostatic pneumonia.

Method of making a Balkan Frame fracture bed With a fracture of the upper extremities no change is necessary in making the bed. With fractures of the lower extremities, the under part of the bed is made as usual. 

In making the upper part the usual application of the bedclothing is impracticable because of the suspension of the fractured limb above the surface of the bed. 

Two sets of bedclothing are used, and they are applied in two cross sections-an upper and lower, corresponding in terms to the part of the body covered. The upper section covers the patient above the shoulders and reaches to the thighs, or as far as the ropes and pulleys of the sus-

BED WITH CRADLE 

47 pended limb will permit. The lower section starts where the upper section leaves off, and covers the uninjured leg. 

It reaches across to the opposite side of the bed under the suspended leg and over the remainder of the bed, and is tucked under the foot of the mattress. 

Each article of bedclothing is folded in two, hem to hem. The folded edges of the upper and lower sections 

BED WITH BALKAN FRAME 

are pinned together across the middle of the bed, on each side of the fractured limb. The folded edges of the two spreads are also pinned together with large safety pins close enough to prevent uncomfortable open spaces. 

Guard against any interference with the ropes which con-

trol the traction. The hems of the sheet of the upper section are turned over the spread at the top. The spread of the lower section is tucked under the mattress at the foot. The corners are turned, and the spread hangs at the side of the foot of the bed in the usual slanting folds. 

The suspended limb, if not encased in plaster, is enveloped for comfort in flannel or sheet wadding.

In cases of fracture of lower extremities when both limbs are suspended, the under part of the bed is made as usual, the patient elevating himself by means of the overhanging hand grasps. In applying the upper bedclothing the same rule is carried out in the arrangement of the upper cross section as in the single fracture, covering the patient's body as far as the thighs of both suspended limbs. A spread will serve to cover the lower part of the bed under the elevated limbs, and is tucked under the foot of the mattress. Finish sides in slanting folds, and turn back as in an open bed.

The Rainbow extension fracture bed In making a bed with a Rainbow attachment, the same rules are carried out as in the making of the Balkan Frame bed, except in cases where the apparatus may interfere with the cross section method. In that case each article of the upper bedclothing is folded in two, lengthwise, in two separate sets, one division being the duplicate of the other. The folded edges are approximated with safety pins from the top, and as far as the suspended fractured limb, which must protrude between the two sections of bedclothing without interference to position. 

Finish by pinning as close to the fractured limb beneath

BED WITH CRADLE 49 as the apparatus will permit, and down as far as the foot of the bed, where the bedding may be neatly arranged in the usual manner. For security the two spreads may be pinned together apart from the underlying bedclothing.

Purpose VIII MAKING OF AN ETHER BED 

An Ether Bed is one that is prepared for a patient who is recovering from the effects of an anesthetic, usually ether, following a surgical operation.

Equipment 

2 gray blankets 

I half sheet 

2 rubber pillow cases dressing towel washed gauze

Preparation of bed 

Under part 

3 hot water bags kidney basin round agate basin wooden gag shock blocks The bed is made as usual. Without removing the pillows the upper bedclothing is turned down to the extreme foot of the bed in a double box plait. The first gray blanket is put on crosswise, so that it may be tucked in securely on each side. The lower corners of the blanket are turned under to permit its introduction to the foot of the mattress without disturbing the upper bedclothing. The selvage edge of this blanket should reach under the pillows at the top. A half sheet is folded 

20 inches wide and serves as a narrow draw sheet 50

MAKING OF AN ETHER BED 

51 between the patient's hips and the rough woolen blanket underneath.

Pillows 

The rubber cases are drawn over the pillows. The first pillow has its cotton case put on in the usual way. The second pillow has its case put on in the opposite direction 

OPEN ETHER BED BEFORE REMOVAL OF HOT WATER BAGS 

to the rubber case, so that each end of the pillow is encased and protected. Place the first pillow on the bed with its closed end towards the table. The second pillow is placed lengthwise on the side opposite the table, with its closed rubber end towards the foot of the bed, overlapping the first pillow at the head. This pillow serves to

support the patient in a semi-lateral position while under ether. If the shock blocks are used for elevation of the foot of the bed, an additional pillow should be bound to the head of the bed to prevent pressure of the patient's head against the frame of the bed. 

Upper part of bed 

The upper gray blanket is put on in the usual way. 

Turn the lower corners back at acute angles and slip under the upper bedclothing even with the foot of the mattress, but do not tuck in, so as to afford easy removal without disturbing the rest of the bedding. Turn back the surplus blanket at the top over itself to prevent coming in contact with the patient's face.

Warming the bed Fill three hot water bags with water of a temperature sufficient to heat the upper and lower bedclothing. Force out the air and test for leakage. Two bags are placed in the middle of the bed, the third at the extreme foot. 

In order to get the upper bedclothing warmed, the gray blanket is pulled up into position over the hot water bags, followed by the bedclothing. 

If only one bag is available, move it about the bed at ten-minute intervals. The number of bags should be noted on a tag and pinned on the outside of the bed so that none may be overlooked on reception of the patient. 

If the patient is delayed, refill the bags.

By the installation of blanket warmers and the use of hot blankets in place of hot water bags all danger of burns from the bags is avoided.¹ 

1 This practice is followed at the Massachusetts General Hospital.

PRELIMINARY COURSE 

53 Arrangement of equipment on table Six or more pieces of washed gauze are folded separately and placed on the right upper corner of the table. 

The dressing towel is folded diagonally and placed at the lower corner of the table. The kidney basin is turned upside down and placed behind the towel. The wooden gag is placed between the towel and the basin. A space is left for the post-operative report chart. On the shelf underneath is placed the round enameled basin for the reception of the soiled gauze.

Accessories 

The shock blocks are placed on the floor at foot of the bed, one beside each leg, ready for use if needed. Table and chair are moved from bedside to afford clear passage way on the arrival of patient.

IX SITTING A PATIENT UP IN BED Purpose 

To aid a convalescing patient in recovery of strength.

For relief of dyspnœa. 

For the prevention of hypostasis. 

To facilitate free drainage of wounds following operation on the thoracic and abdominal cavity. 

For change of position and comfort of patient following a breast operation.

Equipment 

2 iron rods canvas support rubber pillow case safety pins large sheet 

2 small hair or feather pillows shoulder wrap

Slip ordinary pillow into rubber case, place in the middle of a diagonally folded sheet and envelop pillow, twisting the extended corners of the sheet two or three times to secure the pillow, ready for later use as a thigh support. 

Procedure 

Moving the patient to adjust headrest

Move table out from bed. Loosen upper bedclothing half way down and turn back at patient's waist line. Re- 54

SITTING A PATIENT UP IN BED 

55 move top pillow. Pass one arm over the patient's shoulder and under his opposite arm, grasping firmly. Instruct the patient to draw up his knees, brace feet, and hold his body rigid. Bend a trifle forward with knees slightly flexed, and with foot braced as if ready to step. Instruct patient to pass his arm under yours and his opposite arm over your right shoulder and clasp his hands. With the free hand under the patient's sacrum, taking one or two side steps, slide him down in bed as far as possible. 

Draw down the remaining pillow and place under the patient's head until the headrest is adjusted. 

If a second nurse assists in moving the patient she stands on the opposite side of the bed. 

Headrest 

The headrest consists of two iron rods with an adjustable and tightly stretched canvas between them. The canvas is double and made after the fashion of a roller towel. Buckles and straps are sewed to its upper edges. ia 

HEADREST

The rods engage the upright and side frame of the bed, one rod on each side. The canvas is stretched while placing it in position. The headrest can be stored in a very small space when not in use, and the canvas can be easily laundered. 

Adjustment of headrest 

Slip the first iron rod through one side of the circular canvas and adjust to upright head of bed. Step to the opposite side of bed and do the same with the second rod, and while on this side attach the lower end of the rod to the side frame of bed. Return to the first side and complete adjustment of the lower end of the first rod in the same manner. Owing to the tension on the canvas, this procedure is necessary in order to secure it to the bed. 

Buckle the supporting straps on the upper edge of the canvas to the rung of the bedstead to hold in place. 

Moving the patient up on the headrest 

Stand at the patient's right if possible. Remove the firmer pillow, hold horizontally and shake the feathers to the lower edge. Place in position at the lower edge of the headrest. If able to move the patient without help, place one arm under his flexed thighs, and the other over the nearest shoulder and under the opposite axillary space. The patient may support himself by either grasping the nurse's nearer arm above the elbow with both hands; by placing his hands palms down on the bed; or by grasping a suspended support from the head of the bed with both hands. This support may be made of webbing about five inches broad and be attached to the rung of the bed over the patient's head.

SITTING A PATIENT UP IN BED 

57 Arrangement of pillows and shoulder wrap Support the head and shoulders with the second pillow. 

In order to prevent the patient from slipping down in bed, the thigh support is slipped under the patient's flexed knees and braced firmly against the back of the thighs. 

Give a couple of twists to the free ends of the sheet, slide under the canvas headrest at each side, and tie securely to the upright of bed.

An improvised wrap is made from the shoulder blanket. 

Fold back the border, place about the patient, finish at the neck with a narrow rolled collar of the blanket and pin down the front. The border makes the collar with lapels. Pointed cuffs are made by folding back the corners of the blanket at the wrist and fastening with a safety pin. This arrangement permits the patient to move his arms about freely without exposure restriction. or The corner of a small pillow is tucked in on each side of the patient, the remaining part of the pillow serving as an arm support.

The final touches are given the bed. 

Arrangement of bedside table and accessories The bedside table is moved down. The table drawer and necessary articles should be within easy reach of the patient, especially drinking water, and hand bell if not near electric push button.

When the permanent headrest is in use, as in cases of pneumonia and cardiac conditions, the adjustable bedside table is preferable, as it may be placed across the bed and on it a pillow may be laid to support the patient's arms in conditions of dyspnoea.

Observation of the patient 

Note pulse, color, signs of fainting or dizziness. If sitting up for first time, to avoid over-fatigue, twenty to thirty minutes is long enough, unless otherwise ordered by the doctor. 

If an abdominal case, note binder and adjust before sitting the patient up.

When patients are obliged to be in the sitting position day and night, they should be closely observed for pressure sores and preventive measures be taken in the daily care. Frequent bathing, change of position by slightly tipping to one side at four hourly intervals during night and day, by use of pads and air rings (the latter should be in good working order) are some of the preventive measures employed. 

To make a bed without removal of headrest

The upper part of the bed is made as usual. In making the under part the patient and pillows are moved to the opposite side of the headrest and the patient is turned on his side. The first side of the canvas support is unfastened if necessary at the top, and the lower end of the rod is unclamped from the side of the bed frame below and placed on the bed. The rod which is hooked on the upright frame of the bed is left undisturbed. 

The under part of the bed is then made as usual and when completed the iron rod is replaced and the canvas support refastened. The patient is moved over to the finished side of the bed when the same procedure is carried out. The patient is finally moved to the middle of the headrest and the bed completed.

SITTING A PATIENT UP IN BED 

59 

Improvised headrests 

A hard wood board as wide as the bed and three feet deep, padded with an old blanket and covered with a sheet, may be placed against the head of the bed in a slanting position and serve the purpose of a headrest very well. If difficult to hold in position bore auger holes at each end large enough to permit the introduction of fine rope or webbing, and attach to head of the bed.

A common kitchen chair with a firm back may also be used, turned upside down. A double half sheet may be bound around the spaces between the legs, which will prevent damage to the head of the bed and will keep the pillows from slipping. Place the pillows lengthwise against the chair with an additional small pillow for the head.

Removal of the headrest 

For the removal of the headrest the patient is moved down in bed as in the procedure of adjusting the headrest. The iron uprights and canvas are removed. The pillow under the patient's head is taken out and placed against the head of the bed, standing on its side, to prevent collision of the patient's head with the bed while moving him up. When he is in position the pillow is drawn under his head by its lower edge, and the other pillows added. The patient's condition is noted and the bed straightened out.

Purpose 

As an aid in progressive convalescence. 

For change of position and comfort in chronic conditions.

For the relief of dyspnoea. 

Equipment a comfortable wrapper woolen petticoat or pyjamas slippers and stockings 

2 gray blankets 

2 feather pillows or one pillow and an air ring rubber pillow case large comfortable chair hand bell necessary linen for changing the bed as soon as the patient sits up 

Preparation of the chair 

Remove the upper pillow from under the patient's head. 

Slip on the rubber case and place on the seat of the chair crosswise. Place first blanket crosswise over the back of the chair, allowing about 18 inches to hang freely over the back. Arrange second blanket across the seat of the chair and high enough to pin about the patient's waist, 60

which will be sufficiently long to envelop the feet. Place the chair with its back parallel with the foot of the bed and far enough from the side to permit the nurse to step. between it and the chair. If a rocking chair, a sand bag may be placed under each rocker to steady it. 

Preparation of the patient The upper bedding is loosened, spread removed and folded as usual. Remove one blanket. If a patient with an abdominal wound, see that the binder and perineal straps are secure.

The patient is dressed from the feet up with the exception of the wrapper. Turn back each corner of the blanket and sheet at the foot of the bed and dress the patient's feet. Insert the hand into the toe of each stocking and turn it inside out as far as the heel. Slip the foot of the stocking over the patient's foot as far as the heel and gradually draw it up into place. Pull the nightgown down about the patient's knees. 

The pyjamas or petticoat are drawn over the feet. 

When the hips are reached the patient braces the feet on the bed and slightly raises the body, which enables the nurse to adjust the underwear at the waistline. 

Before putting on the wrapper it should be unbuttoned down the front and rolled up at the back from hem to arm spaces. Slip the rolled wrapper behind the patient's neck and towards the arm which is to be first inserted into the sleeve. The nurse inserts her hand into the sleeve of the wrapper and grasps the patient's hand and cuff of the nightgown, and draws them through the sleeve. The rolled wrapper is now drawn towards the second arm and the same process is carried out. The

shoulders of the patient are supported, and the wrapper unrolled towards the waistline. Finally the patient flexes the knees and raises the hips, and the wrapper is pulled down smoothly to the ankles and fastened up in front. 

If it is a closed wrapper it should be gathered up at the back and put on in the same way as a closed nightgown. 

The upper bedclothing may now be folded back to the foot of the bed.

Note the character of the patient's pulse now, especially if getting out of bed for the first time. 

Getting the patient out of bed With one hand behind the patient's shoulders, slowly elevate the body into an upright position, while with the other hand over the knees the feet are drawn over the edge of the bed. Permit the patient to rest a few minutes in this position, supported by the nurse, in order that he may regain his equilibrium and overcome the sense of faintness or dizziness.

The patient's pulse is again noted. If the nurse is getting the patient up without assistance, she stands close at the patient's side and slips her arm over the patient's shoulder nearest to her, and under the opposite arm. Her second arm is passed under the patient's knees. The patient supports himself by clasping his hands firmly about the nurse's shoulders. The nurse then turns to the right, which should bring her parallel with the side of the chair in which she places the patient. 

Assistance should be obtained when sitting up a patient who cannot step on the floor; the reasons are obvious, as it is no longer considered heroic for a nurse to strain her back in lifting or carrying burdens beyond her strength.

Lifting a patient from a bed into a chair on a sheet A large sheet is folded lengthwise 18 inches wide. 

With the patient sitting on the edge of the bed, a nurse and her assistant stand on each side of him and slip the center of the folded sheet under his hips. The ends of the sheet are rolled close to the sides of the patient. He is instructed to place his arms around the shoulders of those lifting. They grasp the sheet firmly with both hands and lift the patient into the chair. The sheet is left under the patient ready for his return to bed. 

This method of moving the patient gives assurance of safety, prevents sagging, and lessens the strain in lifting. 

LIFTING A PATIENT INTO A CHAIR ON A SHEET

Arrangement of the blankets The first blanket is pinned around the waist and down the side, folded under the patient's feet and pinned comfortably behind the ankles.

The shoulder blanket is pinned as described in sitting the patient up in bed. The second pillow is placed open end down behind the patient's back. The chair is moved from the bedside and placed facing a pleasant outlook whether it be door or window, as a change of scene is always welcome to the shut-in or convalescent patient. 

Accessories for the comfort of the patient Move the bedside table within easy reach, with drawer towards the patient. Place a pitcher of fresh water and a drinking tube on the stand. Add a hand bell if the patient is occupying a room and sitting where he cannot reach the electric push button.

If the bed is made up with two mattresses, turn both. 

Make bed at once, in case of need. Fold back upper bedclothing to extreme foot of bed, ready for the reception of the patient later.

Assisting the patient back to bed When it is time for the patient to return to bed, the chair is moved close to the bedside. The back of the chair should be parallel with the foot of the bed. The outside coverings and pillow behind the patient's back are removed, and the pillow placed on the bed. 

If the patient's chair is lower than the bedside, the nurse should have assistance in getting the patient into the bed; otherwise if the patient is not too heavy or help-

less, she may manage alone by placing her arm behind the patient's shoulder nearer to her and extending it towards the opposite arm. The second arm is slipped under the patient's knees. The patient in turn supports himself by firmly clasping his hands over the nurse's shoulders. The patient should be instructed to hold his body rigidly. 

The nurse with knees slightly flexed bends over, raises the patient out of the chair, takes one step sidewise, and a few steps forward, which should bring her to the side of the bed, on which she places the patient. She slowly lowers his shoulders onto the pillow, while with the other hand the lower extremities are lifted and extended towards the foot of the bed. The bedclothing is partially drawn up over the patient, who is then undressed in the same order as he was dressed, the wrapper being removed last of all by rolling it up under the body as far as the shoulders, when the nurse reaches over and draws the gown up to the back of the neck. 

The nurse slips her hand into the arm hole and supports the patient's elbow, while with the other hand she slips off the sleeve. One hand is introduced through the opening of the wrapper, and placed on the crown of the patient's head to prevent contact of the wrapper with the face. With the second hand the nurse slides the opening of the wrapper over her extended hand and behind the patient's neck, when the second sleeve is easily slipped off. 

The nightgown and upper bedclothing are now adjusted, the rubber case removed from the second pillow, which is placed under the patient's head. 

The pulse and the patient's general condition are noted. 

One hour should be the maximum time permitted for a patient to sit up on the first day. Before, or at the end

of thirty minutes, note the pulse for its quality, and whether or not it is increased in rate. Observe the facial expression and general attitude for fatigue. If the observations made are unfavorable, do not wait for the completion of any special time, but get the patient back to bed.

To dress a patient in blankets to go out in a chair The patient may be fully or partially dressed according to the season and the stage of convalescence. One 

PATIENT DRESSED IN BLANKET TO GO OUT IN A CHAIR 

should err on the safe side, and overdress rather than underdress the patient.

The arrangement of the chair and pillows is much the same as in getting the patient out of bed, with the exception of providing an extra wrap under the blankets. The first blanket is so arranged that it may serve as a wrap for the patient's shoulders, and headcovering or improvised hood in one. In order to do this the blanket is placed crosswise over the

chair, allowing about 20 inches of the selvage edge to hang over the back. After the usual arrangement of the pillow and second blanket the patient is moved into the chair. The blanket is pinned at the patient's waist, and down the front or side. Sufficient allowance should be made to turn part under the patient's feet, in which may be enveloped a hot water bag or heated soapstone for comfort in cold weather.

A dressing towel is applied as a covering to the patient's head. It is secured at the back of the neck with a safety pin. The overhanging selvage edge of the first blanket is carried over the patient's shoulders on each side, leaving sufficient at the back of the neck to make the hood. This is done by pinning the two selvage edges together at the back of the patient's neck with a safety pin, thus making a circle for the hood, which is carried over the patient's head.

The selvage edge is folded back, and the hood is fitted neatly about the patient's face. The remaining fullness at each side of the head is gathered into folds and pinned under the chin. The blanket is again pinned over the chest. The bordered ends of the blanket which hang over the side of the chair are brought over the knees of the patient. The center of each end makes a sleeve by folding back a cuff sufficiently wide to envelop the patient's wrist, and also to allow for the insertion of the hands in the opposite sleeve for warmth if desired.

XI ROUTINE CLEANING FOLLOWING THE DISCHARGE OF A PATIENT Purpose 

For cleanliness.

For the reception of a new patient. 

Bedside preparation basin of 2% formaldehyde solution, 6 ounces pail with hot, soft, soapy solution washed gauze sponge whisk broom 2 laundry bags rubber table cover, or newspaper 

Procedure 

Strip the bed, inspect blankets to determine if fit to use again without laundering; reserve for bedmaking, or place in blanket bag. 

Dampen brush in formaldehyde solution and brush the hair mattress on all sides; when brushed remove from bed and slide behind the head of bed. 

Wash rubber draw sheet with soap and water. Dry and suspend over back of chair. Cleanse rubber pillow covers in the same way, if in use. 

Brush the remaining mattress as the first one; remove from bed and place beside the other. 68

ROUTINE CLEANING 

69 

Brush pillows and place over mattresses. 

Use fine sand soap or soap powder for stains on the bedstead.

Wash the bedside chair and table and put fresh paper in drawer. If a bureau, the drawers receive the same attention.
