Carry utensils to lavatory. Dispose of materials as instructed in the disinfection of beds, omitting the sterilization. (See page 74.)

Scrub hands and return to ward. If a room, see that it is aired and make up the bed.

Special observations 

Inspect mattresses. If tufts are missing and if hollowed, tag "to be repaired" and send to mattress room. 

Replace with fresh mattresses.

For blood-stained pillows and mattresses make a paste of raw starch and salt solution, or chlorinated soda solution I-200, and cover the stains. When dry brush off and repeat the treatment if necessary; otherwise scrub with tepid water. The chlorinated soda solution will bleach out the color of the ticking.

Vaseline and oil stains may be removed with benzine. 

For ink stain wash the article in soap and water, boil, and when dry apply oxalic acid solution or salts of lemon, and place in the sun.

Inspect the rubber draw sheet for perforation. Inspect the seams of the pillows for feather leakages, and repair at once.

See that nothing is left by the previous occupant.

XII TERMINAL DISINFECTION OF FURNITURE Purpose AND EQUIPMENT 

For cleanliness and as a safety measure against the spread of disease.

Occasions 

Following the discharge of a patient who has been ill with a communicable disease common to those admitted in a general hospital, or who has been on special precautions on a ward or isolated in a room. 

Following the transfer of a patient to a contagious hospital who, while in the hospital, has developed an acute infectious disease secondary to his first disease, which may be smallpox, scarlet fever, measles, or diphtheria.

Equipment rubber table cover newspapers precaution gown 

2 clean precaution laundry bags special whisk broom precaution tags inscribed as follows: 

Patient's name, ward, disease, date, nurse's signature on reverse side 70

TERMINAL DISINFECTION 

71 In case of death add "Allen Street House," the mortuary basin with 8 ounces of 2% formalin solution enameled pail with one-half gallon of 1-600 of a 2% sylpho-nathol or lysol solution made with hot softsoap suds washed gauze sponge rubber gloves are used in cases of spore-producing diseases, as anthrax, gas bacillus, etc.

Place rubber cover on bedside table, and arrange materials in order. 

Procedures of disinfection

Mattresses and rubber sheet Put on the precaution gown. Tag laundry bags, and place on clean newspaper on the floor. Newspapers may be spread also upon the floor about the field of operation. 

Strip bed, place linen in one bag, and blankets in the other. Attach tags to brass rings on pillows and mattresses. Place card rack and chart holder on table. 

Dip the whisk broom into the formalin solution, and evenly sprinkle the mattress. Brush the surface about the tufts, corners, and sides thoroughly. Reverse the mattress and repeat the process. Remove and stand on its side at the head of the bed. 

Fold the rubber sheet in two and place over remaining mattress. Dip the whisk broom into the formalin solution. 

Sprinkle and brush surface of the sheet. Fold the sheet over end on end so that the disinfected surfaces are brought together. When the sheet is reduced to a small folded square it is taken in the hand, and the last side

which came in contact with the mattress is sprinkled and brushed. The rubber sheet is refolded, the opposite side done in the same order, then wrapped in a clean newspaper and placed over the back of a chair until ready to be washed. The formalin is active meantime. Rubber cases are done in the same manner. 

Dampen and brush the second mattress in the same way as the first, and remove from the bed and place beside the first one.

Pillows 

The pillows are placed on the bedstead and their exposed surfaces sprinkled with formalin solution, and brushed. Before turning the pillows over to disinfect the second side, a clean newspaper is spread on the slats to receive them and prevent contact of disinfected surfaces of pillows with infected bedstead. When done, place the pillows on top of the mattresses. They are now ready to be transferred with the mattresses, to be either hung in the mattress room, sunned and aired, or transferred to the mattress sterilizing room where they are sterilized in autoclave, the treatment depending upon the virulence of the infection. In transportation the bedding is covered with a clean sheet and the orderly wears a gown. 

Walls, woodwork, bedstead

The cleaning of a room is done by the hospital house cleaner under supervision.

If the patient has been one who could be kept on an open ward, as a case of pneumonia, typhoid, or a venereal case, the wall about the bed should be cleansed with the solution of sylpho nathol, and especially, if a corner bed, the wall in contact with the side of the bed.

TERMINAL DISINFECTION 

73 The bedstead, table, and chair are moved to center of room. The walls and woodwork are washed with hot, soft, soap suds, not omitting the outside of the room door. 

Stains should be removed with soap powder, and ventilator wiped out. Window shades wiped off on both sides with damp cloth. Window and transom panes and sashes washed with fine sand soap. Electric push button should be disinfected. (Avoid getting moisture behind button.) 

The bedstead is washed with gauze sponge quite wet with the sylpho-nathol solution. All surfaces and every crack and crevice should be soaked with the solution. 

The bed is gone over a second time with the sponge wrung out dry. Stains are removed with sand soap. The chart holder and card rack are washed with the same solution, dried, and hung on the bed. 

Clean newspapers are spread on the bed. The rubber sheet is spread over the newspapers and thoroughly washed with soapy sylpho-nathol solution, in the same order as when brushed and sprinkled with the formalin solution. It is then hung over the head of the bed to dry and air. 

Bedside furniture

All surfaces of the bedside chair are gone over, special attention being given to under part of feet. A clean newspaper is placed on the seat of the chair, and the articles. from the table are transferred to it. The rubber cover from the stand is disinfected on both sides, and placed on the bedstead to dry. All surfaces of the bedside table are washed, including the drawer and drawer space. As nothing of any value is kept in the drawer, all articles which may be sterilized or burned are temporarily wrapped in clean paper. The drawer is placed at an open

window to air, in the sun if possible. Special attention should be given to the parts handled by the patient, and to the under part of the feet where dust pads form. 

Floor 

The floor is scrubbed, oiled, and polished after disinfection of the furniture.

The room is left to air for 24 hours. 

After care of equipment Materials used for disinfection are carried to the lavatory and placed on clean newspapers. Left-over solutions are emptied into the hopper. The gauze sponge is pulled apart and placed in the gauze pail. The soiled newspapers are wrapped and tied in a clean one, and placed in dust pail to be burned. The whisk broom is washed in hot suds, and hung up to dry and air. The solution pail is sterilized by boiling for ten minutes, dried, and put away.

After care of articles used by patient Ice cap and hot water bags are washed with sylphonathol solution, sunned and aired for a day.

The clinical thermometer is soaked for thirty minutes in 2% formalin solution, washed and dried.

Urinals and bedpans, douche pails and pans are sterilized by steam for ten minutes.

Cutlery and dishes are boiled in a large dishpan or sterilized in the dish sterilizer. 

Personal disinfection

Rubber gloves, when worn, are removed and placed in a basin of soapy solution. They are washed inside and out with hot, soapy water, wrapped securely in gauze,

TERMINAL DISINFECTION 

75 placed in boiling water and sterilized for five minutes. 

Remove, dry, and powder thoroughly inside and out; match and put away wrapped in a clean, not sterile, dressing towel. 

Remove gown and place in a special bag. Soak hands in disinfectant (corrosive sublimate 1-2000) at least two minutes by the clock. Scrub hands, nails, and arms, preferably with yellow soap, in agate basin with special brush for five minutes. Empty water into hopper, and sterilize basin. In the absence of a utensil sterilizer, sterilize basin and nail brush for ten minutes by filling with water and boiling over gas burner.

Sterilization of bedding 

After the following diseases-anthrax, erysipelas, measles, scarlet fever, smallpox, gas bacillus infectionhair mattresses, pillows, and rubber sheets are sent to the sterilizing room. Bed linen and blankets also are sent to the sterilizing room before going to the laundry.

XIII BEDSORES AND PRESSURE SORES 

A fully developed bedsore or pressure sore is a local gangrenous lesion involving the skin and underlying tissues. The chief cause is the constant pressure of an opposing surface against one or more parts of the body, which cuts off the blood, lymph, and nerve supply of the skin. 

Chief contributing causes 

Physical condition of the patient. Cases of lowered vitality, poor circulation, and wasting diseases. 

Continuous positions, either prescribed, as for fractures or drainage; or persistently assumed by the patient for relief from discomfort.

Appliances, worn for support of injuries, or correction of deformities.

Irritating substances in contact with the skin, as bodily discharges; or chemical agents, as iodine. 

Possible causes

Abrasions. These injuries to the skin are apt to be unobserved complications at the time of an accident, frequently about the sacrum. They may become infected. 

There should be immediate protection of the broken skin against infection and removal of pressure. 76

BEDSORES AND PRESSURE SORES 

77 Chafing. Chafing is frequently due to contact with the bedding or of two opposing surfaces of skin. Lubricate and protect skin.

Furunculosis. This infection may be due to uncleanness and moisture, or to some predisposing condition that has lowered the patient's resistance, as typhoid fever. 

Bathe thoroughly and keep the skin dry. 

Points of development 

These are learned best from study of the skeleton. 

Daily observation should be made of all bony prominences; for example, the occipital protuberance, especially in babies; tips of the ears and shoulders; elbows, on which the patient propels himself; the spine especially, including sacrum and coccyx; the hip bones, ankles, and the heels. These parts of the body have very little subcutaneous fat and are very sensitive to pressure. 

Early signs and progressive development A dark red spot may appear over a prominence, usually the sacrum, because of the weight which it carries. 

If immediate steps are taken for relief of the pressure the normal condition of the skin may be restored; otherwise, if neglected, in one day the skin at that point may assume a dark, purplish tinge, and the area about this discoloration will be indurated. Later, gangrene or death of the part takes place and will extend beyond the area of pressure with a resulting separation of the dead and living tissues called sloughing. Skin destruction may require grafting to repair defects; the sacral canal may be involved and lead to meningitis.

Owing to the putrefying nature of the sore, and the lowered resistance of the patient against infection, sufficient toxins may be absorbed by the body cells to change the picture of a bedsore to that of a fatal case of general septicemia.

Observation of a new patient 

If a bed patient before entering the hospital, observations should be made while giving the bed bath, and the following conditions be reported: red and tender areas over bony prominences, abrasions, chafing, furunculosis, signs of recent application of plasters or blistering agents, and active bedsores. Bedsores are seldom found except on those patients who are admitted to the hospital having suffered from lack of nursing care. 

Daily care and preventive measures

The essentials of daily care are an even, firm mattress ; cleanliness of body and bed; dry, unwrinkled sheet and nightgown; frequent change of position at regular intervals by the clock, or such as the patient's condition will permit; frequent rub with bathing solution followed by powder; for reddened area inunctions of castor oil or zinc oxide ointment; removal of pressure by means of small pillows, pads, and rubber appliances.

With patients wearing corrective appliances, undue pressure at some one spot may be brought to the nurse's attention following the application of a splint or plaster cast. This matter should be reported to the proper authorities. If a splint, it is usually readjusted; if a plaster cast, an opening may be made over the point of

BEDSORES AND PRESSURE SORES 

79 pressure. Powder may be puffed under the edges of casts to relieve irritation between the edges and the patient's skin.

Incontinent patients 

Because of the constant irritation from discharges, vigilance on the part of the nurse will be required. To protect the bed, large picked cotton and oakum pads may be used with a paraffin paper or newspaper foundation. 

A supply of these pads should be on hand to avoid delay in changing. Such patients need frequent washing and careful drying with a soft towel or gauze. The skin should be freely covered with castor oil, zinc-oxide ointment, lanolin, or rendered mutton fat to make it waterproof. A small enema is sometimes ordered every other day to clear out the lower bowel.

In changing the bedclothing, wet or damp sheets should be removed with care and not be dragged or pulled from under the sodden and tender skin, because there is danger of abrading it.

TREATMENT OF BEDSORES 

Treatment is directed to promote and improve the circulation of the part affected. More damage must be prevented and the damage already done must be remedied. 

In order to get results from any treatment, removal of pressure on the affected part must be obtained, the sore must be protected, and the skin about the broken down area be kept dry, clean, and massaged. 

In changing dressings use olive or cottonseed oil to cleanse the skin before applying the fresh dressing.

Method I 

zinc oxide of each castor oil one ounce balsam of Peru, one-half ounce

These should be blended, spread thickly on a sterile sponge and also about the sore. Apply the sponge to the sore and cover with a sterile pad held in place with narrow strips of adhesive tape. If the sore is at the end of the spine apply a T binder over the pad.

Method II 

zinc oxide ointment aristol tincture of myrrh 1-8, diluted one-half sterile sponges spatula cottonseed oil adhesive tape

Sponge the sore and edges with warm tincture of myrrh and sprinkle aristol powder over the sore. Spread oxide of zinc ointment thickly on a sterile gauze sponge; over that sprinkle aristol powder and apply over sore. 

Hold in place with adhesive tape.

Sloughing bedsores 

Bedsores vary in degree of severity according to the circumstances which surround their development. The most serious are those in the advanced stage of deep sloughing without any tendency to repair. To the patient with lowered resistance this affords a starting point

BEDSORES AND PRESSURE SORES 

81 for a local and general septicemia. Such patients need the best of nursing care, cleanliness, liberal diet, forced fluids, attention to bowels, fresh air and sunshine. 

Outside of surgical intervention the following methods may be prescribed: flaxseed poultices for a limited period, to clean the sore; application of sterile gauze pads wrung out of hot antiseptic solutions, as chlorinated-soda, I-200; boric solution, 2%; di-chloramin T; exposure to air and sun; exposure to electric light.

Di-chloramin T The skin surrounding the sore is protected with zinc oxide ointment or ambrine, as the di-chloramin T is irritating.

The di-chloramin T is applied directly to the area denuded of skin with an atomizer or medicine dropper, or it is directed on the sterile gauze to be placed over the sore.

The whole area is then covered by a dressing of sterile gauze secured with adhesive tape. 

Open air treatment

Remove dressing, cleanse sore and expose to air and sunshine, if possible, for several hours a day. A surgical cradle is placed over the exposed area and a piece of surgical gauze or cheese cloth is stretched tightly over the cradle and secured to the bed for protection of the sore from dust and flies. 

Electric light treatment

An electric bulb is hung from a cradle near the sore. 

An agate basin is inverted over the bulb to protect the bedclothing. This treatment, which requires close supervision, is applied in 2 and 3-hour periods, according to the depth of the lesion.

Time XIV BED BATH AND GENERAL TOILET 

The best time for giving a bath is before breakfast, or about an hour after. The patient should receive a full bath daily if his condition permits.

When patients do not receive a daily bath they should have arm spaces washed and "local" bathing done.

Equipment 

2 gray blankets rubber table cover patient's towel wash cloth washed gauze nightgown bathing solution

Preparation and procedure powder soap and nail brush scissors orange wood stick tooth brush¹ foot tub hot water bag if needed Carry equipment to the bedside in the empty foot tub. 

Place the rubber cover on the table, and on that arrange equipment in order. Carry empty tub back to lavatory and prepare water at temperature 108°-110° by bath thermometer. Tub should be one-third full.

Close nearby windows. If an open ward, screen the bed thoroughly; if a room, close the door. 

1 For care of mouth, see page 29. 82

BED BATH 83 

Application of first blanket

Turn down the upper bedclothing to waist line, double the first gray blanket end to end, and place over the patient with the open ends towards the head. Tuck the under edge over the patient's shoulders. Reach under the blanket and draw down the turned back fold of the upper bedclothing to the foot of the bed, which leaves the upper bedclothing folded halfway down from the foot of the bed. Draw down the upper edge of the gray blanket with the remaining half of the bedclothing to the foot of the bed. In order to clear the patient's feet an extra fold of the bedclothing is then turned upon itself, making in all three folds at the foot of the bed. 

Application of second blanket

Turn the patient to the opposite side of the bed. If a hospital gown, unbutton while the patient is on the side. 

The blanket is folded in two lengthwise and placed in the bed, with the fold towards the patient, and as high as the patient's neck. The upper half is gathered and pushed under the patient's feet, the remainder tucked against the patient's back. The patient is now rolled over facing the nurse, and the remainder of the blanket pulled through behind and smoothed out. The patient is turned on his back and nightgown is removed. 

Bathing the patient 

Spread the towel under the patient's chin. Bathe the eyes with clear water. Use a different part of the wash cloth for each eye. Dry gently. Use a limited amount of soap in washing the face, while for the remainder of the bath soap may be used more freely. The routine of washing each part of the patient's body with the soapy wash.

cloth, rinsing off the soap, and drying with the towel is carried on throughout the bath. 

The order in giving the bath should be the following: 

First, bathe the eyes, then the face, neck, arms (using the nail brush on the hands), chest, shoulders, and arm spaces, powdering the spaces after thorough drying. 

Next, the abdomen and surrounding parts, including the umbilicus, which may call for special attention in giving the so-called "admission" bath. Extraneous material may be removed by tooth picks with their points covered carefully with absorbent cotton and dipped in a warm, soapy solution, or olive oil, before using.

The thighs and knees are done next, reaching under the blanket, starting with the farther thigh. Each foot is 

NURSE GIVING PATIENT A BED BATH

BED BATH 

85 placed in the tub separately and bathed in the following manner: Turn back the corner of the upper blanket over the patient's leg as far as the knee. Cross that leg over the opposite one out of the way to make room for the tub, which is placed lengthwise at the foot of the bed. 

The patient's leg and foot are supported with both hands and lifted into the tub. The upper blanket is snugly tucked about the thigh to prevent exposure. The leg is washed and foot is scrubbed with the nail brush. The towel is spread out on the side opposite the tub, ready to receive the patient's foot, which is now lifted out of the water, partially dried with the wash cloth, and wrapped in the towel. The tub is placed on the table and the patient's foot is moved into position, dried, and covered with the upper blanket. The same procedure is carried out with the second leg and foot. 

The water is changed for the completion of the bath. 

The patient is now turned for the first time, and the back is bathed from the neck to the waist line, finishing with the sacrum and buttocks. Finally the whole surface of the back is briskly rubbed with bathing solution, and powdered.

Removal of under blanket While the patient is on his side, the under blanket is gathered in folds against his back, the lower part pushed under the feet. The patient is rolled over towards the nurse and the blanket removed. The clean nightgown, if a closed one, is gathered up in the back and slipped over the patient's head and the hands drawn through the sleeves. If an open gown it is put on as usual.

Local bathing 

As a rule a patient prefers to attend to washing between the thighs, if able, but the nurse should assume the responsibility, as it is an important part of the bath. 

Gather the nightgown up about the waist line. Wash the parts with several pieces of wet, soapy gauze and finish with dry gauze. In some conditions it may be necessary to place the patient on a bed pan or douche pan to do this washing; if so, pour warm, soapy water over the parts, dry thoroughly with washed gauze, remove the bed pan, and pull the nightgown down as far as it will reach.

Final toilet 

The finger nails are now manicured, using scissors and not too sharply pointed orangewood stick. Spread out a towel to catch the nail clippings. The finger nails are rounded evenly with the tips of the fingers, and the cuticle at the root of the nails slightly retracted with the blunt end of the orangewood stick.

In cutting the toe nails it is not well to clip off the corners of the great toe nails. Callosities of the soles of the feet are usually treated with prolonged soaking. (See 

Foot Baths, page 104.)

Completion of the bath 

Draw up the gray blanket and bedclothing together as far as the patient's chin. Fold back the bedclothing to the patient's waist line and remove the blanket. If the bed has been made previous to giving the bath the bedding is neatly adjusted, and the bedside put in order. 

The bath is at once recorded in red ink in its special space on the bath chart. The soap and dish go in the

BED BATH 

87 table drawer. The bath equipment is carried out and disposed of in the following order: The bath water is emptied into the hopper, the tub is scrubbed with soapy water, rinsed, dried, and hung up. The folded blankets are put in the linen closet. The washed gauze is put in the gauze pail. The nail brush and orangewood stick are rinsed in hot water and sterilized. The towel is placed in the laundry bag, and the bathing solution and powder are returned to their respective places. 

Care of the patient's hair

If the patient's hair has not been combed earlier in the morning it is done now.

For a woman, place an open towel lengthwise at the side of the patient's head and extend over the shoulder. 

Place the braid of hair upon the towel and separate. If the scalp is sensitive the nurse may grasp the hair midway between the head and end of hair and twist it once about the index finger of one hand, while she combs from the ends up. She then releases the hair and combs from the scalp down. The hair is rebraided in front of the ears, and the ends securely fastened with a small, narrow, rubber band. If the braids are not too heavy they may be crossed at the back of the patient's neck and held in place on the top of the head with a hair pin. 

A patient taking his own bath in bed When a patient can take his own bath he should be provided with blankets and everything else necessary. 

The nearby windows should be closed. The nurse should wash the patient's back and feet. Protect the back with a shoulder blanket. See that the patient is clean after the bath.

Special care of patients 

In cases where the nurse has time at her disposal, when giving a bath it is well to use an extra basin of plain warm water in which to rinse the wash cloth, or in which to keep an extra sponge or wash cloth. A sea sponge should be rinsed in clear water and squeezed dry, never forcibly wrung because of the danger of tearing it. Hang up in a mesh bag to dry in the air and sunshine. 

Separate wash cloths or sponges may be used for face and body. The same rule holds good in regard to the use of towels and soap. It is well to learn about the patient's personal habits in regard to the toilet. 

In the place of blankets a large Turkish towel may be used. The former are preferable, however, even if made of cotton, as they afford better protection. 

If giving a bath in a cold room, the towel may be placed on a hot water bag between the intervals of washing and drying the body. Another bag should be kept at the feet. 

If using alcohol for the final rub, warm by uncorking the bottle and placing in a utensil of hot water. Guard against using freshly laundered bed garments and linen before airing.

A tub bath If the patient can take a tub bath have everything ready on a chair or table in the bathroom. Fill the tub half-full at a temperature of 104° F. Spread a newspaper or folded gray blanket on the floor and render any other assistance necessary. Give as much privacy as possible.

XV INFANTS' BATH Equipment Toilet tray jar of sterile cotton swabs jar sterile balls of cotton safety pins, large and small loose absorbent cotton dish of 2% boric solution, lukewarm dish of cottonseed or other pure oil zinc stearate powder shaker of castile soap powder bottle albolene, medicine dropper in place of a cork pair blunt scissors fine tooth comb and brush

On table nurse's rubber and cotton aprons large soft towel soft face cloth or gauze small bath blanket large paper bag bath thermometer basin 89

Preparation 

During cold weather or in a drafty room it is preferable to bathe an infant before an open fire or radiator. 

The temperature of the room should be 75°-80°. Bathe quickly and deftly with as little waste of the baby's body heat and energy as possible. Bathe before feeding hour, never directly after.

Open paper bag and place on the floor by table. Put on the rubber and cotton aprons. Fill the basin with water 98°-100° F. Place baby on your lap face down on open bath blanket. Unpin all clothes, turn on back, loosen clothes and remove over feet, never over the head. Put soiled clothes on one side of the rack, wrap baby in bath blanket, and weigh him without clothes each morning. Procedure Eyes

Dip cotton ball in dish of boric solution, not getting fingers into cup. Hold ball with finger tips and squeeze lightly. Place left hand on head with thumb on cheek bone, pulling downward, and stroke across eye with cotton once from nose toward ear. Repeat this until eye is clean and well flushed with solution. Go over eye but once with each piece. Dry lids gently with dry cotton. Nose 

Cotton swabs are used, dipped in boric solution or albolene. Clean carefully.

Mouth 

Do not clean mouth without doctor's order. It is not needed in a healthy condition.

INFANTS' BATH 

91 Ears 

Do not touch unless they appear soiled. Then clean out carefully with cotton swabs and water, and dry with clean swab. Place all swabs, etc., in paper bag after use.

Face 

Wash rest of face with clear water, being careful not to touch eyes or mouth. Pat dry, do not rub.

Head 

Add sprinkle of soap powder to bath with hand, soap head and then wash off with cloth and water. If any "cradle cap," apply albolene, followed in a few hours by a castile shampoo. Rub lightly until head is dry. 

Hands and arms 

Soap and rub with the hand. Rinse off with cloth. Be sure to get all lint from about fingers and axilla. Dry by patting. Keep nails short. Cut straight across, covering the baby's eyes when cutting them. 

Neck, chest, abdomen, and legs

Proceed as with arms, paying special attention to the umbilicus, if cord is being dressed. Back 

Turn on stomach. Wash back as arms, finishing by rubbing briskly with hand to stimulate circulation and rest baby. Buttocks

Wash with absorbent cotton; oil or powder may be used, depending on the condition of the skin. 

Turn baby on back.

Vulva Wash vulva gently with cotton, separate and get into all creases. Oil.

Penis 

Clean with cotton and oil. Remove any lint.

Notes 

Heat rash may be powdered lightly. Thin, premature, and incubator babies are bathed with warm oil instead of with water. Oil will clean almost as well as water, but not so well as soap and water.

When the eyes, ears, and nose are done first, sometimes the baby will cry throughout the bath as a result of these disagreeable procedures, which should be carried. out with the utmost gentleness consistent with thoroughness. In such cases they may be left until last, when the bath itself may be enjoyed. 

Tub bath

In giving a tub bath the temperature of the water is usually 98°, at six months, 95°.

The eyes and ears are first done in the usual way, then the infant's head is directed over the tub, supported with the nurse's left hand, while with the other she gently lathers the scalp, rinses off the soap and pats the head dry. 

The arms, thigh spaces, and folds of the skin are next lathered, then the infant is lowered into the tub with the back of its head and shoulders supported in the firm grasp of the nurse's hand, with one of her fingers extended under the axillary space, while with the other she quickly rinses off the soap.

The infant is lifted out of the tub and enveloped in a soft blanket, and patted dry without exposure. Particular attention is paid to parts of the body where opposing surfaces come in contact. The genitalia are attended to following the drying process.

INFANTS' BATH 

93 

Dressing 

Place baby on a dry clean blanket, face down on the lap. If he is wearing a band put that on first, otherwise lay shirt across shoulders and folded diaper across buttocks. Turn baby over on his back. Fasten shirt then diaper. Draw petticoat and dress together over feet and put arms through sleeves. Put on stockings, bootees, etc. 

Turn on face and fasten neck bands. Comb hair. Put blanket about shoulders and turn back. Fasten his blanket and finish his hair. If he is puny, undersized, or very sick, put in bed with hot water bottle at 115°, for at least one hour.

BATHS FOR OLDER CHILDREN 

Children are usually bathed in the regular tub when possible. They have a full bath daily, including the head if the hair is short, and the routine is carried out as in the adult bath.

If a bed bath is given one large blanket is sufficient. 

The blanket is folded in two, one-half is slipped under the child, the remainder is brought over him, and the nurse bathes the patient between its folds.

XVI PEDICULOSIS AND ITS TREATMENT 

Pediculosis may be due to one or more of three types of pediculi: 

Pediculus capitis, or head louse Pediculus corporis or vestimentorum, or body louse Pediculus pubis, pubic or crab louse 

Each is a distinct species.

Preparation of the nurse in treating pediculosis The nurse should wear an all-over apron with short sleeves. She should avoid touching her face and hair during the operation and as a safety measure should comb her head at the earliest opportunity with a fine tooth comb.

PEDICULOSIS CAPITIS Symptoms 

This species, which inhabits the head only, is discovered because of the persistent itching of the head, often with scratched and infected areas and enlarged lymph glands behind the ears or in the upper part of the neck. 

Carbolic acid treatment

This treatment is effective in killing both lice and nits, but it does not remove nits from the hair. It is not injurious to the scalp. 94

PEDICULOSIS 

95 

Equipment half sheet and rubber rubber pillow case rubber shampoo cape two dressing towels round agate basin round agate pitcher foot tub

Preparation and procedure 

Apply rubber case to upper pillow. Protect bed with half sheet and rubber. Pin folded dressing towel around the patient's neck. Spread newspapers on the floor beside the bed and on them set the empty tub. 

For patients with long hair put on the shampoo cape with its end directed into the tub to act as a trough. 

Place the basin in the tub so that it will catch the carbolic solution. Turn the patient on the side with the back of the head directed over the edge of the bed above the tub, and with the hair hanging on the trough of the cape.

Protect the patient's eyes with the folded dressing towel. If necessary, plug the upper ear loosely with a pledget of absorbent cotton. 

Prepare a 1-40 solution of carbolic acid by pouring into the pitcher one pint of I-20 solution of carbolic acid; add one pint of hot water and mix thoroughly. With the mouth of the pitcher directed toward the back of the patient's head, pour the warm solution over the hair. Turn the solution back from basin to pitcher,

TOWEL CAP

and pour over the head about three times, until it is thoroughly saturated. Allow the hair to drip for a few seconds, or longer if it is very thick, before tying it up in the improvised towel cap. For children under three the hair should remain tied up one-half hour; for children from three to twelve, one hour; for adults, two hours.

At end of allotted time, move half sheet and rubber and place under the patient's head. Shake out the hair and let it dry. A shampoo is not necessary. When the hair is dry the dead lice can be combed out with a fine tooth comb.

Care of the cap and other materials On removal of the cap place towel in a basin of hot water and boil for ten minutes. Wash out the towel, shake over the hopper, dry and place in special laundry bag. The half sheet is placed in a pail and boiling water poured over it. It is then disposed of in the same manner as the towel. The chair is wiped with a damp cloth and the newspapers are carefully rolled up, tied in a clean newspaper and burned. 

To remove nits 

Smear the hair well with the following ointment: acidii salicylici sulphuris præcipitati petrolati, one ounce one-half dram each

Tie the hair up in a towel. Let it remain tied up over night. Wash hair in the morning with a very hot soap and water solution. Rinse with hot water and while the hair is wet comb carefully with a very fine comb. Repeat this process every other night until hair is free from nits.

PEDICULOSIS 

97 

PEDICULOSIS CORPORIS 

The adults live in the clothing, especially in the seams of the underclothes, feeding on the skin. 

Symptoms 

Itching, minute hemorrhagic spots on the skin. Bites on the skin are characteristic. Adults can usually be found by careful search of clothes.

Treatment 

Hot bath to remove adults from body. Shave if any ovum or eggs are found adherent to body hair. This is usually sufficient, but may be followed by application of the ointment prescribed for treatment of the head. 

Most important is treatment of clothing. That worn next to the body, if cotton, should be boiled twenty minutes, other clothing steam sterilized. In the absence of a sterilizer the clothing should be ironed twice with a hot iron, paying especial attention to the seams. If a sterilizer is used, one thorough treatment is sufficient. 

PEDICULOSIS PUBIS Symptoms 

Local itching above pubis and about genitalia. Careful search usually shows adults adherent to pubic hair and eggs laid at base of hair.

Treatment 

Thorough washing with soap and hot water followed by application of the ointment. Shaving the pubic hair is more certain to cure. In the place of shaving a fine tooth comb may be effective.

XVII CARE OF HEAD AND HANDS OF MALE Equipment PATIENT 

half sheet and rubber patient's towel wash cloth scissors bathing solution soap nail brush orangewood stick Pediculosis basin of warm water rubber table cover

Observe head for vermin and if present treat as in previous chapter. If an infested beard, it should be shaved, but never without an official order. If vermin are on eyelashes they will be found at extreme root and should be removed by forceps, as anti-vermin treatment may be dangerous to the eyes. But a 1% cream of yellow oxide of mercury may be used. 

In treating such patients the nurse should wear a long gown over uniform. 

Washing the head 

Slip rubber case on pillow. Place half sheet and rubber over pillow under patient's head. Unbutton the neck band of shirt and pull forward to prevent wetting. 

Spread towel under patient's chin ready to use. 

Apply soapy water over entire head with wash cloth. 

Follow by a thorough scrubbing with nail brush. In order 98

PRELIMINARY COURSE 

99 to get at roots of hair, if of any length, separate with thumb and finger of one hand as brush is used with other. 

Wring out wash cloth and wipe off, soap suds. If possible, hold patient's head over basin and rinse with. clear tepid water. Apply bathing solution on hair, rub into scalp, dry thoroughly with towel, and comb later. If patient has a beard, the same treatment is carried out. 

Care of hands

Place half sheet and rubber under patient's hands on near side of bed. On the rubber place basin of hot water and soap dish, in the basin a nail brush and wash cloth. 

Soak hand in basin and scrub briskly with brush about the nails. Dry with wash cloth, then with towel. trim finger nails, never too short. 

Treatment of calloused hands 

Clean and

Soak for an hour or more in hot, soapy water to which have been added a few drops of ammonia or a quarter of a teaspoon of sal soda. After soaking, dry hands and rub vaseline into palms. Use pumice stone to remove. thickened skin. Vaseline and soap poultices may also be used in extreme cases. In any treatment when vaseline is used, the bedding should be protected. 

To remove stains 

To remove machine grease, any sand soap, kerosene or benzine may be used; for paint, use turpentine or kerosene. For discolored finger nails, acetic acid 1-16 may be used, or peroxide of hydrogen.

Close nearby windows. Slip the top pillow into the rubber case. Replace under the head with a slant towards the edge of the bed. Fold the dressing towel about the 100

SHAMPOO OF WOMAN PATIENT 

ΙΟΙ patient's neck, cross in front and pin. Turn the patient with her back towards the nurse. Slip the half sheet and rubber under the lower pillow and against the patient's back. The edges hang over the side of the bed so that the unprotected part of the mattress above the rubber draw sheet is covered. Fasten the rubber cape about the patient's neck with its end directed over the side of the bed and above the tub on the floor. Place one of the folded towels on the pillow between the patient's face and the cape for comfort. Comb the hair, then start washing.

Procedure 

The wash cloth is put in the pitcher of soapy water, slightly wrung and wrapped about the first three fingers with the ends well in hand. Wash the scalp briskly from one temple to the other, meanwhile with the other hand separate the hair at its roots with the thumb and index finger. The remainder of the scalp is washed from the top of the head to the neck, using the comb to separate the hair at half-inch intervals so that no part may be overlooked. As the shampoo progresses the patient gradually turns over on her face. When the procedure is completed she is again turned on her side near the edge of the bed with her head projecting slightly over the edge above the tub on the floor, the hair hanging over the cape. The remainder of the soapy water is poured over the scalp and hair. The scalp is lathered and rubbed with the finger tips. The hanging hair is enveloped in the folded wash cloth and rubbed between the palms of both hands, sliding along its length.

Before rinsing, as much of the suds as is possible is squeezed out of the hair with the wash cloth. Rinse with

the water 100° F., manipulating the scalp at the same time to get out the soap, until the water runs clear. Use the pitcher of cooler water for the final rinsing, at 80° or less if the patient can bear it. 

When the rinsing is completed, envelop the hair in the wash cloth, which has been rinsed, and squeeze along its length to remove the water. Avoid twisting or wringing. Remove the towel from the pillow and bind firmly about the hair to absorb the moisture, then give to the patient to hold until she is moved into position. Push the foot tub under the bed, remove the rubber cape and neck towel, readjust the pillows, and move the patient to the middle of the bed on her back. Remove the half sheet and rubber from the side of the bed and place under her head. Remove the towel, spread out the hair, sprinkle the bathing solution or hair tonic over the hair and rub into the scalp. With the second towel rub the scalp dry, separate the hair in strands, and rub between opposite surfaces of the towel. The hair is then spread out over the pillow. When dry, comb and braid. Finally remove half sheet and rubber pillow case.

Remarks 

To guard against getting water into the ears plug the orifices with absorbent cotton. 

In rinsing long hair which may hang into the water in the tub, an empty basin may be floated on the surface and the hair allowed to drain into the basin. Instead of pitchers for rinsing, a rubber douche bag may be used, to the tubing of which can be attached a metal perforated spray.

The hair may be spread over a hot water bag at 125° to hasten drying in cool weather.

SHAMPOO OF WOMAN PATIENT 

103 After care of equipment Roll up wet articles in the rubber table cover and tuck under arm. Place utensils in foot tub and carry all out together. Hang up damp and wet articles to dry. Wash foot tub and pitcher, dry and hang up. Put newspapers in dust pail and bathing solution in its place. 

Shampoo of patient sitting in chair

Use a comfortable wooden chair and place a rubber cased pillow behind the patient's back. Get the patient. ready in the usual way. Place the foot tub on a stool or chair behind the patient. See that the rubber cape hangs over the back of the chair above the tub. Wash and dry the hair. Remove the cape and neck cloth. Place the half sheet and rubber over the pillow at the back of the patient's neck and spread out the hair to dry. If possible move the patient into the sunshine or in cold weather near a warm radiator. 

Snarled or matted hair

If a bed patient, protect the pillows with rubber cases. 

Rub and soak the hair with liquid petrolatum applied with a piece of gauze. Tie the head up in an old towel and do not disturb until next day. Remove towel, moisten one strand of hair at a time with alcohol, disentangle with the fingers, comb from the end carefully so as not to pull the hair, and braid each strand. Guard against overtiring the patient. In some cases only a little can be accomplished at a time. When the hair is thoroughly disentangled a shampoo is given.

In extreme conditions it may be necessary to cut out the matted sections of the hair, but this is not done without permission.

XIX FOOT BATHS CLEANSING FOOT BATH Purpose 

For cleanliness and comfort and for the treatment of corns, callouses, and thickened nails. 

Equipment gray blanket bath thermometer half sheet and rubber hot water bag foot tub nail brush patient's towel wash cloth and soap

Procedure 

Get the patient ready first. Loosen the upper bedclothing. Fold the spread up out of the way. Turn up the bedclothing even with the foot of the bed. Place a doubled gray blanket on the foot of the bed, tucking in just enough to hold it in place. Bring the upper bedclothing up with the blanket so it comes just below the knees. The blanket is released at the foot of the bed, and half sheet and rubber are placed under the feet. 

The tub is half-filled with water at a temperature of 108-110° by the thermometer and is carried to the bed. 

The feet are moved to the farther side of the bed and the blanket turned back on them. The tub is placed on the bed near the bend of the knees and is covered by the blanket. The nurse, with both hands under the blanket, 104

FOOT BATHS 

105 lifts the feet in so that the heels touch the water first. 

The feet are washed as in the bed bath, except that both feet are placed in the tub at once.

If the feet are to soak, the blanket is draped around the tub neatly, the surplus coming around the knees. A small pillow may be placed under them for comfort. The upper bedclothing is then brought over the tub to the foot of the bed, but the spread is left undisturbed. 

At the end of fifteen minutes the water may be reheated to the first temperature by adding hot water. In doing this the feet are lifted well out toward the side of the tub and the water is poured in with the stream directed away from the feet.

When the bath is finished, the towel is spread under the blanket on the opposite side of the bed and the feet are lifted out and placed on the towel.

The tub is removed and the feet are given a brisk rub under the blanket, dried thoroughly, and the blanket removed.

Purpose MUSTARD FOOT BATH 

For local and general effect in relief of congested conditions or spasmodic pain; in such cases as head colds, acute bronchitis, asthma; in delayed or painful menses. 

Equipment 

Use the equipment for cleansing foot bath, omitting the nail brush, soap, and wash cloth, and adding the 

small piece of muslin or gauze mustard tablespoon

For an adult use two tablespoons of mustard to one gallon of water. Tie the mustard in the gauze so that no free particles will float in the water and burn the patient; place in the water and thoroughly dissolve. The water is brought to the bedside at 108°-110°. 

The procedure is that for the cleansing foot bath. 

A hot water bag at 125° is brought in with the tub. 

The bag is wrapped in the towel and placed in the bed beside the patient. The towel thus is warmed, ready to dry the patient's feet.

When the feet are put into the water the time is noted, since the mustard bath may last from twenty to thirty minutes.

When the bath is finished and the feet have been dried carefully between the toes to prevent irritation, the feet may be wrapped in a blanket and the hot water bag placed between its folds.

If the patient is sitting up in a chair for the foot bath he should be made as comfortable as possible in an easy chair with the tub on a low foot stool. A blanket is draped around the body and tub to keep in the heat.

XX 

LOCAL APPLICATION OF DRY HEAT Purpose 

Dry heat is most commonly used for relief of pain and tension in inflamed areas; to hasten reaction and restore circulation; to promote comfort and induce sleep by its sedative effect.

Agents 

It may be applied locally by means of a hot water bag, and by various substitutes; by electric pads; by baking and by ironing. A hot water bag furnishes a quick and convenient method of application. 

HOW TO FILL AND APPLY A HOT WATER BAG Equipment hot water bag free from imperfections flannel cover for bag washed gauze pitcher bath thermometer hot and cold water

Procedure Filling 

Mix hot and cold water in pitcher and test with thermometer until it registers the standard temperature, 125°F. 107

for an adult, 115° for a child. Any temperature above these must not be used unless prescribed by the doctor. 

Warm the bag with hot water, then empty. For ordinary purposes, i.c., for the feet, the bag should be not more than three-quarters full.

For application to the abdomen, or over tender or sensitive surfaces, one-half full should be sufficient. Following the filling of the bag, the air is expelled by grasping the neck of the bag in the left hand, and holding it bottom down on a solid surface, thereby pressing the water up to the neck, when the stopper is securely screwed in to prevent suction of air.

The neck of the bag is wiped dry with the gauze, and is then tested for leakages by turning upside down and shaking forcibly. If the bag has stood this test the neck should be perfectly dry. If the neck is wet either the stopper is not properly fitted, or there is a deficient washer or leakage in the bag itself. An imperfect bag should be discarded and tagged for repairs. Before applying to patient enclose the bag in its cover and tie securely. 

In the absence of a cover one

APPLICATION OF DRY HEAT 

109 may be improvised from a soft dressing towel or piece of flannel. Spread out the towel and place the water bag on it parallel with its length, the neck protruding over the hemmed end of the towel, which is drawn about the neck loosely, and tied in a knot. The selvage edges of the towel are folded over the sides of the bag, and the surplus end of the towel is carried over the stopper, and the corners crossed on themselves. They are then tied about the neck so that the bag is wholly covered. 

Application 

If applying hot water bag to the patient's feet, place it crosswise.

In warming an empty bed for the reception of a patient, it is permissible to have the water hotter than when the bag is applied to a patient. 

In maintaining the heat in hot wet packs and poultices with hot water bags guard against the great danger of burning the patient because of the penetrating quality of moist heat. A dry blanket should intervene between the moist surface of pack or poultice and the bag, and close supervision should be kept.

Under no conditions should a nurse apply a hot water bag to the right lower quadrant of the abdomen on her own responsibility. Acute pain in that region may indicate an abscess of the appendix which might be ruptured by the application of heat.

IIO  

Caution and good judgment must be exercised in the application of any form of heat to an unconscious or paralyzed patient, to aged persons and infants, or in any case in which sensation is dull, as in surgical shock. 

The majority of serious accidents in nursing are due to the careless use of hot water bags. Burns occasioned by agents of heat are usually of second and third degrees. While in a sense localized, they are similar to bedsores and are deep and difficult to heal because of the destruction of the underlying tissues which nourish the skin. They are open to infection, augmenting the suffering and retarding the recovery of the patient, also involving the loss of confidence in the nurse, and if the patient is burned in a hospital, involving the reputation of the institution and of those in authority. 

After care of hot water bag Owing to the perishability of rubber, certain rules should be employed to prolong the use of water bags.

With proper care a bag should last at least four or five years. Empty and hang upside down to drain for fifteen minutes. Dry the grooved metal surfaces inside the neck of the bag; also the grooved metal stopper. This precaution will prevent the subsequent rusting and adhesion of the stopper to the neck of the bag. Inflate the bag, quickly apply the stopper, screw in, and hang up the bag. If not frequently used, powder and keep in a covered box.

Substitutes 

Substitutes may be used in the application of dry heat. 

Anything that will hold heat may be used, such as

APPLICATION OF DRY HEAT III 

heated plates, flatirons, soapstones, wrapped in flannel or old blanket; hot salt or sand applied in a woolen sock or stocking; or stone bottles filled with hot water and securely corked. Even baked potatoes and ears of corn rolled in the edge of the blanket which has been placed next to the patient have done good service in emergencies. 

Electric pads

These are used in the same general way as hot water bags. They should be closely watched, owing to differences in currents and possibility of fire. 

BAKING 

For this form of treatment portable zinc ovens are used, fitted with electric bulbs and adapted to any part of the body. The oven has a wire and plug for electrical connection. Other ovens are lined with asbestos and heated by Bunsen burners.

These ovens are used as a means of applying dry heat in such cases as old fractures, arthritis, neuritis, myositis, bursitis, and for circulatory conditions, as Raynaud's disease and varicose veins of the leg.

Those for the arm and shoulder are made a little higher than those for the foot and leg. 

The affected extremity is enveloped in several thicknesses of flannel, pieces of old blanket or turkish toweling as a protection against burning, and is thrust into the oven, except in cases when the bare shoulder is directly exposed to the heat. The temperature is controlled by turning on and off the electric current, or if the Bunsen burner is used, by raising or lowering the flame. The

II 

treatment is continued until the skin is hyperemic, which may be from five minutes to one-half hour, depending on the nature of the disease. The after care is usually light massage of the part with an alcoholic mixture and in some cases a cold application. 

IRONING 

This treatment is frequently prescribed as a therapeutic measure for relief of pain in such cases as sacroiliac strain, lumbago, and sciatica. An ordinary electric flatiron is generally used. One heated on a stove would serve nearly as well. The area to be heated is protected by several thicknesses of blanket or flannel; in the absence of either, a turkish towel would serve.

The iron is heated enough to produce a sense of extreme heat upon its application, but not enough to scorch the protective covering. The nurse irons up and down over the affected part with as much pressure as the patient can bear for ten to fifteen minutes, or until the skin is quite red. Blistering the skin must be avoided. 

This treatment is followed by the application of an analgesic preparation of menthol, olive oil, and alcohol.

The ironing treatment is sometimes prescribed twice a day.

For Application of Moist Heat see Poulticing and 

Stuping.

XXI LOCAL APPLICATION OF COLD Purpose DRY COLD 

Cold is applied for the relief of headache in febrile conditions; to relieve pain and swelling of deep-seated congestion or inflammation; as an aid to blood-clotting in hemorrhages; for its sedative effect, especially in the tachycardia of hyperthyroidism. 

Means of application 

When regular caps or bags cannot be obtained, various improvised appliances may be used, such as a rubber douche bag, a bathing cap, a sponge bag, a rubber glove, rubber sheeting or oiled muslin bag, and for small surfaces, as the bridge of the nose, finger cots or rubber glove fingers. 

Caution 

In long-continued application of cold the area to which it is applied should be closely observed for irritation of the skin or frost bite. Cold cream may be applied and a few minutes of gentle massage given before each application of the ice cap. 

In such cases the cap should have a dressing towel cover instead of gauze. If the skin is a deep red color the cap should be omitted for an hour or two. A frost bite is as serious an occurrence as a hot water bag burn, as there is arrest of circulation and destruction of tissue, resulting in a sloughing wound difficult to heal. 113

ICE CAPS OR BAGS Equipment ice cap or bag ice ice pick wooden mallet

Preparation of ice canvas bag washed gauze cover tablespoon enameled basin

With the ice pick mark off the amount needed, apply the mallet to pick with short light taps, when the desired quantity will be separated with but little waste. The ice is then placed in the basin and broken into smaller pieces with the pick, ready to be crushed.

In the absence of an ice crusher fill the canvas bag onethird full of ice, place the bag against a solid surface and pound with the mallet. Shake the ice from time to time. so that it may be broken more evenly. Break large sharpedged pieces again, or hold under running hot water a second in a piece of gauze to melt off the sharp edges. 

Filling and application of the cap Sufficient ice should be used to cover the bottom of the ice cap fully. Much more gives unnecessary weight and requires suspension if the part is tender.

Express the air as in hot water bag. Then quickly screw the stopper down. Perfect flexibility proves the absence of air. The cap should then be examined for leakages in case of an impaired rubber washer.

Dry the cap and cover it with a square of doubled gauze or cheesecloth tied from opposite corners over the stopper. Then push down about its neck. The cover serves to absorb moisture and to guard against frost

APPLICATION OF COLD 

115 bite. See that the whole surface of the cap lies upon the part to which it is applied. Avoid limiting the surface by tying the covering too tight.

If applied over the epigastrium, on a patient in the semi-recumbent position, the cap can be held in place by a loosely applied binder or a yard or more of 2-inch bandage attached to each loop of the cap. One bandage end may be carried under the arm and upward behind the patient's back to meet the second bandage over the opposite shoulder where it may be tied. In these cases a dressing towel is used to cover the bag. 

Replenishing cap 

As the object of an ice cap is the application of continuous cold, the contents should not be permitted to become slushy. When cap shows signs of ballooning, let out the air, pour off the water, and add more ice. The careful application and oversight of an ice cap is an index of good nursing. 

After care of cap 

When no longer needed, empty at once, wipe dry inside and out with washed gauze. Give special attention to the seams and grooved edges about the neck. Pack lightly with dry washed gauze. See that rubber washer is in place. Distend with air and apply stopper. If the bag is not in frequent use powder inside and out with talcum. 

Note 

In applying an ice bag over an outside dressing, a teaspoonful of coarse salt mixed with the ice will intensify the cold but cause the ice to melt more rapidly. If the

supply of ice is limited, place the cracked ice in a basin and mix with as much sawdust, flaxseed, or corn meal as the ice will take up, and a little more. 

AURAL ICE CAP 

This cap is usually ordered in cases of mastoiditis or earache. The hair about the mastoid process may be shaved. The ice should be about the size of lima beans.

Usually these bags are not covered. Tapes are attached to the four rubber loops. The kidney-shaped opening in the bag is slipped over the ear with its convexity. towards the mastoid process. The upper and lower tapes are tied from opposite sides. The bag is small and needs frequent filling.

ICE COLLAR 

The ice collar, as its shape and name imply, is especially adapted for the neck or to surround an extremity, and it may be applied to the hollow of the back in case of severe backache. In order that flexibility and comfort may be obtained the collar should not be more than twothirds full. It is covered with doubled gauze or cheesecloth.

RUBBER SHEETING ICE BAG 

Double a piece of rubber sheeting, about 16 by 24 inches, with the glazed side in, and fold again. The line of junction between the two doubled edges should be folded once upon itself and secured with two rows of fine sewing machine stitching. Leave an opening only large enough to introduce a dessert spoon, which may be used to fill the bag with ice. It should be broken evenly and mixed in a basin of sawdust or bran.

APPLICATION OF COLD 

117 When the bag is filled two-thirds full the opening is folded over upon itself and firmly secured with a clothes pin or a strong paper clip. This sheeting may be made into bags to fit any part of the body. 

ICE WATER COILS 

This is a modified form of cold application used under the same conditions as an ice bag. It is sometimes used in preference for various reasons, such as, the coils are less heavy and less disturbing to the patient; the cold is more evenly distributed over the part to which they are applied; and temperature may be easily regulated. 

Lieter coils are made of soft flexible metal tubes in sets to fit different parts of the body, i.e., cap-shaped coils to fit the head, kidney-shaped to fit the ear and cover the mastoid process, and those made in greater diameter to fit the chest and abdomen. These coils have two open extremities to which rubber tubing may be attached to serve as an inlet and outlet for the circulating ice water. 

Rubber coils are more flexible and can be homemade. 

To make one a little larger than an ice cap, use twentytwo feet of quarter-inch rubber tubing. Take five. twenty-four-inch strips of tape about one inch wide. Beginning three feet from one end of the tubing, which later serves as an inlet for the water, the middle of the first piece of tape is wrapped around the tube and securely fastened by back-stitching with strong thread. 

The other four strips are similarly fastened to the tubing at intervals of one inch.

The tubing is now coiled in close fitting circles, and held in place between the folds of tape, which are stitched together between each coil. The last two feet of tubing is

left free to serve as an outlet. When completed the whole coil should measure 9-10 inches in diameter.

If greater stability is desired the tapes between the rubber tubing may be sewed to a piece of canton flannel. 

Equipment for application to the abdomen rubber table cover double blanket footstool or box 7-8 inches high douche pail large enameled pail 

5-ounce funnel glass U tube, or glass connecting tube ice water coil 

14-inch piece of rubber tubing ice sufficient to lower temperature of water to 45° or 

50° F.

Procedure 

Put rubber case on pillow and rubber cover on table. 

Place footstool on table. Set empty pail on floor near head of bed to receive water. 

Envelop a good-sized piece of ice in cheesecloth or gauze and place in an ordinary five-quart douche pail of cold water. Attach a glass connecting tube to the end of the rubber tubing, which in turn is attached to the ice water coil; the latter being in position on the patient. The douche pail should be 7-8 inches higher than the level of the mattress. Place the outlet tube of the coil in a slop jar or pail.

Open the catch which is attached to the rubber tubing leading from the douche pail and see that the water is

APPLICATION OF COLD 

119 flowing into the pail. The top of the douche pail may be kept covered to save the ice. Guard against unnecessary exposure that not more of the patient's body than the coil will cover is left exposed. 

The patient lies in the dorsal position. The upper bedclothing is folded back to the pubes, and the nightgown is doubled up from the waist line. Folded gauze is placed over the abdomen. Cover the chest and shoulders with double blanket and adjust the coil. 

Should there be any trouble with the siphonage apply suction to the outlet tube with a syringe. The water should be regulated to flow slowly. In order to do so, the elevation of the ice water pail should not be more than 7-8 inches above the level of the mattress, when it will take about thirty minutes for one gallon of water to flow through the length of the coil.

This method of applying cold needs constant supervision, as the reservoir needs frequent replenishing with ice and water. In hot weather the pail may be covered and wrapped with paper.

As the pail on the floor fills it may be emptied back into the ice pail. A metal clamp or clothes pin will serve to shut off the water while the pail is being emptied. 

In some cases a surgical cradle may be placed over the body and covered with a blanket, when sufficient space may be left between the edges of the two coverings for the exit of the two extremities of the tubing. 

After care of coils

When the treatment is completed the rubber coils should be drained, washed with soap and water, dried and powdered, then put away in a towel in a tightly covered box.

I 

Metal coils are cared for in the same way with the exception of powdering.

Notes 

Head coils on a restless patient may be held in position by tapes attached to those which encircle the tubing and be tied under the chin.

If one continuous length of rubber tubing cannot be secured for the coil, glass connecting tubes may be used at either end.

In place of an ice cap, moist applications may be used. 

For the head

Brush the patient's hair out of the way.

Wring the compress out of the solution and apply in smooth folds over the forehead.

The two compresses are changed as often as necessary for comfort and relief. 

Note: If cold moist compresses are to bind the head a thin rubber bathing cap may be used to keep the hair dry.

General application 

The same methods may be carried out for other parts of the body, folding the compress to fit the part. 

Eye compresses

Purpose 

Usually ordered for the relief of conjunctivitis.

Equipment dressing towel rubber pillow case eye bowl with perforated plate and ice finger bowl with 2% boric solution triangular-shaped pieces of gauze or absorbent cotton to fit over globe of eye kidney basin or paper bag pair forceps

In case of gonorrheal infection of eye, the following equipment is needed: rubber gloves and apron

A bowl over which a piece of gauze has been stretched will serve for drainage of ice.

Procedure 

Slip on the rubber pillow case. 

Adjust dressing towel about the neck.

Wring eye compresses out of boric solution and spread on the ice as many as the surface will hold. 

Tip patient's head slightly in direction of affected eye to prevent solution running into other eye.

Place iced compress upon the eye and renew every other minute with a fresh one. Discard used ones into a paper bag and burn. 

The nurse carries on the procedure with the use of forceps, one pair being kept clean for applying the compress to the affected eye, and the second pair used for removing the used compress and putting into the paper bag to be burned.

XXII MISCELLANEOUS APPLIANCES PICKED COTTON PADS 

Picked cotton is made up of odds and ends of discarded cotton material, short bandage ends, scraps from the sewing room, small pieces of gauze and worn-out household supplies. This material is placed in an electrical picking machine which reduces it to a fine shredded fluffy mass, resembling cotton waste, which may be converted into surgical material and used for making pads and sponges. These pads when completed may be sterilized and used to reinforce dressings of discharging wounds.

Method of making 

Cut off two-thirds of a yard of 36-inch gauze. Open the gauze and spread on its surface sufficient picked cotton to make a pad the desired size. The cotton is spread in thin layers and close enough to avoid open spaces so that the pad will be of uniform thickness, about ½-inch. Finish by bringing over from both sides the free selvage edges of the gauze, which should overlap firmly in the center. Four inches of gauze is allowed at each end for folding in. Size of pad when completed should be 10 X 14 inches. 123

SHEET WADDING PADS Purpose 

These are used for covering clean abdominal wounds, also for the application of hot antiseptic solutions and disinfectants.

Method of making 

One double sheet of wadding will make three abdominal pads, or four of smaller size. Cut off about 20 inches of gauze, open and place the sheet wadding crosswise on the gauze. Fold the selvage edges of the gauze over each end of the wadding, which should overlap in the middle of the pad. The raw edges of the gauze are tucked in between the folds of the wadding at the sides. 

When completed these pads are rolled in compress cloth and sterilized.

OAKUM PADS 

Oakum is tarred hemp rope which has been shredded.

Purpose 

Oakum pads are used with incontinent patients for the protection of the bed linen.

Method of making Take three or four sheets of newspaper. Fold the edge I inch on all sides. Cover the exposed surface of the newspaper with a thin layer of picked cotton; over that a thin layer of oakum, and over the oakum is distributed evenly another layer of picked cotton. The surface of the pad is covered with one thickness of washed gauze, which is turned in even with the folded

MISCELLANEOUS APPLIANCES 

125 edges of the newspaper and basted on. Before discarding the pad after use, the gauze is ripped off and placed in the soiled gauze pail.

OAKUM RING Purpose 

This ring is used for the relief of pressure on heel or elbow. 

Method of making

Mold sufficient oakum to form a large sausage or roll, about the size of a man's wrist and 12 inches long. 

Wrap the ends of the roll around the tips of the fingers of one hand. Remove from the fingers, place on a firm surface, and mold with both hands into a rounded compact cake. An opening is made in the middle with the fingers or a pair of pointed scissors, wide enough to permit the insertion of a cotton roller 1½ inches wide, which is wound about the ring until it is evenly covered; over this a finger bandage is evenly applied and finished by splicing it and tying the spliced ends together once, then around the border of the heel ring; otherwise the end may be caught under the turns of the bandage. 

RUBBER AIR RING Purpose 

Rubber air rings may be used to support any part of the body, for comfort and removal of pressure. They are generally applied to the sacrum and coccyx, and should be used in all cases when a patient must sit up in bed permanently.

Preparation 

Loosen the air valve and inflate by a tire pump, or place a piece of thin gauze over the valve, apply the lips and blow until the ring is firm to the touch, then screw the valve tight, as it is bound to leak sooner or later. 

Method of covering 

Bandage the air ring with 3-inch cotton roller, omitting the valve; follow with a 3-inch cotton bandage smoothly and evenly applied; finish by tucking the end under a fold of bandage. Lightly sprinkle the ring with talcum powder before applying.

The ring is sometimes placed in a pillow case when the opening is not essential.

Application 

Grasp the inner circle of the ring opposite the valve and slip under the patient with the valve directed forward between the patient's thighs.

A second method is to turn the patient on his side facing the nurse. Place the air ring close behind the hips and roll the patient over on the ring. See that the patient is evenly balanced on the ring. 

CANVAS CHEST SWATHE 

This swathe is made of canvas with selvage edges. It should be 13 inches wide and of sufficient length to bind the chest and meet at the sternum in front. The swathe is held in place by webbing straps and buckles, which are sewn on 4 inches from the ends to allow for compression.

MISCELLANEOUS APPLIANCES 

127 

Purpose 

It is applied when limited motion of the ribs is desired and for the comfort of the patient in cases of pneumonia, pleurisy, intercostal neuralgia, and in fractures of the ribs.

Application 

Powder chest, back and axillary spaces. Protect the skin from contact with the rough canvas by a layer of sheet wadding about the body. Pad axillary spaces. The swathe is next applied evenly and held in place by slipping each strap through the opposite buckle. Gradually tighten every other buckle, from the bottom up, tightening the others going down so that the pressure is firm and even.

For additional security a cotton strap may be pinned in a V to the back of the swathe and each end carried over the patient's shoulders and pinned in front.

The swathe should be unfastened at least every other day, so that the patient's body may be bathed, powdered and the swathe readjusted.

TRIANGULAR SLING Purpose 

The triangular sling is used for comfort and support in injuries of the forearm and septic wounds of the hand.

Method of making 

One square yard of cotton cloth folded in two diagonally should be sufficient for one double sling. Two single slings may be obtained from a square yard by cutting

through the diagonal fold from opposite corners. The double sling is more desirable when special support to the forearm is needed.

Application 

The injured forearm is flexed at the desired angle, with the hand higher than the elbow and its palm inclined toward the patient's chest. One-half of the sling is slipped behind the flexed forearm, its end placed upon the opposite shoulder, and the apex of the sling is adjusted behind the elbow to be supported. The second end is brought over the outside of the forearm and over that shoulder. 

Both ends are tied under tension in a square knot behind the neck, slightly to one side or below the seventh cervical prominence, and pinned firmly to the clothing with a large safety pin. 

The loose ends of the knot are slipped under the sling at the neck. The apex is folded under the elbow and its base shirred evenly on a large safety pin about 3 inches above the elbow. 4-47 

TRIANGULAR SLING APPLIED TO FOREARM 

If the sling is a little too large and projects beyond the patient's fingers, a fold may be turned back a few inches,

MISCELLANEOUS APPLIANCES 

129 as far as the first joint of the little finger. The hand should not be permitted to hang over the edge of the sling at the wrist, since this would interfere with the circulation and cause swelling of the hand. 

CRAVAT OR WRIST SLING 

A cravat sling may be made from a triangular sling or from a large square neck scarf, as a substitute, by folding the square into a triangle. The apex of the triangle is folded towards the center and again upon itself until the desired width is obtained, usually 4 or 5 inches. 

Purpose 

This sling may be applied in fractures or injuries of the upper arm, shoulder or collar bone; it may also be used in connection with a breast binder following amputations of the breast, for support of the arm on the side of the operation.

Application 

Bend the forearm at the desired angle with the palm of the hand towards the patient's body and in the middle. of the sling. Carry the ends of the sling over the shoulders and fasten to one side behind the neck in the same way as the triangular sling. The sling may be secured at the wrist with a safety pin and attached to the clothing if necessary for firm support.

FOUR-TAILED JAW BANDAGE 

A four-tailed bandage can be made from a 5-inch cotton bandage; if greater width is desired cotton cloth may be used. Length, usually 36-38 inches.

Purpose 

Used in injuries of the lower jaw for support and to hold dressings in place. Sometimes applied following the removal of adenoids and tonsils in children, and as a corrective measure against mouth breathing or snoring. 

In post-mortem cases it is used to hold the jaws together. 

Method of making 

Measure and cut the desired length of bandage. Fold end to end, then fold in two lengthwise and slit through to within 2 inches of the center. When at this point a small buttonhole opening is made as a landmark for the chin.

Application 

In order to hold the jaw in position apply the opening to the tip of the patient's chin and carry the two upper tails straight with the line of the jaw to the back of the neck, and tie once. Carry the two lower tails acutely over the angle of the jaw and upward in front of the ears and tie securely in a square knot on the top of the head. Each bandage end on the top of the head is tied to the opposite one below.

In post-mortem cases a soft padding should be introduced between the bandage and the face.

MISCELLANEOUS APPLIANCES 

131 

Purpose THE TOURNIQUET 

A tourniquet is an appliance for the quick compression of an artery in a limb to check bleeding. 

Those most commonly used in hospital practice are the 

Esmarch rubber bandage and the simple rubber tubing.

Many substitutes can be improvised in emergencies and be used with a small, firm pad made from anything at hand, and applied directly above the bleeding artery for additional pressure.

The Esmarch rubber bandage is usually 5 feet long and is applied to an arm or leg in firm overlapping turns and held in place with an adjustable chain and hook. 

The tourniquet of soft rubber tubing is frequently used in place of the Esmarch bandage in surgical amputations and applied above the line of incision as a provisional measure against bleeding.

For application to the thigh the rubber tubing should be three to four feet long and three-fourths to one inch in diameter. For application above the knee or to the arm below the shoulder, two to three feet long, one-fourth to one-half inch in diameter.

Note: Following cases of amputation and other oper ations of the extremities where large blood vessels may be involved, as in septic and sloughing wounds, the rule is that a rubber tubing tourniquet in working order shall be attached to the head of the patient's bed in plain view, in case of an emergency.

Application 

To permit greater compression without injury to the tissues underneath, bind a folded dressing towel firmly

about the limb above the bleeding point. Over the towel apply the rubber tourniquet by grasping it firmly in the middle between the thumb and index finger of each hand,

ARTERIAL COMPRESSION APPLICATION OF A RUB- BER TUBING TOURNIQUET FOR COMPRESSION OF THE BRACHIAL ARTERY 

stretch the tubing to its utmost and bind about the limb from the front backward, at the same time slipping the thumb and index finger along the tubing while applying it, to maintain its tension. When the limb is encircled bring the ends of the tubing forward and tie in a single bowknot and pull tight. The loop of the bowknot should be directed towards the end of the limb. When the tension is released the compression exerted by the tubing will be sufficient to stop the arterial bleeding. Test the artery for pulsation at a point below the tourniquet. 

The dangers attending too tight and too long continued application of a tourniquet are gangrene, thrombosis and nerve paralysis-perhaps involving permanent loss of function of arm or leg. Tourniquet should not be continuously applied for more than one-half hour.

The age of the patient, also, should be considered. Use of the tourniquet in compressing a vein When an opening or puncture of a vein is desired, to obtain blood for Wasserman test, blood culture or other purpose, slight compression is usually sufficient to retard

MISCELLANEOUS APPLIANCES 

133 the venous circulation and thereby distend the superficial veins of the forearm. For this purpose a piece of rubber tubing 15 inches long and 4-inch in diameter is bound about the upper arm once, crossed in front with slight tension, and the two ends of the tubing held in place by tying in a loop, or sometimes fastened with a pair of hemostats. No towel is used under the tubing; and only enough tension is used to engorge the veins at the bend of the elbow. Having the patient close the fist or slapping the skin over the veins helps to make them become prominent.
