METHOD OF TAKING TEMPERATURE, PULSE AND RESPIRATION Equipment TEMPERATURE 

A deep agate or glass tray containing four thermometer glasses, two of which are filled with corrosive sublimate 1-2000, one labeled "No. 1," the other, "No. 2."

The other two glasses containing water, one labeled "before," the other, "after." A pledget of absorbent cotton is placed in the bottom of each glass to prevent breakage of thermometer bulbs. Squares made from paper napkins 4 by 4 inches for wiping thermometers. 

Thermometers for mouth, rectal and special cases. 

Petrolatum for rectal thermometers.

Varieties of thermometers and their uses The silver-bulbed mouth and rectal thermometers are for general use.

Red-bulbed mouth and rectal thermometers are reserved for typhoid patients and kept in 2% formalin solution. Blue-bulbed mouth and rectal thermometers are reserved for all other special patients. In all cases the rectal thermometers are used also for taking axillary temperatures. 

On surgical wards eight or more thermometers are used, divided into two groups, and kept in two glasses of corrosive sublimate labeled, No. I and No. 2. On the medical and children's wards each patient has his thermometer, which is kept in a separate glass. 

Where each patient has his own thermometer one glass 134

PRELIMINARY COURSE 

135 of water is used besides the one with the corrosive sublimate, with the small squares of paper for drying. 

Routine taking of temperatures 

By mouth 

Remove the four thermometers, one at a time, from glass No. I and distribute thus: Dip in glass of water labeled "before" to wash off disinfectant. Wipe with a paper square. Hold it in one hand horizontally almost on a level with the eyes and observe if the thread of mercury is below the scale of 95°; if not, with a sharp inward turn of the wrist the mercury is shaken down, the thermometer being held near its upper end between the thumb and index finger.

Ambulatory patients should be seated before receiving the thermometer.

Place under the patient's tongue, slightly to one side, where it is held in position by the lips. Note the time and remove promptly in three or five minutes, according to the time of registration of the thermometer. The reading is made and noted on the chart. 

The normal temperature is 98.6°-99°F. in women, and 

98°-98.6° in men. 

On removal from patient's mouth wipe, shake down mercury and treat as follows: Wash in glass labeled 

"after." Wipe with a paper square and replace in corrosive sublimate in glass No. 1, to remain during the distribution of thermometers from glass No. 2, which will permit disinfection between each group.

When through with the thermometers they should be washed in warm soapy water, rinsed in clear warm water, the mercury shaken down, and the thermometers

replaced in fresh corrosive sublimate solution. Hot water is to be avoided, as it will cause the mercury to expand and burst the bulb.

Rectal 

For this purpose a thermometer with a special bulbous end is used and kept in its own special glass of solution. 

In taking this temperature it is more convenient to have the patient on one side.

The thermometer is removed from the disinfectant, rinsed, dried, inspected, lubricated, and grasped at its end with the tips of the fingers. It is carefully and slowly rotated into the rectum about one and a half inches. It is held in position by the nurse for three minutes. On removal the lubricant is wiped off, the reading made and noted on the chart. The thermometer is washed in cold water and then in warm soapy water, wiped dry, the mercury shaken down, and the thermometer replaced in the disinfectant.

For accuracy, all children under eight years should have temperature taken by rectum. Because this may excite the child and so increase the rate of pulse and respiration, both should be recorded before the temperature is taken.

The normal rectal temperature in children under eight years may range from 98°-99.8°F. In infants a temperature of 98°-99° should be maintained constantly. Axillary

This method is used if the patient is feeble or irrational, or when it is unsafe to take the temperature in another way.

A rectal thermometer is used because of its greater adaptability, insuring greater accuracy. Dry the arm pit,

TEMPERATURES 

137 shake down the thermometer and introduce the bulb into the armpit. Flex the forearm and place the patient's hand against the opposite shoulder. This position is held for ten minutes, and the nurse should support the elbow. Groin

The same routine is carried out as in taking axillary temperature, using a rectal thermometer. The knee on that side is flexed for ten minutes and the foot crosse over the other leg. The knee may be held in position by the nurse. 

Observations to insure accuracy in taking temperatures 

The thermometer must register correctly.

The mercury is shaken down below 95°. 

By mouth

The patient should not talk or fall asleep before the removal of the thermometer; it must be observed that he is not too feeble, is not a mouth breather, and docs not cough, i.e., that he may be able to hold his lips closed over the thermometer.

Nothing hot or cold is to be taken by mouth within fifteen minutes before taking the temperature.

The patient must be neither hysterical nor irrational. In cases where there is an element of hysteria there is a tendency on the part of the patient to tamper with the thermometer in order to send up the mercury to make an impression on the doctors and nurses. Such schemes are used as removing the thermometer from the mouth and holding it over a hot register, placing it on a hot water bag, dipping it into hot drinks; or friction, as rubbing

the bulb on the blanket. In such cases the mercury registers much higher in proportion to the rate of the pulse and respiration, and the temperature should be taken again with another thermometer in the presence of the nurse.

An irrational patient may bite off the bulb and swallow the mercury and glass unless the nurse quickly removes them from his mouth with her fingers.

By rectum 

For safety only a rectal thermometer is used. 

The rectum should be clear.

The thermometer should always be held by the nurse to prevent breakage in the rectum.

The temperature is not taken within fifteen minutes of an enema. 

By axilla or groin

See that the bulb of the thermometer is enclosed and not projecting outside the armpit or groin.

THE PULSE 

The pulse is the expansion of an artery following the contraction, or systole, of the heart, and is ordinarily taken at the radial artery on the thumb side of the left wrist. For comparison with the left radial it is sometimes taken on the right radial, at the temporal artery in front of the ears, on the facial in front of the angle of the lower jaw, or at the dorsalis pedis in a line with the great toe.

The important observations to be made in studying the pulse are the rate or frequency, rhythm, force, volume, tension, and comparison of radial pulses if the left is difficult to find.

PULSE 

139 The normal rate of the pulse ranges from 72-80 per minute in women; 60-74 in men; 80-100 in children; 

130-120 in infants, the rate lessening as the age increases. 

Method of taking pulse

With the patient's hand resting on his chest the nurse takes the radial pulse by placing the first three finger tips over the artery with moderate pressure. For greater accuracy the rhythm of the pulse should be noted before counting it. The pulse is counted for one whole minute by the second dial of the watch. Lip counting is better than counting to one's self, as one can better concentrate and the mind is not so apt to wander. 

In studying the quality of the pulse one may be guided by the resistance of the artery on expansion, which can be felt against the finger tips on making moderate pressure.

In counting irregular or intermittent pulses it is well to note the number of beats the first half minute, then compare with the number in the last half minute. S D tiseconat 1Second

Systole Diastole 

بر 

TRACING OF A NORMAL PULSE 

Method of taking apex beat 

The apex beat of the heart may be heard and counted by means of a stethoscope, or by pressing one's ear against the left side of the patient's chest. While taking the apex beat it is customary to have the radial pulse taken at the

same time for comparison. One nurse takes the apex beat with the stethoscope placed over the fifth intercostal space, slightly below the left nipple, while the second nurse holds the watch and takes the pulse at the radial. Both are counted a whole minute and recorded. 

See notes on charting apex beat.

METHOD OF TAKING THE BLOOD PRESSURE 

For more accurate knowledge of the tension or pressure exerted by the blood in its circulation through its vessels than can be gained by taking the pulse in the ordinary way, an instrument known as a sphygmomanometer is used, sphygmos being the Greek term for pulse, and manometer the scale which registers its pressure. It is often the nurse's duty to use this instrument in conditions where the observations of the systolic pressure are necessary, also sometimes the diastolic pressure; in such cases as are attended by shock or hemorrhage, where the blood pressure is low or below normal; in cases of fractured skull or any other conditions causing cerebral pressure; in toxemia of pregnancy; and in acute cardiac diseases where the blood pressure may be high. The blood pressure is sometimes taken at two-hourly or four-hourly periods, while the crisis lasts; in other cases it is taken regularly, morning and evening.

The sphygmomanometer consists of (1) a square elastic bag enclosed in a sateen cuff, which is wrapped snugly about the arm above the elbow; (2) an air bulb for inflating the bag; (3) a round dial which registers the blood pressure. Both are attached to the lower edge of the bag by a short length of rubber tubing.

BLOOD PRESSURE 

141 As a rule, the auscultation method is used by placing a stethoscope over the brachial artery, close to the lower edge of the cuff. The air bulb is worked, and the rubber bag inflated until its pressure causes the pulsation of the artery to disappear entirely. Then as the air is slowly let out, the pulsation will reappear again, and become distinct. The first hearing is the systolic pressure, which is observed on the manometer or dial. As the pulsation becomes fuller, then recedes, and before it finally disappears, the last point of hearing is the diastolic pressure, which is also observed on the manometer, and charted as indicated. 

The range of normal blood pressure is 120S 80D 140S 100D 

THE SPHYGMOMANOMETER

at the left the air bulb

RESPIRATION 

Method of taking respiration with pulse

Without removing her fingers from the patient's wrist the nurse allows the back of her hand to rest lightly on the patient's chest, when the respirations are easily counted with each respiratory movement. This method is especially useful when caring for a self-conscious, nervous patient.

As a rule the respirations should be counted a whole minute, especially in case of diseases of the respiratory and circulatory tracts and following operations on the abdominal cavity, when increased rate should be noted. 

In cases when morphine or codein is being administered regularly for a period, decreased rate should be noted and not be permitted to go below 12 without calling medical attention.

In the case of unconscious patients the respirations may be counted by the rise and fall of the chest and abdomen.

In the case of a child, record the pulse and respiration before taking the temperature.

The normal rate of respiration per minute is 18-22 in women; 16-20 in men; 20-30 in children, and 30-40 in infants.

XXIV CHARTING Purpose 

A clinical chart is a bedside record on which are noted daily, four-hourly or hourly statistics whereby the doctor is kept informed of the patient's condition. 

In hospitals such charts are kept in detailed form and bound with the patient's history and other data pertaining to his case, for future reference. Different hospitals use different outlines for their own convenience. Technic The perfection of charting depends on one's natural inclination toward neatness in detail and on good penmanship. A knowledge of drawing is helpful. 

In order to keep a clearly outlined chart, use a fine pen and ink free from sediment.

The dots should be spherical, not too large, of uniform size, with connecting lines light and straight. The dot should be kept free from the perpendicular line of each square. This helps to make the chart clear. 

The nurse is not permitted to make repairs and erasures. These are attended to by the librarian. 

Description of a typical chart 

At the head of the chart is the patient's name, date of admission, ward, etc. Below, the body of the chart is ruled off in small squares. 143

The whole area is horizontally divided by heavier lines into four spaces, in which are recorded, first, number of dejections in a day, the days of the month, and, if a fourhourly chart, the hours at which temperature should be taken; second, temperature (in the first space are printed the letters M and E, morning and evening, indicating when the last temperature was taken, if a daily chart); third, pulse; fourth, respiration.

On the left-hand margin are sets of figures which designate the rate of the temperature, pulse or respiration recorded in the corresponding square, the fluid intake and output, and the blood pressure, i.e., diastole and systole at the extreme left.

Abbreviations and methods of recording 

Temperature, pulse, respiration. Solid black dots and lines are used in proper spaces for temperature, pulse and respiration. 

Solid red dots and lines indicate rectal temperature. 

A red "ax" over solid black dots signifies axillary temperature. Solid purple dots and lines signify the apex beat.

Blood pressure. A hollow red sphere with connecting lines carried through its center represents systolic pressure. A hollow red sphere with a dot in the middle repre.. sents diastolic pressure.

CHARTING 

145 White count is occasionally charted with red spheres and connecting double lines, dumb-bell fashion. 

Calories. Solid green dots with connecting lines record total caloric intake in 24 hours. 

Fluid intake. Green circles with connecting lines record total fluid intake in ounces in 24 hours. 

Urine. Solid red dots with connecting lines represent total output of urine, in ounces, in 24 hours. When night and day urine are measured separately there are two rows of red dots, the upper being the total amount and the lower being the night amount; the marginal figures in the respiration and pulse spaces being used to count the ounces.

Dejecta are noted by straight lines in black ink, in the first row of squares corresponding to date. Perpendicular lines represent dejecta during the day; horizontal, those at night. If a cleansing enema has been given it is recorded in red, in temperature space of 103°, i.e., suds enema or M. G. & W., etc., according to the kind given. 

Transfers and discharges. All transfers from one ward to another are written perpendicularly in red ink in the pulse division under the date of transfer.

All discharges of patients from the hospital are written in black ink in pulse division under the proper date.

Statement should be made as to where patient went, to 

O. P. D., to L. M. D., or to another hospital, giving name.

The only conditions now noted at time of discharge are "Against advice" and "To Allen Street House," the mortuary, if a discharge after death. The hour and minutes of the time of death are also stated. 

Surgical cases. Following an operation the word "operation" is written perpendicularly in the respiration

division under the proper date in red ink. Beginning with the day after operation the number of each day is placed in the consecutive spaces until discharge. This figure is a guide to the surgeon for the time of removal of stitches and other observations.

Weight. The patient's weekly weight may be written on the medical chart in the last space of the respiration division.

Special notes. Many other incidents are noted, as in case of Widal's reaction in typhoid fever; if a hemorrhage or sputa, the amount in ounces; the injection of salvarsan, insulin, etc.; any special rise of temperature, as that following a chill, or a drop, as that related to the crisis of pneumonia; transfusion and fluids from paracentesis. 

Bedside notes 

In addition to clinical charts, nurses may be expected to keep bedside notes in which are recorded clearly written facts in bulletin fashion, as follows: 

Nourishment, amount taken; whether solid or liquid; estimated quantity each time.

Medicine and treatments, if on forced fluids, the quantity taken each time; if enemata, when given, results obtained, if given for distention, note if flatus has been expelled.

If food or medicine has not been taken, the reason for failure to carry out treatment should be noted, i.e., if patient was asleep at the hour, or refused, or could not be persuaded to take nourishment or medicine. 

Vomiting, all other than that following an anesthetic should be described if unusual in character.

CHARTING 

147 Elimination of wastes, urine measured each time it is passed and dejecta noted. 

Sleep, whether in naps or consecutive hours; its nature, whether restless or otherwise; if wakeful, the cause. 

Pain, its location, duration and nature; whether continuous or intermittent.

Change in condition. Every change should be noted; if a chill, time and duration, temperature by rectum during chill and after, if vomiting during chill. 

Mental cases, whether depressed, suicidal, or exhilarated. Hallucinations and their nature. For diagnostic purposes a verbatim report of what the patient says is necessary.

Emergencies. In cases of accident, escape of a delirious patient, or sudden death, a nurse should be prepared to give an accurate and full report, to the entire satisfaction of those to whom she is responsible.

XXV 

ENEMATA Purpose 

An enema is a quantity of fluid introduced into the large intestine through the rectum, for local or systemic treatment. There are different kinds, prepared in various ways to treat the conditions for which they may be ordered. 

Those most commonly given are for purgative purposes, for relief of gas and distention, for stimulation of the circulation, for increase of body fluids, for medication, and occasionally for nutritive purposes. 

Enemata are usually given with the patient in the lateral or dorsal position, sometimes in the knee-chest position. To get results it is sometimes advisable to remove the pillows and place one under the patient's hips.

The routine of administration is about the same in most cases. The purgative or suds enema is taken as an example of procedure.

Preparation of a purgative or suds enema Into an agate pitcher measure two tablespoons soft soap or soap jelly and a little hot water. Mix thoroughly, then add sufficient water to make one quart and mix again. Skim off soap bubbles. Temperature should be 

102° F. 148

ENEMATA 

149 The pitcher of soapy solution is placed in basin and covered with hopper towel. Connect rectal tube and tubing. Fit a folded piece of toilet paper over the index 

METHOD OF CARRYING EQUIPMENT FOR AN ENEMA 

finger, remove, invert and into it pour a little lubricant. 

Wrap it about the tip of the rectal tube. Place in basin with pitcher and carry all equipment to bedside at one time.

Equipment gray blanket half sheet and rubber protector hopper towel washed gauze agate pitcher enameled basin rectal lubricant rectal tube, glass connecting tube, tubing and funnel bath thermometer bedpan (warmed)

Preparation at bedside If a new patient, tell him what is to be done. 

Place bedpan under bed and arrange remainder of equipment in order on the table. 

If on an open ward, screen bed thoroughly. 

 in lateral position Turn upper bedclothing back to thighs. Place doubled gray blanket over patient. Move patient to opposite side of bed. Arrange half sheet and rubber on this side of bed. 

Draw patient onto half sheet and turn him over in the semi-prone position, with hips even with the edge of bed, and with the upper knee strongly flexed. Slip hopper towel under the nightgown and over buttocks and turn back nightgown.

Procedure 

Place basin and pitcher on the bed behind the patient's knees. Hold the funnel by slipping the index finger

ENEMATA 

151 through its handle and with the thumb hold the rectal tube pinched against the outside of the handle with its tip above the edge of the funnel.

With the other hand pour solution into tubing until it rises into the funnel. In order to expel the air a small quantity of solution is allowed to pass out of end of tube back into the pitcher.

The tubing is sharply bent upon itself at the tip of the funnel and the remaining solution in the funnel is poured out to avoid spilling.

The rectal tube is taken in one hand while funnel is held between thumb and finger of the other hand, and with remaining fingers or back of the hand the patient's buttock is slightly elevated while the tube is slowly introduced into the rectum, about four or five inches, or at least within the internal anal sphincter. 

Before the solution is permitted to run out of the tubing the funnel is filled, then the pressure on the tubing under the thumb is gradually released to permit the escape of the solution into the rectum. The enema is given slowly by regulating the pressure with the fingers on the tubing, at the same time gradually elevating the funnel 6 inches above the hips. Before the solution is allowed to run out completely the funnel is refilled at regular intervals to prevent admission of air. 

On completion of the operation, a valve is again made by bending tubing sharply over tip of funnel to check entrance of air. The tube is slowly withdrawn, and pressure may be made with the towel against the anus to prevent contents being expelled. The nurse withdraws her hand, and the patient is dried with hopper towel and turned on back, then moved to center of bed on half sheet

and rubber. As protection to the edges of the upper bedclothing the lower ends of the half sheet and rubber are drawn up between the patient's thighs and folded back upon the pubes. The gray blanket covering the patient is adjusted and the patient is instructed to draw up the knees while the nurse with one hand supports the sacrum, and with the other introduces the bedpan, slightly sideways, in such a manner that the wedge-shaped end shall first come in contact with back of the nurse's supporting hand. Nurse now withdraws her hand and sees that the patient is left as comfortable as possible. All equipment is then carried out, except washed gauze. 

Note: In order to get good results the patient should be advised to retain enema as long as possible. 

Care of utensils

While the patient is on the bedpan the utensils are cared for as follows: 

Separate rectal tube from glass connecting tube, wipe with toilet paper and hold opening under cold running water. Then wash with warm soapy water in a basin.

Wrap tube loosely in gauze, place in boiling water and boil for three minutes. Dry and put away. 

Cleanse funnel and attached tubing, dry and hang up. 

Wash and dry pitcher and put away. 

After care

Return to bedside with a basin of warm soapy water. 

Support patient's back with one hand and with the other remove the bedpan carefully.

ENEMATA 

153 Glance hastily at contents, for results, and quickly cover.

Local bathing is done, gray blanket and half sheet and rubber are removed, and patient left comfortable. 

Record fecal and gas results on chart. If none, report to nurse in charge.

The remainder of equipment is carried out to lavatory. 

Before emptying bedpan notice nature of fecal result, i.e., the consistency, whether liquid, semi-solid, formed, or in hardened, round lumps (scybala).

The presence of anything unusual should be observed, such as blood, pus, intestinal parasites, etc. Such stools should be saved and reported. If ordered to be sent to the pathological laboratory, the contents of the bedpan should be put in a special jar and be tagged with ward, patient's name and date.

With children the same observations are made regarding fecal discharges. The nurse should note also if foul smelling, green in color, presence of curds, mucus, undigested food, etc. If a child has swallowed a foreign. body, stools should be carefully investigated.

If anything unusual in infant's stools, diapers should be saved in a closely covered utensil. 

Procedure with patient in the dorsal position Turn back blanket and spread as far as the knees, and place a folded blanket over the patient's body. Slip the half sheet and rubber under the hips. Follow with the bedpan on which the patient lies with knees flexed and thighs separated. Protect the edge of the folded back bedclothing with a hopper towel, or lower ends of half sheet and rubber.

Locate the anus with the tip of the second finger of one hand and with the other hand introduce the rectal tube and give the enema in the usual way. 

Retained enema

If several enemata have been given to a patient in the course of a day without fecal result, note whether the amount of fluid passed off has been nearly equal to that which has been given. If not, report. If the enema has not been absorbed the patient will complain of great discomfort from fluid distention. The bowel may be drained of the retained fluid as follows:

Turn the patient on his side and insert rectal tube six inches or more. Attach funnel. Place bedpan on floor or chair and invert funnel over pan in order to drain colon. 

Fecal impaction

This is the lodgment of a hard fecal mass in the rectum or above This condition is often overlooked by an unobserving nurse, and is the cause of much suffering to the patient. He usually complains of rectal distress and may have passages of watery stools. Sometimes the condition is accidentally discovered by obstruction of the rectal tube while attempting to give an enema. 

A digital rectal examination is usually made by using a lubricated finger cot or soapy index finger. If unable to locate impaction by this means an oil enema will probably be ordered, followed later with a suds enema. 

If there is an impaction in the rectum, mechanical removal may be necessary.

Turpentine enema

In using turpentine in enemata, it should be thoroughly mixed with the second ingredient, as it is highly irritating. 

The anal region should be washed with soap and water following the enema.

Method I 

turpentine, two drams yolk of one egg

Method II

Purgative enema for a child 

Into a pitcher measure one tablespoon of ivory soap. jelly, one pint of water at a temperature 100° F. Use a No. 20 American rectal tube. Give to the patient when in a dorsal position on a small sized bedpan. 

If for an infant, the quantity of solution may be anywhere from six to eight ounces, and should be given with a soft-tipped Davol ear syringe. A kidney basin is used instead of a bedpan. 

The use of soft soap should be avoided in the case of children because of its irritating qualities. 

In place of an enema glycerine or soap suppositories are sometimes ordered.

ENEMATA 

157 

LUBRICATING ENEMATA 

Oil enema

Oil enemata are given to soften fecal impaction in extreme constipation and to insure evacuation without straining, following restricted action of the bowels after rectal operations or perineorrhaphies.

Pour six ounces cottonseed oil into a pint graduate. 

Place it in a pitcher of hot water and let remain until temperature of oil is raised to 100° F. Test with a dairy thermometer.

Place rectal tube and funnel in a basin of hot water and carry to bedside. This enema may be ordered given to the patient in the knee-chest position or in the usual way.

Good results have been obtained by giving at bedtime to be retained until the following morning, when a suds enema is given. 

Olive or other vegetable oil may be substituted for cottonseed oil.

Preparation of potato gruel Two teaspoonfuls of potato flour to one quart of water.

Preparation of barium sulphate 

Mix barium sulphate in agate pitcher with sufficient water to form a consistency of soft lumpless paste. It is important that this process of mixing should be thoroughly done to avoid obstruction of the rectal tube. Add one quart of warm (100° F.) potato gruel slowly and stir. 

Administration 

Attach rubber tubing to irrigating can or irrigator and rectal tube. Pour the cup of lukewarm water into the irrigator to expel the air from the tubing, then empty the contents of the agate pitcher into the irrigator. Pinch the tube and quickly suspend about four feet above the patient's body. Lubricate and introduce, if the metal rectal tube, its full length into the rectum. Release the pressure on the rubber tubing and allow the enema to flow. If the patient complains of discomfort, which may be from a gas bubble, permit him to change his position slowly for relief.

XXVI 

VAGINAL DOUCHE 

A douche is a flow of water or solution usually directed from outside into a cavity of an organ, as the vaginal canal.

Purpose 

It is used as an agent for cleansing and disinfection, for applying heat to inflamed parts, and for astringent action.

Equipment gray blanket half sheet and rubber hopper towel washed gauze agate basin douche pail douche pan douche nozzle (glass) solution as ordered

In case of perineal operation 

Add the following to the regular equipment: two packages sterile gauze sterile pad

T bandage rubber or glass catheter No. 9, in place of glass douche nozzle 160

VAGINAL DOUCHE 

161 

Points to be observed in giving douche

The nurse's hands should be thoroughly scrubbed in soap and water both before and after this procedure. 

In giving any vaginal douche the nurse is warned. against touching her face with her hands. In case of getting any discharge into her eyes she should report for immediate treatment.

In using mercurial tablets, in making up the solution see that they are thoroughly dissolved before giving the douche.

If there is any difficulty in introducing the douche nozzle into the vaginal canal, do not use force; suspect the presence of a tampon or sponge, or malformation of the canal.

Following the operation of perineorrhaphy, to avoid straining the stitches a sterile rubber or glass urinary catheter should be used unless otherwise specified. After the douche is given see that the stitches are perfectly dry and place a sterile folded gauze sponge under them on each side to protect from moisture and prevent irritation of opposing surfaces of the skin. Over all a sterile pad should be securely fastened with a T binder.

Preparation of douche 

Place the douche pail on the bridge of the douche pan and clamp the tubing. Carefully estimate, according to rule, the strength of the solution ordered, measure that and the amount of water needed, and test the mixture with a bath thermometer until the desired temperature is reached. 

Remove the glass douche nozzle by its projecting end from the jar of solution and inspect for defects. Do not

handle below that end. Attach to rubber tubing and suspend in the pail; bend the handle of the pail over the tubing to hold it in place.

Put the washed gauze and sterile supplies, when needed, in the agate basin and place over the douche pail. 

Cover all with a clean hopper towel.

The folded blanket, half sheet and rubber are tucked under the nurse's arm, which leaves both hands free to carry utensils to the bedside.

Procedure 

With patient in dorsal position turn up nightgown to waist line.

Turn down upper bedclothing, except sheet, in two folds at foot of bed. 

Turn back sheet to waist line.

Place folded gray blanket across upper body. 

Insert half sheet, and rubber protector under the patient. 

Remove hopper towel and place douche pail in position 

2 or 3 feet above patient.

Pad the douche pan with folded towel. Instruct patient to flex knees and raise herself with feet flat on bed. Nurse supports sacrum with one hand and slips douche pan under with other. The patient's hips should be brought forward over the edge of the bridge of the douche pan to prevent the solution running back into the bed. 

Remove one pillow and place on foot of bed.

Tuck in blanket over shoulder on each side. 

Arrange upper sheet to form a V in the middle. The ends of the V are caught on each side of the patient, leaving room for the introduction of nurse's hand.

VAGINAL DOUCHE 

163 

Grasp douche nozzle at its highest point. (See again that it is perfect.) Open the clamp and direct solution into pan to expel air and to equalize temperature of solution in tubing. Pinch clamp. 

Separate vulva with washed gauze. Wipe, if any evident discharge. Carefully introduce douche nozzle downward and backward in vaginal canal. Open clamp. 

If there is any discharge, gently rotate nozzle to aid escape. 

Clamp tubing before water is quite out of douche pail. 

Remove nozzle with gauze, and place in basin. Remove douche pan. Replace pillow under patient's head. Dry patient with washed gauze and place gauze in basin. 

Remove half sheet and rubber. Draw up sheet and remove gray blanket. Pull down nightgown, make patient comfortable and adjust upper bedclothing. 

Remove utensils, cover with hopper towel, and carry all equipment in one trip to lavatory.

Observations 

Note if any discharge in douche pan. Note color. If pus, it will sink to bottom. If excess of mucus, white shreds will be noted floating in the solution. 

After care of equipment 

Empty douche pan into hopper. If no sterilizer, wash in soapsuds, scald inside and out, disinfect, and dry. 

Wash and scald douche pail, dry tubing. Wash nozzle in cold water, then in hot soap suds. Wrap loosely in gauze, place in tepid water, and boil five minutes. Remove and place in jar of boric solution 1-25. Dispose of gauze in regular pail.

XXVII 

PRECAUTION VAGINAL DOUCHE 

The term "special precaution," or "g. c. precaution," here implies that the patient has an acute local gonorrheal infection of the pelvic organs, a venereal disease characterized by a yellowish vaginal discharge. This condition calls for the exercise by the nurse of certain rules for her own protection and for the prevention of the spread of the disease to other patients. 

When giving this douche the nurse must remain at the patient's bedside until the treatment is entirely completed. She cannot attend to other patients until her hands are thoroughly scrubbed and disinfected at the end of the operation.

The patient should not be permitted to assist in removing the pad or T binder.

The nurse must keep her hands away from her face and eyes during this procedure, and should avoid bending too closely over the field of operation while giving the douche.

If by accident she should get any discharge into her eyes, she should report at once for treatment.

Gonorrheal vaginitis in children is highly communicable and calls for strict precautions on the part of the nurse. All vaginal discharges should be reported and diapers kept apart in a special covered utensil. Such cases are isolated immediately. For safety vaginal cultures are taken on admission to the children's ward to prevent the spread of the disease. 164

PRECAUTION VAGINAL DOUCHE 

165

Douche 

Run some of the solution into the douche pan to equalize temperature and to expel air. 

Separate vulva with gauze. Introduce nozzle in usual way. In case of profuse discharge the nozzle should be rotated in the vaginal tract while the solution is running, to aid in thorough cleansing. 

Before the solution is quite run out, clamp the tubing. 

Remove nozzle, detachable tubing and glass connecting tube, all together, and place in basin.

Wrap in gauze the end of tubing attached to pail and catch under handle of pail.

Loosen sheet and blanket on each side of patient. 

Dry patient with gauze. Remove douche pan. Apply clean pad and adjust T binder.

Slip out half sheet and rubber. Fold upper side in and replace in special half sheet. 

Remove gloves and place in basin. Replace pillow. Pin 

T binder and adjust nightgown.

Remove blanket, fold and place with half sheet and rubber protectors. Bundle in special half sheet and tag. 

Remove apron by slipping it off at the shoulders. Then

PRECAUTION VAGINAL DOUCHE 

167 grasping the edges of the cuffs, pull both sleeves outside in. Fold the clean sides of apron together and place in pillow case.

Leave bedside in order. Carry remaining equipment to lavatory.

After care of special equipment 

Empty douche pan into hopper. If no sterilizer, wash in soapsuds, scald inside and out, dry, tag, and keep apart.

Wash and scald douche pail, disinfect and dry tubing. 

In the special basin wash nozzle and detachable rubber tubing in cold water and then in hot soapsuds. Wrap loosely in gauze, place in tepid water, and boil five minutes. 

Remove and place in special jar in corrosive sublimate I-2000. 

The soiled pad is placed in a paper bag to be burned, and gauze which has been used in the operation is placed in regular gauze pail.

Basin should be washed and sterilized, and kept for this special purpose.

Wash rubber gloves inside and out in cold water, then in hot suds. Wrap in gauze and boil ten minutes. Dry and powder on both sides. Match and wrap in clean towel.

Personal precautions 

Scrub and disinfect hands in special basin. Empty contents into hopper and sterilize the basin.

XXVIII 

CATHETERIZATION Purpose 

To empty the urinary bladder in retention when other means have failed, or before or following operations on genito-urinary organs. 

To obtain a sterile specimen of urine. 

For a renal function test.

Equipment dressing tray on which are four packages sterile picked cotton 

2 kidney basins round basin for soiled sponges 

2 glass finger bowls, one with warm, soapy solution, the other with boric solution 2% 

2 sterile rubber catheters, size No. 7 or No. 8 Am., in a sterile towel bottle of sterile catheter lubricant large sheet half sheet and rubber protector gray blanket 

Procedure 

 and materials 

Dorsal position. 

Turn nightgown back out of the way.

Turn down upper bedclothing slightly below waist line. 168

CATHETERIZATION 

169 Fold gray blanket in two, place over patient's chest, and turn up above pubes. 

Slip half sheet and rubber under the patient. Tuck gray blanket on both sides without confining the patient. 

The draping of the patient is done with the large sheet put on cornerwise.¹ The upper corner is first folded under itself and the sheet is wrapped around the patient's waist to hold it in place. The remainder of the sheet is used to turn down the bedclothing in two folds at the foot of the bed. The outer corners are used to envelop each leg and foot and the lower corner is arranged to hang loosely in a point between the patient's thighs.

Place the sterile towel containing catheters on foot of bed, open carefully, avoiding unsterile contact. Turn out two packages of picked cotton on sterile towel, pour a little lubricant on one package and cover over. Open the two remaining packages, one being dropped into soapy solution and the other into boric solution. 

Place the kidney basin in the space between the patient's knees. Move the bedside table with tray to the side of bed near the foot. Separate picked cotton in soapy solution into four sponges and use to wash the vulva, the last two wrung out tight to dry the patient. 

The nurse's "scrub-up"

Roll sleeves above elbows securely, an ordinary elastic band serving to hold them. From the elbow down, with the nail brush systematically scrub each forearm and hand with quick and vigorous action, two minutes by a watch or clock being allowed for each arm and hand. 

In scrubbing, encircle the forearm from the elbow, working down to wrists, back of hand, between and 

1 Laparotomy stockings may be used in place of a sheet.

around each finger, finishing with palm and finger nails, on which the most time is spent. 

Clean the nails with orange wood stick and scrub again, dipping the brush frequently in the water during the process.

Running water should be used when there is a mixer; if not, a basin of fresh water should be used for each hand and arm. The water should be as hot as can be borne.

Do not go over surface already scrubbed unless contaminated. To remove soap suds wash hands in clear hot water for one minute. Finally, manipulate hands in hot bichloride solution 1-2000 for two minutes, using not less than a quart of solution.

The fingers of one hand should be clasped within the other, and held away from the nurse's person, avoiding contact with surroundings, also, until the bedside is reached.

Catheterization 

Separate picked cotton which is in the boric solution into six sponges.

Turn back point of the draping sheet with little finger of left hand, which is not again brought in contact with clean surfaces. 

Separate vulva with sponge held between thumb and first finger of left hand. The sponge is held there until the operation is over. 

Between thumb and fingers of right hand a boric sponge is held to cleanse the surrounding parts of the urinary meatus, situated between the labia minora at the vestibule of the vagina, wiping gently towards the vaginal

CATHETERIZATION 

171 orifice. Four sponges are used for this purpose, a fresh one for each cleansing stroke. Another sponge is placed over the vaginal orifice. 

With right elbow push kidney basin into position between thighs to receive urine. Dip the right hand into the boric solution, then pick up the catheter near its outlet, coil over back of hand, and hold between thumb and finger, about an inch below its eye. Examine eye to see that it is intact, and rotate in lubricant on sterile cotton. 

Carefully introduce catheter slightly downward and forward about 2 inches into the urinary meatus, but avoiding the danger of getting it into the vaginal canal. If the catheter comes in contact with any other part than the point of insertion, i.e., the urinary meatus, it should be laid aside and the second catheter should be used.

To make sure that the bladder is completely emptied, the catheter is slightly pushed in, then slightly withdrawn before final removal. When the bladder is emptied, place kidney basin on tray. Double up end of catheter and with the other hand steady it at point of insertion, pinch it and withdraw slowly. Place it in round basin. Dry patient and cover with sheet. 

Remove gray blanket, half sheet and rubber. Bed covering and draping sheet are drawn up together and the latter removed. Pull down nightgown and make patient comfortable. 

After care of materials Carry all equipment to lavatory at once. Measure urine; if not to be saved, dispose of it at once. Fold sheet and half sheet and save for the same patient. If clean, the sponge covers are saved.

The catheters are held under cold running water, then washed in hot soap suds, wrapped in gauze, placed in boiling water, and sterilized for five minutes. The water is drained off, the enveloping gauze loosened with sterile forceps, and the catheters turned out upon a sterile dressing towel. Envelop and secure with a clothes pin. 

The remainder of the utensils are washed in suds, dried and put away in their respective places. The soiled picked cotton sponges are not saved, but are placed in the bag to be burned. 

HOW TO OBTAIN A STERILE CATHETER SPECIMEN 

When a sterile specimen is desired it should be discharged from the bladder directly through a sterile catheter into a sterile receptacle, usually a glass test tube, so that nothing outside of the bladder may get into the specimen to contaminate it, and interfere with the true. 

analysis. 

Procedure 

The same procedures and caution are carried out as in an ordinary catheterization. Besides the usual equipment, one or two sterile test tubes are added, depending upon the amount needed for a specimen. The test tubes are labeled with the patient's name, ward, service, and date, also "sterile specimen," and placed upright in a small retainer close at hand.

Place the kidney basin in position, insert the catheter, holding its outer end coiled over the back of the hand to avoid unsterile contact.

Pinch the middle of the catheter and hold the test tube

CATHETERIZATION 

173 in the same hand, until the stopper has been removed with the other hand, to which the tube is exchanged and held, with the stopper. The outer end of the catheter is directed into the test tube without touching its edges, and the pressure upon the catheter with the fingers is sufficiently relaxed to permit the urine to be discharged slowly into the test tube. When nearly filled, the catheter is pinched again, carefully withdrawing the full test tube and again changing it to the other hand, when it is stoppered and replaced in the retainer. If a second test tube is used, the same procedure is repeated. 

Finally the end of the catheter is placed in the kidney basin, into which the bladder may complete emptying itself. The patient is cared for and the specimen dispatched to the laboratory for examination. 

METHODS OF OBTAINING A STERILE SPECIMEN FROM BABIES 

Besides the usual efforts to obtain a specimen, these are sometimes used. 

From a male baby

Method No. 1. Use the index finger of a discarded rubber glove and snip off the finger tip. A rubber finger cot may be used in place of the glove finger. Label a glass test tube in the usual way and insert it into the glove finger as far as its rim. Bind the open tip of the finger about the rim of the test tube with narrow adhesive tape. 

Gently sponge the urinary meatus with a 2% warm boric solution. Draw the rubber finger tip with test tube attached over the penis and hold in place with narrow

adhesive tape. With a folded towel bolster the test tube on the side of a kidney basin, which will also serve to catch the residual urine. When the specimen has been obtained (usually at once) carefully remove adhesive tape from the penis, and glove finger from test tube. Plug test tube with sterile absorbent cotton and place upright in a retainer.

Method No. 2. Cut a 2½-inch square of adhesive plaster. Make an opening in the middle of this square large enough to permit the introduction of a test tube, which is held in place with a piece of narrow adhesive tape. The adhesive side of the plaster should be uppermost, as by it the test tube is secured about the penis. 

From a baby girl

Method No. 1. Tear off four narrow strips of adhesive tape 3 or 4 inches long, and two-thirds of an inch wide. 

Cut off the thumb and base, or broader part of a discarded rubber glove and snip off the tip of the thumb.

Introduce the rim of the test tube through the tip of the thumb and hold in place with adhesive tape. 

Separate the labia majora and gently sponge the urinary meatus with warm 2% boric solution. Dry the vulva and surrounding parts thoroughly with a sterile sponge, especially the perineum below, so that the adhesive tape will stick. 

Application of the test tube 

Apply the broad base of the rubber thumb over the patient's vulva and hold in place with a strip of adhesive

CATHETERIZATION 

175 tape applied horizontally. The narrow end of the base covers the perineum and is held in place with adhesive tape, otherwise a leakage at that point will cause a loss of the specimen. The two remaining strips are applied to each side of base. The rest of the operation is carried on as in the case of the boy baby, using the kidney basin to support the test tube and to receive the residual urine. 

Method No. 2. Use a bird's glass seed dish, the opening of which is placed over the baby's vulva. The base of the dish should rest closely against the perineum and is held in place with adhesive strips and with a firmly applied diaper.

A common resource to obtain a specimen is to support the infant or child, if not too restless, with two small pillows, one supporting the body, the other the legs, between which may be placed a small slipper bedpan. 

Greater success is assured if this operation is done immediately after a feeding.

CATHETERIZATION IN ABNORMAL URINARY CONDITIONS Urinary incontinence 

Urinary incontinence is an involuntary discharge of urine from the bladder. There is evidence of leakage, yet the bladder is never entirely emptied. This condition is sometimes met in caring for a delirious, unconscious, paralyzed, or mental patient; or in the aged is due to loss of muscular tone of bladder walls.

Retention of urine being the cause in most cases, these patients are usually catheterized at regular intervals of 8 to 10 hours. Some of the more direct causes are growths or stone in the bladder, causing pain and irrita-

tion; and interference with nervous control, following operations on the genito-urinary tract and in spinal fractures with injury to the cord, causing paralysis of the centers which control the bladder.

The latter group are usually kept in a plaster shell with a bi-valve opening, and placed on a Bradford frame, which is elevated sufficiently to introduce a bedpan under its open space, or on an air mattress. The skin about the sacral region should be kept clean and dry by frequent gentle washings of warm, soapy water, and patted dry with a soft towel, followed by inunctions of castor oil mixed to a consistency of molasses, with stearate of zinc powder or with aristol powder, or generous application of melted mutton tallow to make the skin waterproof. 

A clean pad should be put under the sacrum to absorb moisture. In ordinary cases where the patient can be moved easily, the patient's back may be elevated on a large air ring to prevent contact with moisture or an oakum pad may be placed on a piece of rubbing sheeting, and slipped under to absorb leakage. The patient's thighs should be supported by a rubber-cased pillow, and a small pillow should be placed under the hollow of the patient's back for greater comfort, if the body is raised from the bed.

Urinary suppression 

This condition, in which the kidneys fail to function, is associated with acute nephritis, and may follow operation. 

Catheterization is done to determine between suspected suppression and a case of retention. This is decided by the amount of urine obtained. If not more than 2 or 3 drams, immediate measures are taken to keep the patient

PRELIMINARY COURSE 

177 warm, and fluids forced until further directions are received.

Urinary retention 

Urinary retention is a condition in which a patient is unable to empty the bladder normally. Some of the causes are: inactivity of the kidneys, due to irritation following ether anæsthesia; lack of sufficient urine in the bladder to act as a stimulant on its walls; ether paralysis of the urethral sphincters, or spasm; inhibition due to pain following operations on the genito-urinary tract and abdomen; also in conditions of paralysis, mental apathy, or feebleness.

This condition may be found also in newly admitted patients, due to the recumbent position in bed. Foreign bodies in the urethra may cause it. On one known occasion, retention was due to the presence of a renal calculus in the urethral canal. 

Signs of retention, as a rule, should be readily recognized (1) by the number of hours since the patient last voided; (2) by the complaint of increasing discomfort and pain, with a sense of fullness of the bladder; (3) by the futile efforts of the patient to empty the bladder. 

These efforts should be limited to one or two trials, and not more, because of the painful exhaustion attending the condition.

The attitude of the nurse should be one of patience and quiet reassurance, especially in the case of a nervous and apprehensive patient. 

If possible the operation of catheterization is avoided and in preference other means are tried for a reasonable length of time. If the patient can bear it, such means as

are here noted have been tried with varied success. Moist heat is more effective than dry heat, and the danger of burning the patient is lessened as stupes are handled. 

Protect the bed and apply flannel stupes, wrung out of hot water, over the pubic regions. 

Place the patient on a warm douche pan or bedpan and direct warm irrigations over the vulva, using a douche bag or a pitcher. A warm enema is often effective. 

Placing the patient on a bedpan containing a little hot water, to which has been added a few drops of oil of turpentine, peppermint, or aromatic spirits of ammonia, is often helpful. When using water in this way the amount should be measured so that, if the patient should void, the amount of water used may be subtracted from the whole, which will give an estimate of the quantity of urine passed.

Results have been obtained reflexly by pouring warm or cool water over the patient's wrist while held over a basin.

There is also the old method of suggestion, by running water from a nearby faucet into a tub or sink, or pouring water at a height from one pitcher into another. 

If two or more of these schemes fail, catheterization is resorted to, and the urine saved and measured. The patient may be placed on a régime of forced fluids, in which case a fluid chart is kept until normal urinary conditions are restored.

Over-distention 

Over-distention is a condition in which there usually is complete retention of urine and the bladder has exceeded its normal capacity, and its walls fail to contract.

PRELIMINARY COURSE 

179 This may be observed by a tumor-like elevation in the region of the pelvis between the umbilicus and the symphysis pubes. The removal of 4 to 8 ounces of urine by hourly catheterization may be ordered until evacuation of the bladder is accomplished. To avoid instrumentation in relieving an over-distended bladder, instead of introducing the catheter every hour, it is left in position and clamped and released at the end of each hour or two until the bladder is emptied, when the catheter is finally removed. The danger of complete removal of urine in an over-distended bladder is its effect upon the circulatory system, shock possibly being caused by the quickly lowered abdominal pressure on the removal of the urine, which has been making back pressure on the kidneys. This condition is seldom met in hospital care of patients, except in newly admitted bed patients where it has been overlooked. 

There is also danger of hemorrhage from the bladder walls when distention is rapidly relieved. 

METHOD OF PREPARING AN ORDINARY SPECIMEN FOR URINALYSIS 

Label a perfectly clean specimen bottle with the patient's full name, ward, service, and date. Sometimes it is necessary to add the time that the specimen was passed and whether it is a single specimen or part of the 

24-hour amount.

To prevent decomposition of the urine from exposure to dust and air the mouth of the bottle should be covered. Paper held in place with an elastic band will serve.

The same rule holds for an empty bottle standing overnight for a morning specimen. If not sent to the laboratory at once the specimen should be kept in a cool place. 

Specimens for urinalysis should be free from feces, blood, and expectoration and other extraneous substances. 

Six ounces, at least, is necessary for a full analysis of an ordinary specimen. In special cases a 24-hour amount is necessary. 

METHOD OF OBTAINING A CLEAN SPECIMEN 

Wash with suds and scald a pint specimen bottle. Label as usual, adding "clean specimen." The mouth of the bottle is covered with a thick sterile sponge, over which is put a paper, held in place by an elastic band. 

Wash a bedpan inside and out with hot suds. Scald and rinse and wrap in a clean hopper towel. 

The patient is washed locally with soap and water, with sterile sponges, and dried, or may be placed on a douche pan and douched off with a pitcher of hot suds, then with at 2% boric solution or plain warm water, and dried. The douche pan is removed and the patient immediately transferred to the clean bedpan.

When the specimen is obtained it is emptied into the bottle, which is re-covered securely with the clean sterile sponge and paper.

HOW TO SECURE A 24-HOUR AMOUNT OF URINE 

This is required when a quantitative test is to be made. 

Have the patient void at a specified time, i.e., 7 A.M.

Throw away that urine. All subsequent urine passed is measured or saved, according to directions received, including that which is passed at 7 A.M., the following morning.

XXIX 

ALCOHOL SPONGE BATH Purpose 

This bath is given chiefly for the sedative effect upon the nervous system; for stimulation to the circulation; to raise the blood pressure; and for reduction of temperature. As a rule it is given every four hours when the patient's temperature reaches a certain point, 102½° F. by mouth or 103° by rectum.

Equipment 

2 gray blankets large rubber sheet rubber pillow case patient's towel, 2 if a typhoid patient gauze sponge washed gauze gauze head binder ice cap hot water bag 

2 basins, one containing bathing solution and water, proportion, I to 12, in which the thermometer is kept; the other, a lump of ice about 6 x 6 inches, on which is placed the head binder bath thermometer 

If a typhoid or pneumonia patient, special precautions are taken by the nurse and she wears a rubber apron and 181

cotton gown with short sleeves, which are added to the equipment.

Procedure 

Make sure that all equipment is at bedside before beginning.

Turn upper bedclothes to waist line. Place folded blanket over chest, tucking upper edge over both shoulders. Use lower edge to carry upper bedclothing to foot of bed in three folds as in ordinary bath. 

If a typhoid or pneumonia patient, the rubber pillow case is in position, otherwise a rubber case is put on when the cotton case is removed. 

Move the patient carefully to the opposite side of the bed. Fold the rubber sheet in two lengthwise with the enameled side out, and place on the bed with its fold next to the patient. Arrange the blanket in the same way and place upon the rubber sheet. Turn the first fold of the blanket over the patient, out of the way, exposing its lower fold upon the upper half of the rubber sheet. Roll both together until the center is reached. Cross hands, firmly grasp the roll top and bottom and turn from head to foot, which will bring the arms parallel and the roll into position against the patient's back, when it is easily slipped under the patient's legs. He is then slowly turned on his side and the remainder of the blanket and rubber sheet are pulled through at his back. 

The nurse reaches to the opposite side of bed and smooths out the blanket and rubber sheet, and the patient is now lying on his back between the two blankets. The

ALCOHOL SPONGE BATH 

183 under blanket and rubber sheet in position should be as high as the back of the patient's neck. 

The nightgown is removed.

Place hot water bag, 125°, at feet, under blanket. Fold head binder and wring out in solution. Bind around head from back to front in turban fashion and place ice cap over it.

If a typhoid patient, wring towel out of solution, double and place over abdomen, and avoid touching abdomen during bath.

Observe three points: the patient's pulse for later comparison; the temperature of the water, which usually starts at 80°, gradually reaching a mean temperature of 70° after the patient's face and arms are done, when he has become accustomed to the lowered temperature; and the time, by the watch. The bath should be of twenty minutes' duration.

The face is bathed first and dried, using washed gauze. 

With a gauze sponge the solution is applied; one arm is done and then the other, using long soothing strokes downward, followed by brisk, circular friction with the nurse's hands. Procedure is repeated, rubbing until solution has evaporated from skin. Dry with towel, using light friction.

The ice is now put in basin of solution in order to reduce its temperature to 70°, when the ice is removed. 

This procedure is repeated from time to time throughout the bath, as the solution grows warmer. 

The bath is resumed and the order is as follows: the chest, as far as end of sternum; each side, pausing with sponge in axillary spaces; if a typhoid patient, the towel

is wrung out a second time and replaced, otherwise the abdomen is bathed as the remainder of the body; the lower extremities are then done. Fold back upper blanket over each leg separately, so that the thigh is exposed, when the front of each leg is done. Sponging the feet is omitted if they are cold. Instead, dry friction with the hands is sufficient. Blanket is replaced. 

Pulse and patient's general condition are again noted. 

Before turning patient on side, the abdominal towel is changed for the last time, for the typhoid patient. The patient is turned carefully. The back is done to end of sacrum, then blanket is turned back and thighs and back of legs are done.

The bath being now completed, with patient still on his side, the ice cap and head binder are removed and the hair is dried about the neck.

The abdominal towel is removed from the typhoid patient and the hot water bag removed. The blanket and rubber sheet are gathered in smooth folds, pressed under patient and removed from opposite side. The patient is rolled on his back, the outer rubber case is removed from pillow, or cotton case replaced, and nightgown put on. 

With lower edge of gray blanket the bedclothes are pulled up and the blanket slipped out, unless the patient is chilly, when it is left and hot water bag refilled, covered, and put at his feet until reaction has set in. 

Note patient's pulse and color, also the time, for later observations of the "drop" temperature.

Gather up and put away equipment; if a typhoid case, with the usual precautions. 

One-half hour after bath, "drop" temperature and pulse are taken and recorded on chart under the last pulse and temperature taken previous to the bath.

XXX 

POULTICES 

A poultice is a composition of water and some thickening ingredient, usually meal, which has been boiled until it is of the right consistency to spread on a foundation of cloth or paper for application to the skin when heat and moisture are desired.

As a rule in poulticing, flaxseed meal is used, which is the ground ripe seed of the flax plant. Flaxseed contains a mucilage that is extracted by boiling and gives the poultice a gelatinous consistency capable of holding the air beaten into it to make it light. Flaxseed also contains a fixed oil which helps to retain the heat and moisture for a longer time than any other ingredient used for poulticing.

Purpose 

Poultices are often prescribed for the sense of comfort and relief which they afford the patient in deep-seated pain.

They are applied to boils and abscesses to hasten their development preparatory to incision and drainage. They are sometimes used in the place of stupes in gaseous distention of the abdomen when constant heat is desired, and are less disturbing to a very sick patient than the frequent changing of stupes. They are used as a local application for stimulating effect upon the kidneys in conditions of urinary suppression. 185

FLAXSEED POULTICES Dimensions of a flaxseed poultice 

The poultice should more than cover the affected area by 4 or 5 inches. Poultices applied to the bony thorax and more solid muscular parts of the body may be of greater thickness than those applied to the more tender and sensitive abdomen. As a rule, those for the chest should be 34 of an inch thick; those for the abdomen, ½ an inch; and those for smaller areas about 1 inch. 

Amounts of water and meal

The amount of water needed in making a flaxseed poultice should be in proportion to its size and thickness. 

These are averages for an adult :

For the chest or abdomen, one quart to five half-pints For poulticed areas of arm or leg, one pint to one quart 

Areas of the face, neck, and finger, 6 to 8 ounces

The amounts of meal that these quantities of water will absorb will be sufficient to cover the affected parts. 

Materials which may be used for a foundation A light weight, pliant, brown wrapping paper is quite satisfactory as a foundation for the larger poultices of the chest and abdomen. Paper is a poor conductor of heat, easy to secure, costs less than cotton cloth, and is easily disposed of with the poultice. 

Cheesecloth and sheet wadding as a foundation are better for the smaller poultices, as they are more adapt-

POULTICES 

187 able to smaller parts, like the face or neck, and afford greater comfort. In all cases the covering of a poultice. should be thin and porous, so that the affected parts may have the full benefit of heat and moisture. 

Frequency of application 

Poultices applied to the abdomen and chest should be changed at least every hour; those applied to the limbs, every thirty to forty-five minutes, according to size; those of smaller dimensions, applied to the face or neck every twenty minutes. 

Method of holding in place

On the chest or back, a straight binder and shoulder straps are used; on the abdomen, a straight binder and perineal straps; on the limbs, a many-tailed bandage of cotton cloth or a dressing towel applied on the bias; on the hands and feet, a triangular bandage or a dressing 

FLAXSEED POULTICE APPLIED TO JAW

towel; on the neck, a dressing towel folded in two, then in bias folds as deep as needed. Folded in this way the towel adjusts itself to the curve of the neck, and is pinned at the side with two safety pins. To give more support to the poultice a piece of flexible cardboard, about 3 by 5 inches, is introduced into the bias fold of the towel against the back of the poultice.

For those applied to the face, as for a carbuncle of the lip, a 6 to 8-inch four-tailed bandage is used, the tails being tied on the side of the head opposite the affected part.

A poultice for a finger is made in the shape of a little bag into which the finger is thrust, and the poultice bandaged on. 

An abdominal flaxseed poultice

Equipment small blanket binder perineal straps safety pins gauze or thin cheesecloth sheet wadding brown wrapping paper two basins pint graduate flaxseed meal poultice saucepan poultice board iron spoon or wooden spatula vaseline and small spatula bathing solution

POULTICES 

189 

Preparation 

Into the poultice saucepan measure one quart or more of hot water, according to size of abdomen; cover. While the water is heating, prepare materials. Cut a piece of medium weight wrapping paper for the foundation, the required size. Fold down the edges 14 inches, as a confining border to the poultice. Measure the folded sheet wadding, which should extend slightly beyond the edges of the paper foundation to keep in the heat. In preparing the gauze or cheesecloth covering of the poultice allow a 

3-inch margin to tuck under.

If the water has reached the boiling point, remove the cover of the saucepan. Take large pinches of the flaxseed meal. Hold it loosely so that it may run into the boiling water between the fingers, not too rapidly, and at the same time stir constantly to prevent lumping or scorching of the flaxseed. When it has boiled and is of a semi-solid thickness, so that it drops in large clots from the spoon, remove from the fire and add vaseline the size of a pigeon's egg, or two teaspoons of bicarbonate of soda.

Stir through the flaxseed thoroughly and then beat vigorously two or three minutes, or until light and smooth.

Turn the flaxseed into the center of the paper foundation. Fill the saucepan with cold water, into which dip the iron spoon to prevent sticking to the flaxseed while spreading. Spread with circular motions of the spoon from the center out to the enclosed edges of the paper. 

When through, place the spoon in the water. Turn down the edges of the paper upon the flaxseed, then apply the gauze covering, which is drawn taut and tucked under the poultice. Place, with paper side down, upon the sheet

wadding. Both are rolled together and enveloped in a doubled towel, and carried to the patient's bedside in a warm basin, covered with another.

Application 

The upper bedclothing is turned down to the pubes. 

The small blanket is placed over the body, the nightgown folded out of the way, and the binder adjusted under the patient's back. If the poultice is applied for tympanites. following operation, the abdomen is lubricated with vaseline or oil because the circulation of the part may be poor and sensation less acute, so that it burns more readily. 

Uncover the basin and remove the poultice from the towel. Partly unroll, and with the flaxseed side of the poultice on the back of both hands, gradually transfer to the patient's abdomen, permitting only the edge of the poultice to touch the skin until the patient can bear it. 

Before pinning the binder it is well to lift the poultice again at each end several times to let more of the steam escape. The binder is pinned rather loosely to avoid pressure on the hot poultice against the skin. The perineal straps are adjusted, the patient covered, and the time noted.

When the poultice is changed, the discarded one is placed in the basin and the moist, poulticed area of the skin is sponged with bathing solution and dried, to prevent maceration. This is done each time the poultice is changed, and is followed with the lubrication of the skin, if sensitive.

Under no conditions should a nurse apply a poultice to the abdomen on her own responsibility.

POULTICES 

191 After care of materials It is well to wash the utensils before applying the poultice, unless it is a small poultice, when no time should elapse between its completion and its application. As the saucepan has been soaking in cold water, it is easily scraped clean with the spoon, rinsed, and scoured with fine sand soap with the special cloth reserved for this purpose. The spoon receives the same care.

In large poultices the gauze covering is loosened from the surface of the discarded poultice, rinsed in cold water, washed and hung up to dry, to be used next time. The discarded poultice is wrapped in several thicknesses of newspaper, securely tied and placed in a receptacle to be burned.

After care of the skin When a poultice to the chest or abdomen is finally omitted, the poulticed area is washed gently with soap and hot water, dried, sponged with bathing solution, and covered with a layer of sheet wadding or a piece of flannel, which is held in place with a binder for a few days until the skin is less sensitive. 

CHARCOAL AND FLAXSEED POULTICE 

Poultices are useful also for softening eczematous encrustations which occur in cases of neglected varicose ulcers or in stimulating the separation of slough in slowly healing wounds, as bedsores. In such cases, if a fetid odor be present, two or three tablespoons of powdered charcoal may be added to a quart of poultice. This acts as a deodorizing agent when applied to the wound. 

Such poultices are not repeated more than two or three times.

MUSTARD AND FLAXSEED POULTICE 

By adding mustard or ginger, the heat of the poultice may be intensified, when desired, for deep-seated pain in the chest, in place of a mustard paste. The proportion of mustard is two tablespoons to a quart poultice. Since ginger is not so irritating as mustard, three tablespoons may be used, added to the poultice batter just before beating by sifting the powdered ingredient over the surface of the poultice, or thoroughly dissolved in a little tepid water before adding. Such poultices are not usually applied more than once, and are removed when the rubefacient stage has been reached. 

As a substitute for flaxseed, cereals may be used. 

ROLLED OATS AND BRAN POULTICE 

rolled oats, two cups (6-oz. cup) bran, one cup water, one quart

This quantity would be sufficient for a chest or medium-sized abdominal poultice. When the water reaches the boiling point add the rolled oats and allow to boil. Stir until it begins to thicken, then add bran and continue to stir and let boil slowly. When a little thicker than porridge, turn the poultice onto its paper foundation and arrange as in the flaxseed poultice. Care must be exercised in its application, as this form of poultice is apt to burn the patient more readily than a flaxseed poultice, as the cereal does not absorb the water so completely. 

Instead of rolled oats, oatmeal may be used, with the same quantity of bran and water.

POULTICES 

193 

POTATO STARCH POULTICE 

These poultices are sometimes used in place of flaxseed poultices for softening encrustations of the skin in cases of neglected indolent or varicose ulcers. 

Wash four large-sized potatoes, pare as thinly as possible, wash again and grate with a vegetable grater down to the heart. Add one pint of cold water and permit to stand twenty minutes, stirring occasionally to soak out the starch. Strain through a wire strainer into a saucepan. Boil slowly until the potato water has become a thick starch. Cool until it thickens, then spread on cotton cloth, leaving a 2-inch margin to turn over the poultice. 

Cover the face of the poultice with gauze or thin cheesecloth. Add sheet wadding and parchment paper to the back of the poultice. Apply and hold in place with a dressing towel and safety pins. The bed should be protected. Leave on overnight.

VASELINE APPLICATIONS 

Vaseline applications are used to soften hardened or hypertrophied toe nails before clipping, and for the treatment of callouses on the soles of the feet. In either case soak the feet in hot suds for one hour, then apply compress cloth, thickly spread with vaseline, to the affected parts. Cover with sheet wadding and dressing towel to prevent exudation of vaseline. Apply a protective for the bed under the patient's feet and a cradle over them to prevent staining the bed linen. Allow the vaseline poultice to remain a day, remove and soak the feet again in hot suds, when in the case of the nails they may be successfully clipped and filed; if necessary, repeat the

treatment. In the case of callouses, the after treatment would be the application of pumice stone or sand soap with friction, followed with the application of a thin lubrication of vaseline or oil to subdue the irritation which may be produced by the friction.

XXXI STUPES 

Stuping is the application of moist heat to the body at frequent intervals by means of a cloth wrung out of hot or medicated water. These applications are sometimes called fomentations.

The action of the stupe may be increased by adding spirits of turpentine, combined with cottonseed oil to counteract the danger of blistering.

Purpose 

Stupes are applied for the relief of tympanites of typhoid fever, pneumonia, peritonitis, and for postoperative distention. Before stuping, to facilitate the escape of intestinal gas, a rectal tube, may be inserted to dilate the sphincters ani. The tip of the rectal tube is lubricated before insertion and the other end is placed in a urinal. 

Method of making a stupe wringer

The materials needed are: 

I yard heavy crash toweling 

2 broom sticks, 14 inches long

Double the toweling end to end and sew together, then sew each end across about 2 inches or more from the folded edge to permit the introduction of the broom 195

sticks. Sew up one side of the toweling along its length and leave the other side open as a pocket to receive the stupe between its folds. 

Abdominal stuping

The bedside table is moved toward the foot of the bed and protected with a rubber cover or newspaper, ready for the materials for stuping. In communicable diseases the nurse wears a special gown. 

Equipment small blanket half sheet and rubber protector straight binder with two perineal straps safety pins dressing towel stupe wringer two flannel pads for stupes, 16 by 11 inches sheet wadding, two pieces large enough to cover abdomen absorbent cotton ball applicator parchment paper agate pail medicine cup with turpentine mixture of cottonseed oil, 4 parts; spirits of turpentine, 2 parts rectal tube and urinal, if needed 

Preparation 

Arrange materials on table in order of use. Turn back upper clothing to patient's waist line. The patient raises his arms on the pillow out of the way. Place small blanket, doubled, over body. Turn nightgown up over

STUPES 

197 chest and draw upper bedclothing down to pubes. Under the patient insert half sheet and rubber rolled within the binder so that the half sheet is next to the patient. If the rectal tube is to be used, it is now inserted. Place a piece of sheet wadding large enough to cover the patient's abdomen over the turned back fold of bedclothing. Over the wadding the dressing towel is folded at one end and tucked over the edge of the upper bedclothing as a protective. On the towel is placed a second piece of wadding ready to receive the moist parchment paper later on. 

The sticks of the wringer are slipped into place and the wringer spread over the pail. One of the blanket pads or stupes is inserted between the folds of the wringer, and the parchment paper, which is loosely crumpled, is placed on the outside of the wringer. When all is ready a large pitcher of boiling hot water is carried to the bedside and poured over the contents of the wringer until saturated, when the sticks are grasped firmly, held as far apart as possible, and forcibly twisted in opposite directions until there is not drop of water evident. The parchment paper is placed over the sheet wadding, which is folded over to keep in the heat. Application

Fold back the blanket sufficiently to expose the abdomen. Saturate the absorbent cotton ball in the turpentine mixture and apply, starting at the umbilicus and covering the right and left upper and lower quadrants of the abdominal surface. Withdraw hot stupe from folds of wringer, shake out, place on back of both hands, and apply carefully to the patient. Cover at once with the sheet wadding containing paper. Raise each end

alternately a few times until the patient can bear the heat. Turn down the blanket over it and prepare another stupe before the first is removed. The interchange of the stupes is made quickly by turning the blanket back over the patient's chest and turning the sheet wadding and paper down together on the towel. The stupe on the patient is picked up in the center with one hand and placed upon the wringer, while at the same time the hot stupe is withdrawn from its folds with the other hand, and with the backs of both hands, applied as in the first place.

Stuping may be kept up for fifteen to twenty minutes out of every hour.

After care of patient At the end of each twenty minutes, the stupe and sheet wadding, with parchment paper, are removed, and the abdomen gently patted dry with the dressing towel, which has served previously as a protector to bedclothing, and the abdomen is covered with the second piece of sheet wadding. Remove half sheet and rubber. Adjust the binder and do not pin too snugly while distention is evident. Apply perineal straps to hold the binder in place. Pull down nightgown, remove small blanket and readjust bedclothing. As an aid to abdominal relaxation and comfort of patient with distention, support the back of the thighs with a pillow.

After care of materials The stupe wringer, parchment paper, and sheet wadding are hung up to dry, ready to use again. The stuping flannels are washed in cold water, then in hot soapy water,

STUPES 

199 after each treatment. The remainder of the turpentine mixture, if any, is tightly covered and used again for the same patient.
