Note 

The turpentine mixture should not be applied more than twice in 24 hours, to avoid blistering and the danger of absorption. If the urine is smoky in appearance, test for presence of albumin.

Laudanum or tincture of opium stupes Such stupes are prescribed for their sedative effect. 

The stupe is wrung out of boiling water and the laudanum is sprinkled evenly over its surface and applied to the abdomen and left on from twenty to thirty minutes. 

If the patient is not relieved, a second application may be prescribed. Sufficient opium may be absorbed through the skin to produce toxic symptoms. Observe for unusual and increasing drowsiness, pin point pupils, and slowed respiration.

Oil of gaultheria stupes 

Oil of gaultheria (wintergreen) contains a volatile oil composed largely of methyl salicylate, a specific for acute rheumatism, and is applied locally to swollen and inflamed joints. There are two methods of application, the wet and dry.

The first is done by applying the oil, followed by application of hot flannel stupes. The joints are stuped in succession for twenty minutes or more, according to number of joints involved. Finally the parts are en-

veloped in absorbent cotton or sheet wadding bandages, which are held in place by a cotton bandage. Instead of a cotton bandage, a dressing towel may be used to save unnecessary motion of winding and unwinding bandages. The bed should be well protected and the patient guarded against exposure during the operation, which may be done every four hours.

By the dry method the oil of gaultheria is applied to the joints with the absorbent cotton applicator, the cotton roller then being put on. Over the cotton roller, cotton bandages are adjusted to prevent fraying of the roller. 

The oil of gaultheria is applied twice a day. 

Sodium bicarbonate applications

Sodium bicarbonate applications are used as an anodyne for the joint inflammation of acute rheumatism.

If flannel bandages are used, the soda is rubbed into the bandage and rolled, then bound about the painful joints. 

The moisture of the patient's body dissolves the soda, some of which is absorbed. A solution may be made of bicarbonate of soda and the bandages wrung out and applied. The urine is tested daily for alkalinity.

XXXII COUNTERIRRITANTS 

Counterirritants are therapeutic agents possessing special irritating qualities when applied to the skin. They are used for the relief of acute and deep-seated pain and congestion.

The action of the counterirritant is felt at its point of application through its stimulation of the sensory nerve endings in the skin. These send the impulse to the transforming nerve centers, thus causing the superficial blood vessels to be dilated; in this way an increased supply of blood is sent to the surface, producing heat and redness, thereby relieving the congested organ or part beneath by lessening the blood supply and tension. The sense of heat and irritation to the skin produced by the counterirritant may also divert the patient's mind from the actual pain.

Counterirritants are classified according to the extent of their action on the skin :

Rubefacients, which redden the skin. 

Vesicants, which blister the skin.

Escharotics, which destroy the skin and soft tissue beneath.

RUBEFACIENTS 

Rubefacients are as a rule of a volatile nature. They should be closely observed in their action to avoid blister- 201

ing the skin, which might become infected and cause a deep sloughing wound.

Those commonly used are mustard, tincture of iodine, capsicum or menthol plasters, liniments, cupping, and heat, dry or moist. Heat is more likely to be ordered for the sedative effect. 

Mustard plaster

The application of a mustard plaster is a quick and convenient method of applying a rubefacient. 

Equipment mustard plaster tissue paper ½ yard compress cloth bowl of tepid water

Preparation 

Place the plaster, with the medicated side down, in the bowl of water. Fold compress cloth or small towel a little larger than the plaster, which is placed on the towel, mustard side up. The mustard is covered with the tissue paper, then placed on a folded towel, and applied over the affected part. Leave on for five or ten minutes, or until the skin is a bright red. Plasters are more irritating than mustard paste and need close watching. On removal of the plaster, the skin may be gently wiped with sweet oil or powdered with talcum, and protected from the friction of the clothing by covering the part with a piece of soft linen or muslin. If the area of pain is greater than one plaster can cover, two may be used.

COUNTERIRRITANTS 

203 

Mustard paste

A mustard paste may be increased or modified in strength by adding more or less mustard or flour as 

Equipment poultice board or paper compress cloth dressing towel scissors tissue paper flour mustard cottonseed oil dessert spoon small mixing bowl small pitcher of tepid water hot water bag In cases when the skin is oily or quick action is desired, a basin of warm soapy water should be added to bathe the patient's skin before applying the paste. 

Preparation 

The cloth. If the application is for the patient's chest, the cloth is doubled to the desired size (usually 10 x 6½ inches for an adult), allowing one inch in addition for folding over. A cut one inch deep is made on each side of the fold. The cloth is spread open on the board, ready to receive the paste, with one-half hanging over the edge of the board.

The paste flour, four dessertspoonfuls mustard, two dessertspoonfuls cottonseed oil, one dessertspoonful

In the absence of cottonseed oil, olive oil, or white of egg, or bicarbonate of soda may be used.

In preparing the paste for a child, the quantity of mustard would be one teaspoonful to four dessertspoonfuls of flour. 

First measure the flour, then the mustard, to avoid washing the spoon. Mix the dry ingredients together; add the oil and mix; next enough tepid water to make of the same consistency as a cake batter.

Completion and application The paste is scraped out of the bowl into the center of one-half of the compress cloth, and spread with the tip of the spoon in the form of a four-pointed star. Then it is spread between the points, making an even square within an inch of the points. The edges of the cloth are folded over the paste on its three sides, then it is covered with the other half and its three edges turned under. The completed paste is folded in thirds. The dressing towel is doubled lengthwise, then folded in thirds. One end is turned back and the mustard paste deposited upon the thickest part of the towel and adjusted. Pieces of tissue paper should cover the paste, to intervene between it and the skin to prevent blistering. The free end of the towel covers the face of the mustard paste, which is carried to the patient. If the patient is sensitive to cold, the mustard paste may be carried to the bedside upon a warm plate or a hot water bag.

COUNTERIRRITANTS 

205 The free end of the towel is folded back from the paste, which is reversed upon the exposed part of the chest. The towel serves to hold the paste in place, to prevent too rapid evaporation of the volatile content, and to protect the patient's clothing. 

Observations 

The skin under the mustard paste should be observed within ten minutes of application. It may be left on from fifteen to twenty minutes, depending chiefly upon the reaction of the skin to the mustard, and the toleration of the patient. The skin of some persons does not redden to any extent, but is sensitive and may easily blister; that of others develops a bright red blush in ten minutes, but is not especially sensitive.

When a mustard plaster or paste is ordered applied to the face or neck of a patient, special care must be exercised against blistering, which may result in a disfiguring scar, or an unsightly pigmentation of the skin. 

Removal of the paste 

When the paste is removed, pat the skin dry, oil or powder it, and protect from friction of the clothing. 

Substitutes 

In place of mustard, ground ginger, capsicum, or any of the pungent spices may be used. Double the quantity of these ingredients is used to the same proportion of flour as in the mustard paste. 

Tincture of iodine

Iodine should be kept in a glass-stoppered and glasslabeled bottle. It is highly volatile, and should be fresh

when used, as its strength increases with age. 

As a counterirritant it is usually ordered at the strength of 7%.

Equipment bottle of iodine small glass camel's hair brush or absorbent cotton applicator small piece of compress cloth 

2-inch cotton roller 1½-inch cotton bandage, if for an extremity Carry on a tray to the bedside. 

Application 

The part to be treated is outlined with cold cream, using the small piece of compress cloth, over the tip of the index finger. Just enough iodine is poured into the small glass. The applicator is dipped into the iodine, which is evenly applied to the skin with the side of the applicator, starting within the margin of the cold cream and working towards the center with circular strokes. A single coat of iodine is usually enough; when it has dried the cotton roller is applied for comfort, and over that the cotton bandage to hold it in place and for slight pressure when there is swelling. If the iodine is applied to the body, a sheet of wadding and binder are necessary, adjusted loosely so as to permit some evaporation and avoid blistering the skin. If the iodine burns more than it should, it is at once washed off with ether or alcohol. Ammonia water removes the stains from cotton material.

COUNTERIRRITANTS 

207 

Disposal of materials 

If an applicator has been used, it is thrown away. If a camel's hair brush, it is cleaned in ether or alcohol, ready for use again. The glass should be wiped with ether or alcohol, then washed with soap and water. Liniments

Liniments are preparations of several liquid volatile substances, with an oily base, for application to the skin. 

Because of the slight counterirritation and relief which they afford, especially if applied with brisk rubbing, or friction by the nurse's hand, if the patient can bear it, they are sometimes prescribed in muscle strain and painful affections of the joints. The treatment is followed by the application of a flannel bandage or a sheet wadding roller.

Commercial plasters 

Those used as mild counterirritants are medicated preparations, as of capsicum, menthol or belladonna, made to adhere to the skin. The drugs are incorporated in a base of tar and spread on cotton cloth by means of hot rollers. The plasters are usually perforated to permit evaporation of moisture from the skin, and are called porous plasters.

They are occasionally used for deep-seated pain and for relief of muscular strains of the back. The part to which the plaster is to be applied is washed with soap and hot water, then dried thoroughly. The plaster is slightly warmed and the strips of cloth covering it are removed before applying. If applied to the lumbar region, the patient should first bend slightly forward from the hips,

so that he may assume the upright position free from the discomfort of the restraint of the plaster. 

Dry cupping 

Dry cupping is a mechanical method of producing a circumscribed hyperemia of the skin over the affected part, for the relief of congested organs, as the lungs in pneumonia, the kidneys in nephritis, where suppression may be present, or for the evacuation of deep abscesses. 

Two methods are employed in the operation of cupping; one exhausts the air in the cupping glasses by means of a rubber bulb attachment like that of a breast pump. As the air is pumped out of the glass a partial vacuum is made into which the skin is sucked, causing it to balloon under the glass and the superficial blood vessels to dilate. In the second method, an alcohol flame is used to heat the air in the glass, when a partial vacuum is obtained through air expansion. In this method, if cupping glasses are not available, ordinary 4 or 5-ounce glasses, with smooth edges, may be used.

Equipment deep tray containing cupping glasses, four or five, according to surface to be cupped spirit lamp matches alcohol, 95% small glass orange wood stick with pointed end basin with soap and water package of sterile gauze blanket

COUNTERIRRITANTS 

209 Arrangement of materials at bedside The operation of cupping calls for a clear head and a deft and steady hand. The bedside table on which the materials are placed, and the screen, if one is used, should be so arranged as to give plenty of room to avoid danger of fire. If the cupping is to cover a large area of the body, the patient and bed should be protected with a blanket, and the patient reassured if he is apprehensive of being burned.

Procedure The part to be cupped is cleaned with soap, warm water, and sterile gauze, and then is thoroughly dried. 

Pour a half ounce of alcohol into the glass, and moisten the point of the orange stick in the alcohol. Over the point twist a piece of absorbent cotton, making a mediumsized swab. Light the spirit lamp, dip the swab in the alcohol, and press it against the side of the glass to squeeze out the excess alcohol. Pick up a cupping glass in one hand and invert; ignite the swab in the other hand and quickly sweep the inside of the cupping glass with the blazing swab, avoiding its edges. This sets the glass on fire, when it is quickly applied over the affected part while still flaming. The flame is extinguished on shutting out the air. The remaining glasses should follow in quick succession until the area to be cupped is covered. The swab should be dipped in the alcohol following the application of every second cup. This prevents charring the swab and overheating the cupping glass. 

Removal of cupping glasses

In two or three minutes, when the skin under the cups shows signs of deep redness, the skin at the margin of

the glass is pressed down, when the air rushes in and the glass is readily removed.

A cup, if permitted to remain overtime, may produce a bruise-like discoloration due to the rupture of the thin capillary walls by the suction of the cupping glass. 

VESICANTS 

Vesicants are agents used to produce a circumscribed blister. They are applied chiefly for the relief of pressure from effusions in a joint, as in acute synovitis. 

Cantharides, the agent commonly used, is prepared from the dried and powdered bodies of the Cantharis or blister beetle, better known as Spanish fly, from which are made for external application a collodion, a cerate, and a plaster.

Before applying a vesicant, the skin is shaved if necessary, cleaned with soap and hot water, and finally with. corrosive alcohol, using sterile sponges for scrubbing and drying the skin. This is done to prevent infection from the subsequent broken skin.

Cantharidal collodion 

The skin is prepared as in the application of all vesicants. The affected part is outlined with boric acid ointment, painted with the collodion and protected with a sterile sponge over which a loose bandage is applied. 

This is left until a blister appears, which may not be for 6 or 8 hours. The collodion about the margin of the blister is carefully removed with alcohol or ether. The blister is then snipped at its lower edge with a pair of sterile scissors, and its contents caught in a sterile sponge pressed against the opening. When it is empty

COUNTERIRRITANTS 

211 a sterile sponge thickly spread with boric acid ointment. to prevent sticking is applied and held in place with a comfortable bandage. This dressing may be changed daily until the blister is healed. 

Cantharidal cerate

A piece of sterile cotton cloth cut the size ordered for the part to be blistered is spread with the cerate and covered with a sterile sponge, the edges of which may be held in place with narrow strips of adhesive tape just tight enough to hold the sponge in place and loose enough to permit a blister to rise. The cerate is usually slower in action than the collodion. 

Cantharidal plaster

The size of the plaster ordered is seldom larger than 

2 x 2 inches. Following the preparation of the skin, sweet oil is applied to its surface or to the face of the plaster, which is held in place as in the other cases, avoiding pressure. If a blister is not evident in 6 or 8 hours, to hasten its development, especially where the skin may be thick, as over the knee, moist heat is applied in the form of a small flaxseed poultice or sterile sponges wrung out of hot sterile water. The usual careful treatment of the blister is carried out. 

Certain observations should be made in the use of cantharides and reported, as enough may be absorbed into the circulation to cause irritation of the urinary system. 

The patient may complain of pain, and difficulty in voiding urine (strangury). The urine may present a smoky appearance, and if so, a specimen should be saved for analysis.

Ammonia and chloroform 

Ammonia water may be used as a vesicant. Secure a large-sized thimble and pack a little absorbent cotton into its tip. Then pour a few drops of ammonia into the thimble and invert over the part to be blistered. 

Chloroform may be applied for the same purpose. Leeching 

The medicinal leech has a prehensile disk at each extremity by means of which locomotion is produced by lengthening and shortening its body. The leech attaches itself at each end to the patient's body while abstracting blood. 

Its mouth is in the anterior disk.

Leeches are used for the relief of acute local inflammations, especially those of the eye and ear, and have been used on other parts of the body for deep-seated congestion in place of wet cupping. For application a leech is placed in a wide-mouthed bottle. Application

The exact point prescribed at which the leech is to be applied is cleaned with a sterile sponge, unscented soap and hot water, then wiped with a dry sterile sponge. In applications to the face where the contact of the leech is disagreeable to the patient, a double piece of sterile gauze may be used in which one or more small openings may be cut opposite the points to be leeched, through which the leech may bite. The patient's ears should be plugged with absorbent cotton or a towel bound snugly over both ears and pinned on the top of the head. A second towel should be pinned firmly about the patient's neck and

COUNTERIRRITANTS 

213 tucked over his shoulders. These precautions are taken in case the leech escapes.

In applying the leech the direction in which it is headed is noted, usually towards the mouth of the bottle; if not, the bottle should be shaken until the leech heads outward.

The cork is partly removed from the bottle, the water drained off and the mouth of the bottle quickly inverted over the point to be treated. The bottle is held in place and the leech attaches itself to the patient's skin, where it usually remains until engorged with blood, when it rolls off. If to be applied to a part for a limited time, salt is sprinkled on the leech in order to detach it. If more than one leech is applied, the patient's pulse should be watched for quality and rate, to avoid overbleeding. 

Keep the leech out of sight if possible.

After removal the bleeding points are treated with antiseptic precautions and a sterile dressing is applied. If bleeding is persistent, report.

Leeches are usually disposed of by burning or burying.

ESCHAROTICS 

The term escharotic is rarely heard in the present day; nevertheless such agents are frequently used for the destruction of morbid tissue and the removal of abnormal growths in the skin, by burning or corroding. 

For the application of heat the thermo-cautery is used, especially where large surfaces of the skin must be treated, as in cases of congenital angiomata, disfiguring pigmentations of the skin and cancerous growths. The thermo-cautery acts more quickly and the degree of appli-

cation desired can be better controlled than in the use of chemical caustics. 

Thermo-Cautery 

A thermo-cautery consists of a hollow platinum tip screwed into a wooden handle which is attached to a rubber tubing and connected with a T-shaped tube that fits into a metal cap and screws into the lamp. Attached to the opposite or right side of the T is an extension of rubber tubing, on the end of which is a rubber bulb enclosed in a mesh covering. Within the lamp is a small piece of sea sponge or lamb's wool, which is saturated with benzine. It is important that only enough benzine to saturate the wool is used. At the side of the lamp is a metal hook for suspension on the belt of the operator's gown.

HOOK PLATINUM TIP WOODEN HANDLE THERMO-CAUTERY AIR BULB

COUNTERIRRITANTS 

215 The spirit lamp is lighted, and the platinum tip is heated in the flame. The fumes of benzine are slowly forced from the lamp into the platinum tip by pressure on the bulb until the tip is a red or white heat, as desired. If the operation is a long one, gentle pressure is made on the bulb at short intervals to keep the tip at constant heat. 

When the thermo-cautery is used in surgery, the assistant operating the bulb stands as far away from the anæsthetizer as possible, to prevent an explosion due to the heat of the cautery and the inflammable nature of anesthetics. 

On completion of the operation by the doctor, the nurse takes charge of the cautery, which is still hot, and if it is not possible to attend to it at once, she is careful to place it in a fireproof receptacle, with the tip pointed upwards until ready to care for it. There are two reasons for these precautions: first, the danger of fire if the tip is placed near inflammable material and second, because of its delicate mechanism; if any pressure is brought to bear on the tip it is easily dented and damaged beyond repair.

If the platinum tip has been used upon live tissue, the only way to clean it is to burn the adherent tissue off in a flame. When cool the tip is carefully wiped with a sponge and put away.

The more modern thermo-cautery is attached to an electric plug in the wall. It is manipulated by pressure on a thermostat which controls the amount of heat desired.

Following this treatment, if severe, the skin and underlying tissues slough, and are gradually replaced by new granulating tissue. In several weeks skin grafting is usually done.

Caustics 

Chemical caustics are applied for the treatment of small blemishes of the skin, for the removal of warts, which are hypertrophied papilla of the skin, and corns, which are hypertrophied layers of skin caused by pressure. 

In using caustics, the area of the skin around the part to be treated should be protected. 

The acid caustics are more commonly used than the alkali. Some of these are: glacial acetic acid nitric acid carbolic acid, 95% trichloracetic acid. acid nitrous of mercury carbon dioxide snow lunar caustic pencils, which are kept with their tips imbedded in flaxseed in a wide, shallow-mouthed bottle to keep dry.

XXXIII INHALATIONS AND CROUP TENT 

Inhalation affords a means of treatment for certain affections of the respiratory and circulatory systems.

For such treatment preparations of a volatile nature are used, as gas, the vapor of volatile drugs, or plain steam. Certain drugs are volatilized in boiling water, others give off the vapor by burning.

Oxygen GAS 

Oxygen is used in emergency for treatment of sudden heart failure, shock, gas or chloroform poisoning, and in pneumonia and dyspnoea or any other conditions in which there is cyanosis. 

This gas can be made in several ways. One is by heating potassium chlorate and manganese dioxide together, which releases the oxygen. It is furnished in steel cylinders, under pressure, and is given to the patient directly from the cylinder. Before the oxygen can be used, the cylinder is fitted out with a wide-mouthed 10-ounce bottle, half filled with water, the cork perforated by two bent glass tubes, one longer than the other. The longer tube dips under the water, the shorter projects below the cork about an inch. A 10-inch piece of rubber tubing connects the escape valve of the cylinder with the longer glass tube. The outer end of the shorter tube is con- 217

nected with an inhaler or mask by a 3-foot piece of tubing. 

Method of administration 

The oxygen is turned on slowly by means of a stopcock, permitting the gas to pass through the short rubber tubing into the longer glass tube, under water, which helps to moisten the gas. From the water the gas escapes through the shorter glass tube and longer rubber tubing to the inhaler, from which the patient inhales it. The size of the bubbles in the water indicates the pressure of the oxygen, which should be regulated to run rather slowly, making small bubbles.

In certain emergencies, as gas poisoning, the closed method of giving oxygen is sometimes used. The end of the long rubber tubing connected with the bottle is introduced through the tip of the cardboard ether cone as far as its mouthpiece, and the cone is then placed over the patient's face and the oxygen is turned on. A gag is sometimes used to hold the patient's jaws apart. 

In other conditions of unconsciousness the oxygen may be administered by means of a small rubber catheter introduced into the naso-pharynx. This method is the most efficient of any. 

Points to be noted

An oxygen tank should be in readiness at all times. 

The inhaler should always be attached to the longer piece of tubing, otherwise the force of the gas will drive the contents of the bottle into the patient's face. 

Do not crowd the inhaler over a patient's face. 

Inhaler and tubing should be sterilized before using on a second patient.

INHALATIONS AND CROUP TENT 

219 The valve of the cylinder should be closed tight when not in use, to prevent escape of gas. 

VOLATILE DRUGS Amyl nitrate 

This drug is a vaso-dilator and a muscle depressant ; it acts upon the walls of the arteries and bronchi, causing them to relax. It is given in angina pectoris and in the paroxysms of dyspnoea and in asthma. If given properly it acts instantaneously. 

The drug is confined in covered glass ampules containing 3-5 minims. It is administered by first breaking the ampule within the folds of a gauze sponge, or small towel, which is held near the patient's nostrils. Inhalation should be slow, with the mouth closed. 

After inhalation it produces a sense of fullness of head or sharp headache, flushing of face, and increased heart action and respiration for a short time. An overdose will produce cyanosis, cold clammy skin, irregular respiration, and death from paralysis of the heart. After inhalation the patient should be closely observed. 

Other drugs 

Aromatic spirits of ammonia and smelling salts may be inhaled from the receptacles in which they are held. 

They are used in faintness as respiratory stimulants.

Ammonia gas is sometimes used as a stimulant in extreme conditions, as heart failure. It is very pungent and irritating to the eyes, which should be protected.

STEAM INHALATIONS 

Steam inhalations are used especially for local treatment in acute nasal or bronchial affections. The medium is boiling water into which may be introduced a drug, ordered for the purpose, whose medicinal properties may be antiseptic, or antispasmodic, and stimulating. Ther drug should be thoroughly mixed with the boiling water and the steam inhaled.

For these conditions some of the drugs given are compound tincture of benzoin, oil of eucalyptus, menthol, thymol, and spirits of camphor. 

This treatment is best at bedtime, following a hot bath.

Improvised method of giving a steam inhalation In the absence of a special inhaler or atomizer, one may easily use improvised methods with good results.

The necessary equip ment includes a strong paper bag, large enough to hold a three-pint pitcher, with the narrow side cut out to fit under the patient's chin and permit the introduc tion of his face into PATIENT TAKING A STEAM INHALATION 

the bag close to the rising steam; a three-pint pitcher of boiling water; and an enamel basin.

INHALATIONS AND CROUP TENT 

221 The pitcher is first put into the paper bag. The drug is measured, turned into the boiling water in the pitcher, stirred, and the mouth of the paper bag rolled tight to keep in the steam. It is then carried in the basin to the bedside. The patient is instructed to place his face in the bag over the pitcher, at the same time fitting the bag round his face to prevent escape of the steam. A small blanket or turkish towel thrown over his head will still further serve this purpose.

If compound tincture of benzoin is used, it should be stirred at intervals with a wooden spatula to break up the film which forms on the surface and allow the steam to escape. 

Following the use of benzoin, alcohol or ether will remove the gummy deposit in cleansing utensils.

A CROUP TENT 

This treatment is sometimes ordered for croup in children, in cases of bronchial colds, and following operations on the throat. It may be kept up indefinitely until relief is obtained.'

Equipment screen, three-folded heavy cord safety pins

Procedure gray blanket two sheets

The blanket is spread over the top of the screen, hanging down 10 inches in front and evenly at the sides. The corners are squared with the screen and pinned. The

first sheet covers the blanket and falls over in front about 10 inches and over one side of the screen. The second sheet is folded lengthwise and applied to the remaining side and back. It is pinned at the top along its folded. edges, meeting the first sheet at the back corner of the screen, where an opening is made for the introduction of the spout of the croup kettle. 

Great care should be exercised to prevent fire from the alcohol lamp, which should be surrounded with a metal screen, and the patient should be far enough removed from direct steam to prevent being scalded. An electric plate may be used instead of an alcohol lamp.

DRY INHALATIONS 

In such inhalations the drug, which is in dry form, is ignited and produces a smoky vapor. This is used as an antispasmodic in treatment of asthma, either bronchial or cardiac.

Stramonium in leaf or powder form may be ordered, combined with potassium nitrate. The leaves are usually 1 2 3 1. Pitcher within the paper bag in position for a steam inhalation. 

2. Paper bag inverted over a bowl for dry inhalation. 

3. When the patient cannot sit up he may take the steam inhalation through a window cut in the side of the paper. 

PAPER BAGS CUT FOR INHALATIONS

INHALATIONS AND CROUP TENT 

223 smoked in a clay pipe or made into cigarettes. The pulverized form is ignited and inhaled. 

For this purpose a bowl and paper bag are used. Even the top of the bag with the scissors and cut an oval hole in the bottom for the introduction of nose and mouth. 

In the bowl is placed one teaspoonful of stramonium, which is ignited. As it smoulders the bag is inverted over the bowl and all is carried to bedside in an enamel basin.

The patient holds the basin between his knees and is instructed to hold his nose and mouth over the opening in the bag, and inhale until relief is obtained. 

Treatment may be repeated as often as necessary, but relief should be obtained in one-half hour. 

Sometimes medicated papers are burned which have first been soaked in the solution of the drug and dried. 

Potassium nitrate or sodium nitrate are administered in this way and are called niter papers.

XXXIV COLONIC IRRIGATION 

A colonic irrigation is a slow and continuous inflow and outflow of a fluid into the colon by way of the rectum, a douche bag or douche pail serving as a reservoir for the solution.

Purpose 

Irrigations are prescribed for local and systemic treatment. Locally they are sometimes given for relief of flatus, for disinfection and cleansing of the colon in ulcerative colitis, in mucous colitis, constipation, diarrhea, and inflammation of the cecum.

They are prescribed for systemic absorption of water or saline fluids in certain cases in which the patient's tissues have lost a large amount of fluid, as in hemorrhages, shock, vomiting and diarrhea; or in cases when fluid cannot be tolerated by mouth. 

Preparations which may be ordered

Local. For local treatment the irrigations most commonly used are sterile saline solutions or 2-3% solutions of nitrate of silver.

Systemic. In forcing fluids for systemic absorption irrigations of plain tap water or 0.6 saline solutions are used.

In mercurial poisoning potassium acetate is sometimes prescribed in proportions of one ounce to a gallon of water, for its diuretic action. 224

COLONIC IRRIGATION 

225 

Equipment blanket half sheet and rubber protector hopper towel bath thermometer douche bag or douche pail, rubber tubing with a metal clamp and glass pipette or glass connecting tube to which is attached catheter (Am. No. 10 or No. 12) for inflow. Around the catheter should be a narrow strip of adhesive tape, 3 inches below the eye to serve as a marker. rubber rectal tube (Am. No. 22) with a catheter eye and glass connecting tube to which is attached 18 inches of rubber tubing for outflow. Seven inches from catheter eye of rectal tube is also a narrow adhesive strip to serve as a marker. lubricant small rubber sheet slop jar or pail prescribed solution

Procedure 

In place of a Kelly pad, a trough may be made by rolling the edges of a small oblong piece of rubber sheeting on each side towards its center, leaving a foot-wide space for the escape of fluid into a receptacle below. 

Each rolled edge is held in place by a small safety pin on the under side.

Prepare solution in receptacle, and cover to keep in heat. Suspend 2 feet above patient.

Remove one pillow.

Turn back upper bedclothing to waist. 

Protect patient with extra blanket; half sheet and rubber arranged as for an enema; patient usually placed on the left side with head lower than the hips, which may be raised by a pillow, and the upper knee flexed.

Place rubber trough under hips for drainage into the jar at side of bed. 

Drape hips with hopper towel under blanket.

Operation 

Temperature of the solution is 110°-115° F., unless otherwise ordered. The purpose of the treatment determines the quantity. If given for absorption, ½ to 2 gallons of saline solution or sterile water is ordered; if a medicated fluid, the quantity may be less and may be preceded with a large saline irrigation, to cleanse the colon of mucus.

Allow sufficient fluid to run through inflow catheter to expel air and equalize temperature of water in tubing, then shut off. Lubricate catheter and rectal tube and introduce together into anus, as one. Slowly withdraw the catheter and push in the rectal tube until the two adhesive strips are on a level just outside the anus.

Release snap and allow the solution to flow. 

Cover patient and carry on the irrigation.

When irrigation is completed, clamp tubing and withdraw inflow catheter first, then larger rectal tube. 

Dry patient and rearrange bed for comfort.

COLONIC IRRIGATION 

227 An interrupted method of giving the irrigation is sometimes used, by clamping or pinching the outflow tube and permitting the inflow to continue until the colon is filled, or to the extent of toleration by the patient. The inflow tube is clamped when the outflow is released. This method may be used in constipation or other local affections of the colon to insure more complete cleansing. 

Observations 

Flatus is shown by bubbles in the glass tube as the solution flows out into the pail. 

Observe washings for mucus and blood, and report results.

For local treatment this may be given twice a day, or for systemic treatment it may be continuous as prescribed. 

Obstructions of the outflow tube may be caused by small fecal masses. In such cases it is necessary to remove the rectal tube and clear it out. 

The use of one tube

In the absence of the double rectal tubes for flushing out the colon a single rectal tube and funnel may be used with good results. Insert the rectal tube and pour the solution in slowly, one pint or more at a time.

The patient is persuaded to hold the solution a minute or two, then it is drained off through the tube into a receptacle on the floor. The operation is repeated until the prescribed amount of solution has been used or until it runs clear.

Metal tubes for irrigations 

In place of the rectal tube and catheter, a metal irrigating tube may be used. These tubes are simple in construction and easily manipulated. They are planned to work on the same principle as an intra-uterine douche nozzle with a return flow. They are made up with an inflow and outflow tube all in one. The connection of the rubber tubing is on the same principle as the catheter and rectal tube, as described on page 225. 

The metal irrigator should be lubricated before insertion, being careful not to plug up the perforations, as this would obstruct the return flow. 

Before inserting, allow the solution to run long enough to warm the irrigator slightly, as well as to expel air. 

In cleansing the irrigator before putting away, the inner tube, which is detachable, should be unscrewed and removed, and all washed in cold water, scrubbed with soap and hot water, and sterilized.

Rubber tubes are preferred in most cases because of their flexibility.

Solutions 

COLONIC IRRIGATION FOR INFANTS 

The solutions generally used are saline, o.6 salt solution, sodium bicarbonate, 120 grains to I quart, or tap water.

Equipment ordinary table low chair

COLONIC IRRIGATION 

229 tub or pail soft rubber catheter agate irrigating can glass connecting tube rectal lubricant hopper towel half sheet and rubber protector

Procedure 

Not less than two quarts of solution should be used for systemic absorption. 

Not more than one irrigation should be given in 24 hours.

The temperature of solution is 105° F. unless otherwise ordered. The size catheter is No. 18-13 Am., depending on the size of the baby.

Prepare the solution in the irrigating can and attach the catheter with glass connecting tube. Bring to the bedside with the tub, half sheet and rubber, and rectal lubricant; cover with the hopper towel.

Place low chair before table, facing it, with the tub on the floor between the chair and table. The nurse sits in the chair with the half sheet and rubber over her lap, one end of the rubber extending down as a trough into the tub or pail.

The baby's clothes must be well turned back, exposing the buttocks. It is best to roll a small blanket around him, thus securing his arms, as he is apt to struggle. He is placed across the nurse's lap with the buttocks extending beyond her knees over the tub.

PROCEDURES IN. NURSING 

Lubricate the catheter. Let a little solution run through the tube to get out the air. With the left hand, hold the baby's legs, so that the buttocks are slightly elevated. 

With the right hand insert the catheter about 12 to 14 inches into the rectum. If water is allowed to flow during insertion of tube, it will dilate the colon, preventing the tube from doubling back on itself, preventing any forcing of the tube against the intestinal wall. There is no danger in inserting the tube this distance if the water is allowed to flow ahead of it.

The irrigating can should not be more than 2 feet above the level of the baby. Let the fluid run in till the abdomen is slightly distended, then allow it to run out, and so on until the water returns clear. Gentle massage of the abdomen facilitates the reaching of the fluid to the upper part of the colon. If the object of the irrigation is to cleanse the colon, enough liquid should be used to do this whether it is much or little.

The connecting tube is detached after the irrigation, the catheter remaining in for a few minutes until all the fluid possible is drained off.

XXXV 

In a general hospital there are different avenues of admission which are governed by the patient's physical condition. 

Front door admissions. So-called "front door" admissions are those who have been recommended to the hospital by their family physician, who, as a rule, makes arrangements by telephone for the entrance of the patient as soon as a bed can be secured. Such cases are, generally, not so ill as to need immediate hospital attention, and later formally enter at a definite time through the admitting office. 

Emergencies, medical, surgical, and recent accidents, are admitted at all hours, and are never turned away. 

Ambulance cases may also be emergency cases, but are frequently those who have been ill at home, and are unable to walk. These are generally called "front door" admissions, as previous official arrangements have been made before entering the hospital.

FRONT DOOR ADMISSIONS 

Routine care of patient on admission

A front door admission usually walks into the ward with a messenger who brings the patient's yellow slip and name card. Cardiac patients are brought to the ward in a wheel chair. 231

The patient is met by the head nurse, or by any nurse who is at hand, so that he may have immediate attention. Be as courteous as though you were receiving a guest in your own home. The first impression a patient receives is seldom forgotten.

Take the temperature, pulse, and respiration at once and record on the yellow slip for the time being. The chart will come from the Treadwell medical library the day of admission, with top filled out in typewriting, and the temperature, pulse, and respiration are noted, to be copied later in their proper places on the chart. Patient may be asked if he has been in bed at home or before admission, duration of illness, symptoms, and, according to the time of day, if he has had dinner or supper. 

Notify at once the house officer who is in charge of the ward at that time. He can be reached by telephone. 

Give name of admission; for example, John Doe: diagnosis, right inguinal hernia; also the temperature, pulse, and respiration. If the patient has not had a meal, ask what he may have. The house officer often asks about the patient's general condition at this time. 

The nurse in charge assigns the patient to a bed, and decides if he may have a tub bath. If temperature is over 100° F. or subnormal, or if the pulse is weak and rapid, the routine is generally a bed bath. 

The tub bath 

If the patient is permitted to have a tub bath the water is drawn by the nurse. The tub should be not more than half full, at a temperature of 100° F. Instead of a bath mat, newspapers may be spread on the floor at the side

of the tub. On a chair nearby should be towels, face cloth, soap, nail brush. In addition to toilet articles, nightgown, wrapper, stockings and slippers should be laid out for a woman; pyjamas, wrapper, socks and slippers for a man. On the floor, in both cases, should be spread a clean cotton square upon which is placed temporarily the clothing of the patient, carefully folded, to be tagged later.

The nurse renders what assistance is necessary, according to the patient's condition and age. In the case of a male patient, the orderly aids him. The patient, if feeble, should be assisted into and out of the tub. The nurse or orderly may help with the bath and at the same time. should closely observe the skin for any unusual condition, such as minute hemorrhagic spots or scratches that would indicate the presence of pediculi and would call for thorough inspection of clothing and body, with treatment of both according to special directions. 

It frequently happens that the patient may be admitted to a surgical ward for a minor operation, and is otherwise fairly well and strong. In such cases the patient may prefer the privacy of the bathroom in taking his bath.

With a woman patient the nurse should attend to all needs and, as in the first case, offer assistance, or if not needed she should see that the tub is securely curtained before leaving, and tell the patient that she will be within call. Having the patient in mind, she should look in on her in time to assist her in dressing, and observe the condition of the bath water before it is allowed to run out, also to scrutinize the patient, without attracting attention, and note whether or not she is clean.

When through with the bath, if patients are to remain up, they are in ward clothes. They are shown the toilet and where drinking water may be obtained. Men are told that they must not smoke without the doctor's permission, and then they must smoke outside. 

They are then guided to the ward and comfortably seated by the bedside, or put to bed if necessary. 

All medical cases are put to bed on admission until ordered otherwise. They should be weighed when possible.

At this time if the patients in neighboring beds are not too ill they may be informally introduced to each other. 

It is sometimes possible to place together the beds of foreign patients who are of the same nationality. 

The hair

A woman patient is supposed to wear her hair in braids, as it will prove to be more comfortable, and the hair is less likely to snarl. While the nurse attends to this she will be given the opportunity to observe the condition of the hair, when, if necessary, immediate treatment will be tactfully suggested and carried out as if it were a mere matter of routine, so that the patient may in no way feel sensitive.

In the case of a male patient or a child, the same treatment should be carried on. See Chapters XVI, XVII, and XVIII. 

The patient's clothing

Private patients are allowed to keep and wear their own clothes and sign a slip to this effect. Otherwise clothes are tagged with patient's full name, listed cor-

rectly on Clothes Slips, signed by nurse and patient, and taken with slips to the General Locker Room, where the slips are verified and a locker number is put on the slips, one of which is taken back and kept on the chart rack. 

If the patient is on a special ward, such as the skin ward, the clothes are tagged as usual, hung up enclosed in cotton covers, and number of locker put on slip. 

Clothes Slips have to be made out for these also. 

Soiled clothing, with the exception of woolen articles which are sent to the clothes room, must be sent home if possible, or be marked with a cotton tag on which is printed in indelible ink the name of the patient and the ward, and sent to the laundry. . Clothing of precaution cases is tagged, fumigated, and placed in the General Locker. 

Care of personal property

Valuables, with the exception of $1.00, are given at once to the nurse in charge, who is responsible for them. 

They are listed on the Clothes Slip, under the proper heading, and signed for by the patient and the nurse. 

They are put in an envelope and listed on the outside, with the date, ward, and name of the patient. They are then taken as soon as possible to the Cashier's office, where they are to be kept. They are signed for on the Valuables 

Slip which is returned to the nurse, and finally after five o'clock the head nurse checks valuables with the admitting superintendent, who places them in the safe in the office.

No one is allowed to keep valuables except at his own risk, when a Responsibility Slip for such articles has to be signed by the patient.

EMERGENCY WARD ADMISSIONS 

In such cases the ward will be notified by telephone so that the bed may be ready. These cases are reported to the house officer in charge of the ward, although the patient has been seen by some one in the Emergency Ward. The clothes and valuables are listed in the Emergency Ward, and only the listed and signed Clothes Slip comes with the patient.

If no special orders come with the patient, he is given bed bath and liquids without milk until further order. 

AMBULANCE CASES 

Such ambulance cases as are front door admissions are reported to the house officer.

These patients are generally so ill that the bath is given in bed.

Bed bath 

Turn down the bedclothing; at the same time add the bath blanket, and if in the cool season, a hot water bag. 

When the details of admission have been attended to and the patient has rested for a little while, he will be ready for the bath, which is carried out as in the ordinary bed bath with the usual observations of head and body. Any burns or pressure sores or any other unusual condition should be reported to the head nurse, who in turn reports in writing to the superintendent of the training school. 

MEDICO LEGAL CASES 

These are industrial accidents, burns, fractures, etc. 

Clothes should be folded compactly, with as little dis-

turbance to their actual condition as possible, and made into a bundle. This bundle must not be removed from the ward or room in which the patient is until 12 hours have elapsed since the patient was brought to the hospital, when this bundle, properly tagged, may be sent to the General Locker Room. If the patient should die within 12 hours, the clothing goes to the morgue.

XXXVI PHYSICAL EXAMINATION 

On entrance to the ward all patients have a bath before physical examination unless their condition does not permit.

As a rule the patient's physical history is taken first by the doctor and followed with the physical examination. 

Other special examinations may be done later. If the patient is a woman, the nurse is always present and assists the doctor.

Equipment large gray blanket shoulder blanket dressing towel kidney basin sphygmomanometer

The doctor usually carries with him: stethoscope tape measure blood pipette wooden tongue depressors flashlight reflex hammer ear speculum 2.38

PHYSICAL EXAMINATION 

239 

If an open ward, screen the bed carefully and see that the patient is not directly exposed to an open window during the examination. If it is the first time the patient has been in a hospital, explain to her, if possible, in a brief but reassuring manner, what is going to be done, and its relation to her welfare. Besides being kind, this may help in getting better coöperation from the patient if she is timid.

Loosen the upper bedclothing at both sides and turn back at the waist. Apply the gray blanket as in a bath, carry the bedclothing to the extreme foot of the bed, and arrange in small folds. The patient is now lying under the blanket. Remove her nightgown and replace with the shoulder blanket, loosely pinned at her neck in front.

General survey 

The first steps in the examination may be a general survey of the patient's physical development, the skin and mucous membranes, followed with inspection of the ears, nose, mouth, neck and lymph nodes.

The chest 

If the patient is able, she is assisted to sit up in bed. 

She should be comfortably balanced in the sitting position, with her hips well drawn back. This detail should not be overlooked by the nurse, otherwise the upright position without adjustment is the cause of extreme fatigue to the patient because of the strain thrown upon the muscles

in the ilio-sacral region. A pillow at her back offers. no relief in this position.

While the patient must be protected from unnecessary exposure, it is also important that the procedure of examination by the doctor should not be restricted by clothing coming in contact with the stethoscope during auscultation, or interfering with the process of percussion. 

The front of the chest

The upper end of the blanket covering the patient should be tucked snugly about the waist and hips, and a pillow should be pressed against the lower edge of her back. 

As the shoulder blanket is pinned in front, each side is lifted in turn, or moved about at will out of the way by the nurse. The patient's face is always turned away from the side which the doctor is examining, or an interposing folded towel may be used, a little distance from the patient's face.

The back of the chest The shoulder blanket is turned around with the opening at the back and the corners thrown over the shoulders out of the way.

The abdomen 

The patient lies flat on her back, the shoulder blanket is removed, placed across the chest and folded back at the waist line. The gray blanket is turned back at the pubes and tucked about the hips on each side, allowing sufficient room for flexion of the knees, in the routine examination of the abdomen.

PHYSICAL EXAMINATION 

241 

Extremities 

The hands and arms are uncovered and tested for tremor, etc., and the lower extremities for the reflexes. 

A depression is made in the blanket, between the patient's thighs, and one leg is exposed at a time from above the knee to the foot.

Spinal 

Although the spine may be examined with the patient in bed, when observation of posture is desired it is best done with the patient standing on the floor. The patient is dressed in nightgown, opened at the back, wrapper, blanket, stockings and slippers. The blanket is pinned low about the hips and the wrapper is put on with the opening at the back, so that an unobstructed and complete view of the spinal column is obtained. The nurse stands beside the patient, ready to give support. Pelvic

Rectal 

In a routine rectal examination an enema may be ordered.

The patient is placed in the lateral position with hips on the edge of the bed, upper knee flexed and shoulder directed forward, and is protected with a blanket and towel as for an enema. If the rectal speculum is used, it is placed in a basin of warm 2% boric solution. A pair of rubber gloves, a small jar of boric ointment, a dressing towel should be at the bedside in a tray, and a round enamel basin.

Vaginal 

Unless for special reasons, a vaginal examination is rarely done on a patient admitted to the medical service. 

In the surgical service it is usually part of the routine examination.

If the patient is able to get out of bed she is dressed in nightgown, wrapper, stockings and slippers. The patient should void, as the bladder should be emptied before all pelvic examinations. An enema is given, in addition, in special cases, when ordered by the doctor. 

A large sheet and dressing towel should be provided, also a tray containing a small glass of lubricant, gloves, powder, bivalve speculum, uterine forceps, uterine sound, pledgets of cotton attached to long wooden applicators, cotton balls, round enamel basin. Slippers are removed and the patient assisted onto the table, and placed in the dorsal position. The clothing under her is tucked smoothly out of the way so that she is not lying on it. 

The hips should be at the extreme edge of the foot of the table and the heels should be braced in openings of the adjustable supports. The draping is done with the large sheet, as in catheterization, with the sides of the sheet hanging free. The wrapper and gown are folded back from the pubes. In addition to the draping sheet, a dressing towel may be placed over the vulva. The nurse's duties may include handing instruments, holding basin to receive soiled dressings or instruments, and changing the patient's position at the doctor's directions. 

It is sometimes necessary to examine a patient in bed. 

If only a digital examination is to be made, the patient is left in the middle of the bed in the dorsal recumbent

PHYSICAL EXAMINATION 

243 position, with the knees drawn up and separated. All the upper bedclothing is turned down below the knees except the top sheet, which is used for draping; this is done in the same manner as in giving a douche. 

If a speculum is used, the patient is placed on her back across the bed with the hips at the extreme edge and the knees flexed, the feet being placed on wooden extensions thrust under the sides of the mattress, allowing 27 inches spread for the feet. Adjustable metal stirrups which may be clamped to the side of the bed are very useful. The patient is draped as if on the examining table. 

Other positions, such as the Sims, knee-chest, or standing position, are rarely used for examination, but chiefly for treatment. In such cases an experienced nurse should be called in to help the doctor.

XXXVII 

PREPARATION OF PATIENT FOR OPERATION 

General preparation the night before 

Bath, head shampoo, if possible.

A light supper, or diet as prescribed. 

Catharsis as prescribed. 

Local preparation. 

Local Preparation 

The local preparation is an important procedure. An extensive shave is necessary in all cases, having the skin well lathered, and the razor sharp. Hold the razor nearly parallel with the skin, not at right angles to it. Pull back the skin to avoid injuring it and shave obliquely to the shafts of hair.

Preparation of nurse's hands and arm 

A hot soap and water scrub under running water, paying particular attention to the finger nails. Scrub again, and finish with a thorough rinsing from hands to elbows. 

Final steps in preparation

The shaven area is scrubbed five minutes with hot soap solution and a sterile gauze sponge, giving special attention to the region of the umbilicus in abdominal cases. Complete the scrubbing, using 70% alcohol two minutes. 

Finally, the prepared area is protected with a dry sterile towel or pad, held securely in place. 244

PRELIMINARY COURSE 

245 

Areas for shaving in special operations

Head and Face. Shaved by the hospital barber accord ing to directions. (See Part II, page 398.)

Neck. In male patients with thyroidal diseases, shaving is done daily except on the day of operation. In neck operations the patient is shaved from angle of lower jawbone (mandible) from one side to the other, reaching down on the neck on each side as far as the interclavicular notch on front.

Chest. Shave from the neck to the umbilicus and as far as midline in the back. 

Breast. The same as chest, including the axillary space and the upper arm to elbow. 

Abdomen. This area includes gall bladder, gastric and appendiceal operations, hysterectomy, ovariotomy, and salpingitis; for the last three, including the shaving of the vulva and perineum. (See Part II, page 398.)

Kidney. Shave from beneath breast to median line. in front, to midline behind, and over hips.

Vaginal and Perineum. (See Part II, page 398.) Rectal. (See Part II, page 398.) 

Varicose Veins. Hip joint to ankle on all sides. After preparation in some cases, in order to facilitate the location of the vein in operation, the nurse is asked to outline the varicose veins with gentian-violet or methylene-blue. The patient stands while this is done, in order to dilate the veins. 

Preparation the morning of operation 

Soap suds enema, if ordered. 

Ether breakfast,

In stomach cases nothing by mouth. 

If the operation is to be done under local anesthesia, the patient can have a light breakfast of toast or coffee. 

If done in the afternoon, a light breakfast may be taken and no luncheon.
