Mouth cases 

Mouth wash every 2 hours, as a rule an antiseptic alkaline solution.

Orthopedic surgery 

Local preparation is done 24 or 48 hours before operation. 

Shave the area of operation, as above, page 244.

PRELIMINARY COURSE 

247 Preparation of nurse's hands and arms 

Soap and water scrub for three minutes and soak in corrosive sublimate 1-2000 solution two minutes. 

Completion of local preparation Scrub the shaven area with soap and hot water and sterile sponges five minutes, with ether one minute; with alcohol three minutes; with corrosive sublimate 1-2000 two minutes. 

Apply sterile dressing.

In a 48-hour preparation this routine is repeated, and each time the patient is scrubbed the operation should be carried out as an absolutely sterile procedure. 

Vaginal cases. (See Part II, page 398, for shaving.) 

Shave, scrub with soap and water.

A vaginal douche of corrosive sublimate 1-8000 is given to the patient either on the ward or operating room, where the preparation is completed. 

A clean pad and T binder is put on the patient before going to operation.

Rectal cases. 

(See Part II, page 398, for shaving.)

Scrub the anal region with soap and water, apply pad and T binder.

A rectal irrigation of 1 gallon of salt solution is given in the operating room and the preparation for operation completed.

XXXVIII  Patients are discharged: 

To Out-Patient Department 

To local physician

To other institutions 

"Against advice"

By death Dressing the patient 

The house officer has notified the nurse, and probably the patient, of the date of discharge. The nurse takes the clothes slip to the General Locker, where each article is checked, and all are tied in a half sheet and carried back to the ward. If the clothes are in ward lockers, as on Ward G, the skin ward, simply bring everything up to the ward. Carry a woman's hat separately, that it may not become crushed. Be sure to have all extra articles, such as bags, umbrellas, etc.

If the patient were an ambulance admission or did not have suitable clothing, see that some one is notified in time so there will be no delay. The outside clothing must be sufficient for comfort under existing weather conditions.

When no clothing can be obtained, second-hand clothes may be given the patient from the hospital supply. This is arranged by the head nurse. 

The patient is allowed to dress an hour before going 248

home, not sooner, as he might walk out unnoticed by the nurse.

If he has been up walking about for some time, the clothes are taken to the bathroom, all tags are removed, and everything placed conveniently, including a chair, for the patient may wish to rest during the process of dressing. Be ready to assist if necessary, or if a male patient, see that an orderly assists him.

When the patient is too weak or excited, take the clothes to the bedside instead of to the bathroom. 

In case the patient occupies a room he dresses there instead of in the bathroom.

Preparation of card and chart On the back of the name card is printed in black ink, the date of discharge, service, diagnosis, and where discharged. The last diagnosis is written in red ink by house officer on the medication sheet and must be copied exactly on the card by the nurse. 

The discharge is printed on the chart in black ink, under proper date, to whom, also, being written in, as "Discharged to local M. D." in pulse space. This, with medication sheet, history and yellow slip, is left on the head nurse's desk.

When the patient has received his belongings (nurse must make sure of this) he signs his name in the space indicated on the Clothes Slip, or if the clothes have been kept, as for private patients, signs a statement to that effect. The Clothes Slip is left with the yellow admission slip.

Determine from the list what the patient has in the safe and notify the cashier, who checks up the articles.

Discharge of patient from the ward Unless the nurse is sure the patient will be able to walk the distance, he should be taken down in a wheel chair. 

The nurse is the best judge of the distance and the condition of the patient. In cases of doubt, always use the chair. 

Give any slips to the patient which belong to him and take with patient to the cashier, who receives the patient's card, makes out the bill and has the patient sign for the return of any valuables.

When possible, the head nurse has sent a list of discharges to the cashier before eleven o'clock, that the bills may be ready. A slip showing how much is due is given. to the nurse with the name card. The bill is given to the patient. Take patient, with card and slip, to the superintendent, who decides regarding payment of the bill. If satisfied, the superintendent gives the card to the nurse, who then takes the patient to Information window. Leave the card there.

Before leaving him with his friends the nurse should make final observations as to the patient's condition for making the journey home, whether long or short distance. 

Special rules governing discharges 

Every patient must be accompanied by a friend or relative unless permission to go alone is given by the superintendent on duty. If unable to notify the friends by letter, a telephone or telegraph message is sent by the head nurse or senior nurse. No action may be taken or notification given to friends or front door until such discharge has been authorized by the senior house officer. 

The following is an example of a telephone message:

45 Blank St., Stoneham, Mass. Tel. 446 M. To Mrs.

Mary Doe. The patient, Maude Doe, will be ready to be discharged between one and two o'clock Sunday, November 2, 19, from the Massachusetts General Hospital, if some one calls for her. November 1, 19-, Ward 16.

Surgical cases must leave hospital swathes, straps, etc., unless they are purchased. If not purchased, replace them with compress cloth.

Ambulance cases 

These cases need not be dressed completely, but with enough to keep them warm. The remainder of the clothes are tied up in bundles for the friends to carry. 

The friend or relative should sign for clothes and valuables, if the patient is too ill. 

The house officer arranges with the superintendent or social service department about ambulance cases. The ward is notified when to have the patient ready and to be sure everything is ready at the appointed time. 

In such cases the patient is taken directly to the ambulance. The nurse then goes through the same routine of discharging a patient, stating to each office that it is an ambulance case. Friends or relatives may go with the nurse to receive property in safe, pay bill, etc.

Daily discharges 

If the patient is to be discharged before 8 A.M., permission must be obtained and the patient's card put through the day before. It should be taken to the superintendent and cashier, not to the front door.

If the patient is to leave after 5 P.M., the card must be put through during the day.

Do not write in the date of discharge until the patient actually goes.

On the day of discharge, or at any time when the card is put through, go first to the cashier, then to the superintendent and finally to Information window. 

Discharges on Sundays and holidays 

Discharges on Sundays and holidays must be between 

12.30-1 o'clock, or 2-3 P.M. If for some special reason, as "Discharged against advice," permission may be obtained from the superintendent to discharge the patient before or after that time.

The card is always sent to the cashier and superintendent the day before, so that the cashier may make out the bill and leave the property in the superintendent's safe. Do not show the card at Information window until the patient leaves, the next day, as that means that the patient's name is taken off the list as discharged, which makes the count of patients wrong; it is confusing, also, if friends telephone about the patient.

DISCHARGED TO OUT-PATIENT DEPARTMENT When the patient is discharged to Out-Patient De partment, the doctor makes out a slip, the lower part of which is given to patient for admission. The upper part is left at Information window with name card. 

Other slips, such as those regarding diet, prescriptions, etc., are made out at the same time and given to the patient with detached portion of Out-Patient Department slips.

Prescriptions may be filled at O. P.D. Apothecary before 2 P.M. before going, if patient is able to pay for them.

DISCHARGED TO LOCAL M. D. 

The patient is sent home, further care being given by the local physician. These are usually cases who have never been admitted to Out-Patient Department. 

DISCHARGED TO OTHER INSTITUTIONS 

The supervisor is notified that a patient is going from this hospital to another. She examines the head, nails, and general appearance before the patient is discharged. The nurse in charge of the patient is responsible for her condition.

DISCHARGED BY DEATH 

A disease may terminate in recovery, in retarded or incomplete recovery, or in death. Death may occur suddenly and unexpectedly, but is usually preceded by certain indications of failing vitality. Among the earliest signs may be difficulty in swallowing; a gradual weakening of the radial pulse, with increasing rate and irregularity; and deep and labored respirations with tracheal râles, due to the presence of mucus. The temperature may suddenly rise a few hours before death, then slowly drop as the pulse rate increases. The condition of the skin is the same as in shock, cold and moist, the face drawn and pinched, and somewhat dusky. The patient shows marked mental apathy, indifference to what is going on, and is aroused with difficulty. On the other hand the patient

may be mentally alert, and apprehensive to the end. Such cases are inclined to get out of bed and should not be left alone on any account.

When the patient's illness assumes a serious aspect it is usual in a hospital to place his name on the "danger list." Under these circumstances the relatives or friends are notified, and are permitted to visit the patient in limited numbers at any time during the day or night as long as they are quiet and self-controlled in behavior, and do not disturb the patient. 

The nurse is cautioned against attaching her signature as a witness to any legal paper at this time. If she should be asked to do this, she should refer the matter to the head nurse.

The attitude of the nurse should be that of quiet, sympathetic thoughtfulness and kindness to the family and friends. If the patient does not have a special nurse, this case should be one of her chief responsibilities. She should make an effort to be at the bedside of the patient as much as possible, and do what she can in the way of small attentions outside of her routine duties in caring for him. She may moisten his lips and tongue, bathe his face or gently fan, if it does not appear to annoy him. 

The knowledge that some one is at the bedside may, in itself, afford some comfort if the patient's mind be not too clouded.

As the end approaches the doctor, who is already aware of the condition of the patient, is notified. The nurse remains at the bedside with her fingers over the radial artery and retains that position two or three minutes. after the patient has stopped breathing, to assure herself

that death has taken place. She then gently closes the eyes, and at once reports the death to the one in authority on the ward, who notifies the doctor. The doctor, following auscultation, announces the death of the patient, and the hour is noted as being the official time of death. In If relatives or friends are absent they are immediately notified by the hospital authorities. As soon as the doctor leaves the bedside, the nurse removes any apparatus used in the care of the patient, and leaves the body in the dorsal position, with one or two pillows under the head, and straightens the extremities. If friends are present they may desire to be left alone with the deceased for a few minutes. The nurse in the meantime prepares the necessary materials for bathing and dressing the body. the case of a man the orderly cares for the body, but before it is taken from the ward the nurse should be sure that the work has been done properly. If necessary, the friends are courteously asked to withdraw before any evidences of the final preparations are in sight. 

They are advised and directed to the hospital office, where final business affairs are transacted. The nurse then carries her materials to the bedside. An assistant may be assigned if necessary. 

Equipment for care of the body after death The usual bathing materials, omitting bath blankets, and using washed gauze instead of a wash cloth. 2 laundry bags 2 large sheets I gray blanket 2 special tags, if a precaution case special gown for the nurse

shroud Mortuary package containing large compress square large picked cotton pad pair of white stockings 

6-inch four-tailed bandage for jaw 2-inch roll of cotton bandage metal comb orange wood stick toothpicks small piece of absorbent cotton 2 cotton roller bandages safety pins, large and small 

2 paper identification tags Additional equipment is as follows: 

If a surgical case, 2 packages of surgical gauze and adhesive plaster pair of scissors

If death is due to streptococcus or any other infection, or to communicable disease, rubber gloves 

If a death following burns, sheet wadding or cotton rollers compress cloth or cotton bandages washed gauze dressings according to location of burnt area, whether of the body or extremities

The attitude of those who care for the body While engaged in caring for the body, out of respect for the dead perfect quiet should be the rule. If any conversation, it should be carried on in a low tone of voice, and should be concerned only with the duties being performed. What must be done should be carried out. thoroughly, decently, and expeditiously, without seeming haste. An hour is usually sufficient for this procedure. 

If near the visiting hour, transportation of the body will not be permitted until it is over. 

Procedure 

If on an open ward, screen the bed thoroughly. Have everything needed at the bedside. Loosen upper bedclothing. Place cotton articles in one laundry bag, and blankets in the other. The sheet over the body is removed later. Remove jewelry, if any, and place in envelope marked "Valuables." If unable to remove wedding ring, bind on with a narrow bandage and note on Valuables list. 

Care of the head

Wash the face, attending to nostrils, and cleanse the teeth; if false, replace and, if necessary, use absorbent cotton to hold upper plate in position. Comb hair, part and braid neatly. 

Apply four-tailed bandage over two cotton rollers, which are held in place under each angle of the jaw by an assistant. These permit tight application of the bandage without marring the skin.

Care of the body 

Remove the nightgown. Bathe the body with as little exposure and disturbance as possible. It is slightly turned by the assistant while the back is being bathed. 

If the undersheet has become soiled, it is at this time folded upon itself and pushed out of the way. 

The nails are now attended to. Then follows the application of the hip binder, made from the large piece of compress cloth folded in a triangle, in the center of which is placed the picked cotton pad. Both are slipped under the hips together; the pad arranged between the thighs and buttocks. The ends of the triangle are secured with large safety pins in front, the apex of the triangle being carried up between the thighs. Put on stockings and secure to the hip binder at each side; if the stockings do not reach, attach a piece of cotton bandage to the stocking, neatly, to serve as an extension. Bind the limbs together over the stockings above the knees, and at the ankles, with the 2-inch bandage, being careful not to let it cut in at its edges. 

Final care 

Put on the shroud, which is a long plain gown, gathered at the neck and wrists with a running tape. Remove the upper sheet and place in the proper laundry bag. To the wrist attach one of the identification tags on which has been plainly written in ink the patient's full name, ward, service, month, date, year, official time of death to the minute, and the statement "friends present" or "no friends present" at the time of death. Bring the arms close to the side of the body, cross one hand over the other, and hold in position with a couple of turns of cotton bandage carefully applied.

The body is slowly turned, and the edges of the shroud are overlapped at the back. At the same time one of the sheets is slipped under the body and so arranged that there shall be a little more sheet at the head than at the feet to allow for folding over, after the body has been enveloped, so that in transfer from bed to stretcher there shall be no disturbance of the covering. The orderly is notified to bring the stretcher, upon which the second sheet is spread and the body placed upon it and again enveloped as in the first sheet. Over all is placed the gray blanket in order not to attract attention, when transferring the body to the mortuary. At this time the orderly is given the second identification tag, filled out in duplicate of the first, which is placed on a stub on the desk of the official who delivers the body to the undertaker. If a woman or child, the nurse accompanies the body as far as the mortuary.

Special procedures 

Following the death of a surgical patient, leave drains and tubes in, put on a clean dressing without disturbing these drains, fastening dressings with adhesive. Should there be a tube or drain (such as a tracheotomy tube), which is a matter of some value, a record should be made on the ward and in the Pathological Department. 

In case of death from burns, any remaining blisters are opened, drained, and dried. They are then covered with washed gauze, over which may be placed a layer of sheet wadding and a compress cloth binder. In burns of the extremities, also, gauze is used, with cotton rollers and cotton bandage. In burns of the face little can be done except to clean with oil or vaseline, then with alcohol.

The final official steps The usual routine is that of daily discharge, see page 251. 

The name card, on which has been written the official time of death and the diagnosis, is taken to the information office within ten minutes after death.

On the chart is written in the space of the date "Discharged dead to Allen Street House," the mortuary; this is taken later to the Treadwell medical library to be filed, with history and treatment sheet. "Allen Street House" is written on the Clothes Slip also. 

The Clothes Slip is taken to the clothes room and given to the officer in charge, where the clothing is claimed by relatives or undertaker and signed for. As the hospital is responsible for all property belonging to the deceased, until claimed and properly signed for, nothing is to be taken from the room by relatives or friends. Valuables are placed in a manilla clasp envelope, with the contents listed on the outside, name and ward of the deceased, and a duplicate list is made in the book provided for this purpose. The envelope and book are taken to the cashier, who retains the envelope with the valuables, and signs under the duplicate list in the Valuables book, which is returned to the ward. The valuables are later delivered to some responsible member of the family who is looking after the property, and who signs for them. 

Care of bedside or room The bedstead, rubber sheet, and stand drawers are washed with soap and water. The mattress and pillows are tagged for airing and replaced by others. The bed

is made up with fresh mattresses, linen and blankets, the room aired, and set in order. 

Following a communicable disease, besides the care above, the walls are washed by the house cleaner, the mattresses tagged and sterilized, and all utensils of metal, china, or glass used in the care of the patient are boiled. 

Rubber goods are washed with hot suds and aired. 

Blankets and linen are placed in clean precaution bags, and tagged with the name of disease.

PART II

XXXIX 

HOT AIR BATHS 

Hot air or sweat baths are used to increase the functional activities of the skin and kidneys in such conditions as are associated with acute or chronic nephritis, namely, edema, anuria, or complete suppression, and in the early symptoms of uremia. They may be prescribed, also, in rheumatism to assist the elimination of certain acid bodies and toxins in the blood, to soften the fibrous tissues and lessen the stiffness of affected joints, and to relieve pain.

There are many methods employed and various appliances used for this treatment, such as the electric light or bunsen burner in place of the alcohol lamp, and for those who can sit up, the electric light cabinet. 

As a rule the treatment is best given in bed to patients who may suffer a setback from exposure or change of atmosphere, such as nephritic and rheumatic patients. 

Points to be remembered during the bath

Patient should be given an opportunity to empty bladder before starting. Oversight of this may be the cause of great discomfort because of the effect of the treatment on the kidneys. The amount of urine voided immediately after the bath, if any, should be observed.

In dropsical conditions the patient is sometimes weighed before and after the treatment to learn the extent of elimination. 

If not on limited fluids the patient is given cool water or orangeade to drink at intervals of ten or fifteen 265

minutes throughout the hot air treatment. In nephritis, if the patient is edematous and on restricted fluids, the amount taken during confinement in the hot air bath goes into the sum total of 24 hours. The amount of fluids allowed in such cases is 60 ounces in 24 hours. 

Note temporal pulse every ten minutes.

Watch thermometer closely. In some cases temperature of bath may be permitted to go as high as 140° F. before discontinuing the treatment. 

Bathe patient's face with cool water for comfort during bath. 

Have a second lamp ready to replace first if necessary, so that an even temperature may be sustained. 

Take all precautions to avoid fire, using a damp cloth around funnel, and keep an extra blanket on hand. 

If for any reason the nurse should be called away, a written note regarding present temperature and expiration of time of bath should be pinned to the cradle, and the head nurse should be notified. In any case the patient should be under close observation. 

Equipment 

5 gray blankets 

2 large rubber sheets patient's towel rubber pillow case gauze sponge gauze head binder ice cap hot water bag dairy thermometer enameled basin. bathing solution long wooden cradle asbestos covered funnel and oven attachment to hold lamp 

2 metal alcohol lamps, or can of solid alcohol matches pitcher of cool water or orangeade and drinking tube

HOT AIR BATHS 

267 

Procedure 

Preparation of bed and patient Loosen upper bedclothing and remove spread. Turn back bedclothing at foot of bed, even with mattress, and at waist line from the head.

Place gray blanket over patient. Turn down bedclothing as usual to foot of bed. Fold neatly and remove. 

Slip rubber case over pillow. Place a towel under patient's head. Turn patient and move to opposite side of bed. Unbutton nightgown.

A second blanket is used as a protective covering against accidental burning of the patient, and is also arranged for comfort, so that no two opposing surfaces of the skin are in contact, in the following manner: one of the blankets is doubled in two crosswise, and while the patient is still on his side it is placed on the bed with the fold towards his back and as high on the pillow as his head. The upper half is gathered and pushed under his back and thighs, the remainder tucked against his back. The patient is now rolled over facing the nurse, and the blanket is pulled through on the opposite side.

The patient is turned on his back and nightgown is removed. His arms are raised above his head and the first side of the blanket at the top is placed across his chest as high as the axillary spaces and tucked in on the opposite side. The remainder of the blanket on the same side is tucked between the patient's thighs and knees and as far as it reaches. The patient lowers his arms to his sides and the second side of the blanket

is brought down in a straight fold over the shoulder, then turned sharply upon itself and carried over the opposite shoulder securely. Remainder of blanket is tucked under body.

A gauze head binder is wrung out in cold water and brought around from back of neck, and around head in turban fashion. Over it is placed the ice cap without a cover, the head binder taking its place. 

A covered hot water bag at 125° F. is placed at the feet. The thermometer, which is attached to the rung of the bed above the pillow by a string, is placed in the region of the pelvis. 

The cradle is placed in position and funnel and oven are attached to foot of bed, slightly at one side, to avoid the feet. A blanket is placed over cradle crosswise, over patient's shoulders, carried under lower pillows, and securely tucked under mattress.

Another blanket covers remaining portion of cradle, securely tucked in at foot of bed, snugly enveloping the funnel to prevent escape of hot air. 

Administration and observation 

Light the lamp and place in the oven. Note the time.

Two rubber sheets are added and arranged in the same manner as the blankets. Two more blankets are placed over rubber sheets. Finally, over all, the spread is put on and tucked under patient's chin to prevent contact with rough blanket. The end of spread is folded under itself even with foot of cradle to avoid coming in contact with hot oven and funnel. The temporal pulse is noted and the patient is given a drink.

HOT AIR BATHS 

269 

At the end of twenty or twenty-five minutes note temperature of bath by carefully drawing the thermometer by the string to the edge of the blanket and reading the scale without exposing the bulb or permitting escape of hot air. Frequent observation of the patient's temporal pulse should be made throughout the bath.

If no special order has been given for the duration of the bath, and the temperature has reached 120° F., the bath may be continued fifteen minutes longer, unless it is contraindicated by the patient's condition, i.e., weak and rapid pulse, restlessness, and a sense of oppression, flushed face and dry skin. If these signs are observed, the light should be extinguished at once and the patient's condition reported. The alcohol sponge and rub, part of the usual procedure, would then follow immediately. 

Removal of blankets, cradle, and funnel

The light is put out and bedclothing is loosened to permit escape of hot air and slow evaporation of moisture from the patient's skin. 

In about fifteen minutes all rubber sheets are removed and all blankets over the patient, save one, which is adjusted lengthwise to cover him fully, and tucked behind his shoulders to hold it in place. The funnel and cradle are then removed. The blanket in which the patient is enveloped is loosened on each side and serves as an under blanket during the sponge bath. 

The ice cap and head binder are left on until the end of the bath.

The sponge bath 

While the patient is drying, preparations are made to give him a ten-minute sponge to restore the tone to the relaxed muscles, to cleanse the skin from the products of the sweat and sebaceous glands, and to refresh. A basin with one part of bathing solution to twenty parts water is used at 100° F. 

The sponge is given in the same order as the alcohol bath in febrile conditions, except that towel friction is used altogether and exposure of the body is avoided in order not to chill the patient.

After the bath is finished the hot water bag may be refilled at the usual temperature and placed at the patient's feet. In some cases the blanket is allowed to remain over his body so that the activity of the sweat glands may be continued. The pulse is noted and the patient is allowed to rest.

XL PACKS 

A pack is the application of heat or cold to the body, in dry or moist form, through the medium of blankets or sheets.

HOT DRY PACK 

When stimulation of the skin and kidneys is desired, the hot dry pack is sometimes used in place of the hot air bath, as a less extreme measure. It may also serve as a substitute when equipment for the hot air bath is not available in private practice.

Equipment 

4 blankets large rubber sheet head binder gauze sponge patient's towel 

4 hot water bags, or substitutes. See page 110 Procedure ice cap basin with ice water bathing solution pitcher of cool water and drinking tube

It is less disturbing to the patient to have the first arrangement of the blankets and rubber sheet prepared before bringing to the bedside. The pack is so arranged that the three blankets and rubber sheet are 271

introduced together under the patient, with the canvas Iside of the rubber sheet next to the bed. 

Double the rubber sheet in two, rubber side out, and with open edges towards the nurse. 

Double the three blankets crosswise in order to have each even with the others and the rubber sheet. Place the folded crease of the first blanket even with the folded crease of the rubber sheet underneath. 

Throw back the upper half of the blanket. Arrange the other two blankets on top of the first in the same way, one over the other, so that a single layer, or half of each blanket, is lying upon the doubled rubber sheet. Grasp the edges of the blankets and the upper edge of the rubber sheet with both hands and fold over to the center crease and again upon itself. Next, grasp the folded thicknesses, raise and swing the remaining half of the rubber sheet to the opposite side. 

The remaining half of the three blankets and rubber sheet are drawn forward in three or four fan-like folds meeting the folded thicknesses. These are brought together and compactly folded end to end in thirds and. carried to the patient's bedside. 

The upper bedclothing is turned down to the foot of the bed with the fourth blanket. The rubber pillow case is drawn over the cotton one. The patient is rolled on his side and the fan-folded part of the blankets and rubber sheet are slipped under him together. All are placed high enough on the pillow to cover his shoulders later. The nightgown is removed and each blanket in turn, and finally the rubber sheet is wrapped around the patient in the same manner as

PACKS 

273 the enveloping blanket in the hot air bath, except that they are turned under the feet. If not long enough an extra one may be used to cover the feet. 

A hot water bag, half full and free of air, temperature 140° F., is placed on each side of the patient, a third is placed under his knees, and a fourth at his feet, all outside of the blankets. Finally the large rubber sheet is wrapped about all to keep in the heat. 

The fourth blanket, which was used to turn down the bedclothing, may be folded across and laid over the rubber sheet. The upper bedclothing is drawn up and the sheet tucked under the patient's chin to prevent contact with blanket.

The head binder is wrung out of ice water and applied, and over that, the ice cap. The patient's temporal pulse is noted and a drink of cool water given every ten or fifteen minutes, if on forced fluids. The patient is usually left in the pack from thirty minutes to one hour. 

Sponge bath

This treatment is followed with an alcohol sponge and rub, with the temperature between 85°-95°. While giving the sponge, avoid exposure of patient and consequent chilling by too sudden change of temperature.

HOT WET PACK 

A hot wet pack, because of its moist, penetrating heat, is usually more effective than the hot air bath or hot dry pack when quick stimulation of the elimina-

tive organs is desired, in such cases as uremia and general edema, in acute nephritis, in renal irritations following corrosive poisoning, and occasionally in the toxemia of pregnancy to ward off convulsions.
